#10759. Working With a Marketing Agency: A Technical Guide for Developers and Designers

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later, most teams that build websites end up working alongside a digital marketing agency. The agency asks for CMS access, wants tracking scripts added, suggests URL changes, and sets targets for page speed. If nobody on the product side understands what is being asked and why, the result is broken analytics, accidental noindex tags, and migrations that quietly erase years of search visibility.

This guide is for developers, designers, and technical founders. It covers the engineering side of that collaboration: environments and access, analytics implementation, redirects, structured data, performance budgets, and crawler access, with code you can adapt and a checklist you can hand to whoever you are working with.

Marketing briefs rarely say it, but most outcomes are decided in the codebase and the design system:

That is why healthy collaborations look like a shared engineering effort with a reviewable change process, not a monthly PDF report.

Almost every painful marketing-and-engineering story falls into one of the patterns below. Each has a cause you can see in the code and a fix that takes minutes, if you catch it before launch.

Give the agency a staging environment and least-privilege access rather than production admin rights. Keep staging out of search results so test pages are never indexed. At the web-server level that can be a single header:

The risk is the other direction: that header reaching production. Add a post-deploy check to your pipeline so a launch fails loudly instead of silently:

Reports are only as good as the data behind them. Agree on a naming convention for UTM parameters before the first campaign, and document it:

For conversions, prefer explicit events pushed to a data layer over fragile click-based triggers. Developers implement them once, and every tool downstream can reuse them:

Test each event in your tag manager's preview mode on staging, and treat event names and parameters as part of your API: changing them is a breaking change that should be announced.

Changing URLs without redirects discards accumulated search visibility. Before launch, export every indexable URL, map each to its new location, and implement permanent redirects:

Do not rely on spot checks. This script reads a two-column CSV of old and new paths and verifies that each old URL returns a single 301 to a destination that returns 200:

Run it against staging before launch and against production right after. Redirect chains (A to B to C) and redirects that land on a 404 are the two failures it catches most often.

Structured data helps search engines and AI systems understand who you are. Generate it from the same data that renders the page, so it never drifts from the visible content, and escape it properly when you embed it:

Validate the output with Google's Rich Results Test or the Schema Markup Validator as part of review, not after launch. If you would rather not hand-write the JSON-LD for every page type, free Schema Markup Generator can produce a starting point, which you should still validate with the tools above.

Ask for Core Web Vitals targets, and make sure marketing scripts do not break them. Google's published "good" thresholds, measured at the 75th percentile of real-user visits, are:

A budget nobody checks is a wish. Lighthouse CI can fail a build when a page exceeds one, which turns "please keep it fast" into a rule that applies equally to your code and to any tag the agency adds:

Sizes are in kilobytes and timings in milliseconds. Route every new tracking or advertising tag through code review and measure its cost before it ships.

AI-generated answers favor content that is clearly structured, factual, and easy to attribute. In practice that means descriptive headings, concise answers near the top of a page, accurate structured data, server-rendered content, and a deliberate decision about which crawlers may access your site. That decision lives in robots.txt, and it is an engineering and business call, not a default:

Check the documentation of each crawler you care about for its current user-agent name, since these change. If a vendor promises AI search visibility, ask exactly what they will change on the site and how they will measure the result. The field is young and claims are easy to make.

The following scenario is illustrative, not a real client story, but it combines problems that appear again and again. A team redesigns its site and changes URLs from /services/seo-audit to /solutions/audit. The redirect map covers the top 50 pages, so the launch looks fine. Three weeks later, organic traffic is down by a third. The cause is a long tail of 800 blog and resource URLs with no redirects, plus a CDN rule that rewrites trailing slashes and turns every single redirect into a chain of two hops.

Both problems would have been caught by the checks above: exporting every indexable URL from the sitemap and server logs rather than only the top pages, and running the redirect verification script against staging. The lesson is that migration work is mostly verification, and verification can be automated.

The best collaboration is the one that fits your stack and your team's way of working. Put the agreements above in writing, automate the checks that can be automated, and treat marketing changes like any other change to the product: reviewed, tested in staging, and measured after release. When engineering and marketing share the same process, the results tend to follow.

#10756. University mascot dog trains puppy to replace him before retirement

A dog who has served as a university’s live mascot for nearly five years is training his replacement before he retires.

Baloo is a Saint Bernard who became the live mascot of Siena University in New York back in 2021. He has become a familiar face at basketball games and campus events, posing for hundreds of photographs with students and fans.

Now, with Baloo approaching his seventh birthday, the university has introduced three-month-old Baby Baloo, another Saint Bernard that is being trained to take over the role next year.

Baby Baloo is set to accompany his predecessor to home games throughout the season, learning appropriate behavior from the older dog.

“The way dogs work is they mimic a lot of behaviors and, so, repetition is a big thing for them,” their handler, Charles Rice told TimesUnion. “Basically, Baby Baloo is literally just following suit with whatever he sees Baloo doing.”

During an exhibition match against Syracuse at the MVP Arena, Baby Baloo wore a vest that says “In Training, Do Not Pet.” Rice said he will probably wear the vest until January.

A post shared by Siena University (@sienauniversity)

“Really, what it is, is to help him learn to self-regulate,” Rice continued. “There’s just a lot of chaos that happens during the game. There’s a lot of sights and sounds and things like that. He’ll stay focused on me, learn to relax.”

Baby Baloo currently weighs around 40 lbs, but is expected to grow to around 190 lbs, potentially making him even bigger than the 160-pound Baloo.

Baloo is expected to retire at the end of this academic year, leaving Baby Baloo to become the university’s sole mascot in 2027.

#10751. Anthropic adds new Claude rule banning recreations of existing games

Anthropic has changed its Claude Code tool so that it won’t recreate specific existing video games, following growing concern from developers about their games being cloned.

The change was spotted around October 27 by X user Chasm before Anthropic said anything publicly. The new rule tells Claude: “Do not build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is renamed or called original.”

Anthropic has since confirmed the change, with Addy Osmani, a member of technical staff, explaining in a post on X that the rule was added because using Claude to infringe on IP breaks the company’s usage policy.

We added two sentences to Claude Code's prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else's specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy. Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to…

“We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy,” he wrote.

“Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working.”

The change comes as more developers worry about their work being copied before it even launches. Alliance Arts recently cancelled the public demo for its upcoming Steam game Drunken Goddess Reflux, warning that its content “could be used to quickly imitate or replicate the game’s presentation and gameplay with AI.”

Other developers have been pressing Valve on how it plans to stop clones from flooding Steam.

How well the new rule works remains to be seen, as Claude has no way of checking whether a user actually owns the game they’re working on, and other models that can be run locally don’t have the same restrictions.

Osmani said Anthropic will “continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance,” and asked anyone who has their own project turned down by mistake to contact him.

#10750. Check if eMudhra CA Is Still Trusted in Your Runtime

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Eight eMudhra intermediate Certificate Authority private keys are trivially recoverable from their public certificates. Any attacker can mint trusted TLS certificates for arbitrary domains. Browsers have already blocked these compromised intermediates through revocation mechanisms, but most non-browser runtimes — curl, Python, Node.js, Go, and Rust — still silently trust them. This affects any team operating server-side infrastructure, CI pipelines, or CLI tooling that makes outbound TLS connections. If your stack trusts the eMudhra chain, an attacker can forge certificates your code will accept without complaint.

This guide provides a diagnostic sequence: test every runtime in the stack against the compromised certificates, remediate by updating trust stores, and verify the fix sticks. The primary source for this disclosure is Scott Helme's detailed writeup.

In RSA key generation, you derive the private exponent d via the modular inverse of the public exponent e modulo λ(n) (the Carmichael totient of the modulus), where λ(n) depends on the prime factors of n. RSA's security depends entirely on d being large — typically hundreds of digits — making it computationally infeasible to derive from the public key alone.

eMudhra generated eight intermediate CA certificates with single-byte d values. Specific recovered values include d=4, d=56, and d=115, as documented in Scott Helme's disclosure post. These are not large numbers. They are recoverable from the public certificate in milliseconds. Anyone with access to the public certificate (which is, by definition, public) can reconstruct the private key and sign certificates that chain up to eMudhra's trusted root. That means arbitrary TLS certificates for any domain, indistinguishable from legitimate ones to any client that trusts the eMudhra chain.

An attacker holding one of these recovered keys can issue a certificate for any hostname, and any TLS client trusting the eMudhra intermediate will accept it without complaint.

The practical consequence is total impersonation capability. An attacker holding one of these recovered keys can issue a certificate for any hostname, and any TLS client trusting the eMudhra intermediate will accept it without complaint.

Major browsers have deployed mitigations through their respective revocation infrastructure. The compromise was reported to Apple, Google, Microsoft, Mozilla, and eMudhra on Saturday, October 17, 2026. Chrome pushed a CRLSet update blocking the intermediates the same evening, Mozilla blocked them in Firefox via OneCRL within hours, Apple applied its platform block on October 19, and Microsoft rolled out a disallowed-certificate CTL update. Current browser versions already reject the compromised intermediates.

The problem lies everywhere else. Non-browser TLS clients skip revocation checks by default. curl does not check CRLs or OCSP unless explicitly configured to do so. Python's requests and urllib3 libraries rely on the certifi bundle and perform no revocation checking. Python's standard library urllib.request module uses the system SSL store, not certifi. Node.js's https module uses either a compiled-in root store or the system store depending on build configuration, with no active revocation. Go's crypto/tls loads system roots but does not perform revocation checks. Rust's rustls paired with webpki-roots uses a bundled Mozilla root set, while native-tls defers to the system store; neither checks revocation by default.

This creates a trust store gap. The system CA bundle on a Linux server, the certifi bundle shipped with Python, and the compiled-in roots in Node.js might all still contain the eMudhra intermediates or trust the root that chains to them. Without manual intervention, these runtimes remain vulnerable.

The incident's official test infrastructure, documented on the status page at emudhra.we-have-a-problem.com, provides twelve TLS endpoints: one for each of the six compromised TLS intermediates, served twice - once chaining to eMudhra’s emSign ECC root and once chaining to Sectigo’s cross-signing AAA Certificate Services root. Host names follow the pattern dv-g3b-emudhra.we-have-a-problem.com (eMudhra chain) and dv-g3b-aaa-emudhra.we-have-a-problem.com (Sectigo cross-signed chain), with dv/ov/ev and g3b/g3c variants covering all six TLS intermediates. The diagnostic logic is straightforward: if a runtime connects successfully, it still trusts the compromised chain and is vulnerable. If the TLS handshake fails with a certificate verification error, the runtime correctly distrusts the intermediate. The bare domain emudhra.we-have-a-problem.com serves only the status page itself over an ordinary Let’s Encrypt certificate, so connecting to it proves nothing - always test the per-intermediate subdomains. Test at least one -emudhra host and its -aaa-emudhra counterpart: a store can distrust one root path while still trusting the other.

Important: A network or DNS error will also produce a connection failure. Before concluding SAFE, confirm the endpoint is reachable by running curl -v --insecure https://dv-g3b-emudhra.we-have-a-problem.com/ and verify that any TLS error is certificate-related (e.g., "certificate verify failed"), not a network connectivity error.

Run curl with verbose output against the test endpoint:

If the connection succeeds and returns an HTTP response, the system's CA bundle still trusts the compromised intermediate. Look for SSL certificate verify ok in the verbose output. If instead curl reports an error such as SSL certificate problem: unable to get local issuer certificate or certificate verify failed, the intermediate is distrusted and the runtime is safe.

For a more granular view, use OpenSSL's s_client directly:

A Verify return code: 0 (ok) means the chain is trusted and the system is vulnerable. Any non-zero return code, such as Verify return code: 20 (unable to get local issuer certificate), indicates the intermediate is not trusted.

Each runtime below connects to the same test endpoint. A successful HTTP response means the runtime trusts the compromised chain and is vulnerable. A certificate verification error means the runtime correctly rejects it.

This tests the system SSL store only; urllib.request uses the system SSL context, not the certifi bundle. An ssl.SSLCertVerificationError confirms the intermediate is distrusted. Check the error message to confirm it references a certificate verification failure rather than a configuration or network problem.

A successful response here means the certifi bundle specifically trusts the compromised chain, independent of the system store.

If Node.js prints a status code, it trusts the compromised intermediate. A certificate-related error in the output means it does not. Whether Node.js uses its compiled-in roots or the system store depends on build configuration, so this test reflects whichever store your build uses.

Initialize a module first, then save the following as check.go:

Run with go run check.go. Go loads system roots via crypto/x509, so the result reflects whether the OS trust store has been updated.

Run with cargo run. With rustls + webpki-roots, the result depends on whether the bundled root set has been updated. With native-tls, the result reflects the system store.

Offline verification needs PEM chains for the eight compromised intermediates. The research disclosure site linked in Scott Helme's post at https://scotthelme.co.uk/eight-bits-of-trust-emudhras-ca-keys-cracked-in-milliseconds/ is the canonical starting point for the incident's test material. Use only chains you have verified come from that source, save them as individual .pem files in a local emudhra-chains/ directory, and check their integrity against any checksum the researchers publish. Do not use copies from unverified mirrors.

The directory should contain one PEM file per compromised intermediate chain before you continue.

Use a shell loop to test each chain individually against the system CA bundle. The glob form (openssl verify ... *.pem) is unreliable across OpenSSL versions — multi-file positional arguments require OpenSSL ≥ 3.0 and can silently skip files on older versions. Use the loop instead:

On Debian/Ubuntu systems, the bundle is typically at /etc/ssl/certs/ca-certificates.crt. On RHEL/Fedora/CentOS, look for /etc/pki/tls/certs/ca-bundle.crt. On macOS, the system bundle is at /etc/ssl/cert.pem, though Homebrew-installed OpenSSL uses its own path.

If any chain returns OK, the system store still trusts that compromised intermediate and is vulnerable. A verification failure means it is distrusted.

Python's certifi bundle location can be found with:

Node.js behavior varies: some builds use a compiled-in root store derived from Mozilla's NSS roots, while others defer to the system store. The --use-openssl-ca flag forces Node.js to use the system's OpenSSL CA store instead of its compiled-in bundle. This flag only works on Node.js builds compiled with --openssl-use-def-ca-store. Verify support with node --help | grep openssl-ca; if no output appears, the flag has no effect on that build.

Go's crypto/x509 package loads system roots at runtime, so Go programs inherit the system trust store. There is no separate bundle to verify.

Run a shell loop to verify all eight chains against the certifi bundle:

Any OK result means the certifi bundle is still vulnerable.

The following commands apply to specific distributions. Run openssl version first to confirm your baseline.

If the distribution has not yet issued a patch removing the eMudhra intermediates, you must manually distrust the certificates. The correct mechanism is distribution-specific:

After any manual changes, regenerate the consolidated bundle by running the appropriate update command for your distribution and re-verify with the shell loop above.

Important: Homebrew's CA bundle only affects Homebrew-linked binaries (e.g., Homebrew-installed curl, python3). System tools (/usr/bin/curl, Safari, system Python) use the macOS Keychain and require a macOS security update via System Settings → Software Update. You can verify which curl you are using with which curl.

macOS caveat: Apple’s October 19 platform block covers Safari and Keychain-based clients, but the built-in curl, git, python3, perl, and ruby verify against /etc/ssl/cert.pem without checking revocation, and that file has not been updated at time of writing. The incident status page publishes an official workaround that strips the two affected roots from /etc/ssl/cert.pem, plus a PIP_CERT fix for the built-in pip. Homebrew’s ca-certificates 2026-09-25_1 is patched.

For the system Keychain, open Keychain Access and search for eMudhra intermediates. Apple's platform-level block propagates through macOS security updates, though exact timelines for older macOS versions differ. Older macOS versions that no longer receive security updates will never receive the distrust entry.

Verify the new version by checking pip show certifi and comparing against upstream release notes. Applications that pin an older certifi version in their dependency files remain vulnerable until that pin is updated.

For Node.js, upgrading to a patched release that includes updated compiled-in roots is the primary path. Alternatively, launching Node.js with --use-openssl-ca defers trust decisions to the system store, which you might have already patched. This flag only works on Node.js builds compiled with --openssl-use-def-ca-store; verify support with node --help | grep openssl-ca. Pre-built binaries from nodejs.org might not honor this flag. Different LTS lines will receive the updated root store at different times.

Go relies entirely on the system store, so confirming the system CA update has been applied is sufficient.

For Rust projects using rustls with webpki-roots, run cargo update to pull the latest version of the webpki-roots crate, which derives its root set from Mozilla's NSS. Projects using native-tls defer to the system store.

Rebuild container images with updated CA certificates. Pinned base images are a particular risk — they can lag behind security patches indefinitely.

CI pipelines using cached or pinned base images should pull fresh images or explicitly upgrade ca-certificates as a build step.

Electron's trust store source depends on version and platform. Consult the Electron documentation on certificate verification to determine whether your version uses Node.js's compiled-in root store or the OS certificate store — the boundary has shifted across major releases, and no single version threshold applies cleanly. Upgrading the Electron version or overriding certificate verification via the session.setCertificateVerifyProc() method are the available remediation paths. Verify the specific behavior against the Electron version in use.

Distrust at the intermediate level stops the current attack, but it is fragile: new intermediates cross-signed under the same root would be trusted by default. Mozilla has therefore gone further and removed the eMudhra root entirely in NSS 3.131.1, and Chrome has confirmed it is removing emSign ECC Root CA - G3, the root that signed the compromised intermediates, from the Chrome Root Store, with the change reaching devices from October 27. Note also that the compromised intermediates were cross-signed by Sectigo’s AAA Certificate Services root, so a chain can build through either root - blocking only the eMudhra root path does not close the Sectigo one, which is why the incident’s official Linux workaround distrusts both roots.

The following shell script runs openssl verify against all eight chains and exits non-zero if any chain is still trusted. Drop this into a CI step or run it as a cron job.

Monitor the following sources for updates on CA trust store changes:

Browsers auto-protect through active revocation infrastructure. Everything else — server-side HTTP clients, CLI tools, containerized services — requires manual verification and remediation.

Browsers auto-protect through active revocation infrastructure. Everything else — server-side HTTP clients, CLI tools, containerized services — requires manual verification and remediation. The action sequence is: (1) test against the online diagnostic endpoints, (2) verify offline against local trust stores, (3) update all system and language-level CA bundles, and (4) automate the check in CI so regressions from image rebuilds or dependency changes are caught immediately.

#10739. Ambulance driver accused of leaving chest pain patient to Uber home after getting lost

A private ambulance driver in Cleveland, Ohio, has been accused of abandoning a patient after getting lost, telling her he was “done driving for the day” before calling an Uber home.

The incident happened on Wednesday, October 28, while the woman was being taken to Cleveland Clinic with chest pain, according to Arizona’s Family.

As the story spread online, the Cleveland Association of Rescue Employees (CARE) Local 1975 stepped in to clarify that the driver did not work for Cleveland EMS.

“We want to clarify it was NOT a Cleveland EMS ambulance, but rather a provider working on a private ambulance,” the union wrote. “We stand by our EMTs, Paramedics, and Dispatchers who strive to provide the best care to anyone who is in need of it.”

The union also urged people to “research the facts before blindly sharing AI-generated images and articles,” pointing to an image that had been circulating alongside the story.

The driver worked for Lynx EMS, a private ambulance company. He reportedly got lost on the way to the hospital before allegedly telling the patient he was done for the day and leaving in an Uber.

A Cleveland EMS unit was sent to the scene, where paramedics treated the woman and took her to the hospital.

Lynx said it had taken immediate steps to address the situation and is reviewing the incident, but declined to comment further as it involves personnel and patient matters.

#10738. Prompt Caching API Example: Cut Token Costs in Node.js

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Prompt caching reduces LLM token costs by reusing a previously processed prompt prefix so the provider skips the need to re-tokenize and re-encode it on subsequent requests. This tutorial walks through building a TypeScript request-builder and usage-logging helper that normalizes prompt caching behavior across all three providers in Node.js.

Prompt caching reduces LLM token costs by reusing a previously processed prompt prefix so the provider skips the need to re-tokenize and re-encode it on subsequent requests. For Node.js developers making repeated API calls with stable system instructions, few-shot examples, or long reference documents, prompt caching can deliver up to 90% cost reduction on cached tokens.

The problem is that three major providers — OpenAI, Anthropic, and Gemini — each implement prompt caching differently. Opt-in mechanisms, minimum token thresholds, TTL behavior, cache-hit indicators, and discount rates all vary. Developers must piece together incompatible documentation.

The result is a single factory function that constructs cache-optimized requests and a unified logger that verifies cache hits regardless of provider.

This tutorial walks through building a TypeScript request-builder and usage-logging helper that normalizes prompt caching behavior across all three providers in Node.js. The result is a single factory function that constructs cache-optimized requests and a unified logger that verifies cache hits regardless of provider.

Prerequisites: Node.js 20+, TypeScript 5+, an API key for at least one of the three providers, and familiarity with async/await and REST API consumption.

All three providers cache from the beginning of the prompt forward — only the prefix qualifies. A "stable prefix" is any content that does not change between requests: system instructions, few-shot examples, long reference documents, or boilerplate context. Keep the prefix byte-identical across calls. Any mutation, even a whitespace change, invalidates the cache and forces a full re-processing pass.

Prompt design matters. Front-load all static content and append variable content (the user's actual question, dynamic context) at the end.

The following table captures the critical differences across OpenAI, Anthropic, and Gemini:

For explicit caching, Gemini requires the largest minimum prefix at 32,768 tokens, making it suitable only for very large context windows. Gemini 2.5 and newer also enable implicit caching by default, which works more like OpenAI’s automatic approach. Anthropic offers the steepest read-token discount at 90%, though Anthropic bills cache creation calls at 25% above standard input pricing. OpenAI is zero-config but offers the smallest discount at 50%.

Update tsconfig.json with these essentials:

The CacheAwareRequest holds the provider-agnostic input shape. The CacheUsageReport normalizes the output so that calling code never needs to know which provider-specific field indicates a cache hit. The cachedTokenDiscountPercent reflects the provider's discount rate on cached tokens, while estimatedCostSavingsPercent approximates actual savings relative to total tokens.

OpenAI caches automatically. There is no flag to set, no resource to create. The only requirement is that the prompt prefix must be at least 1,024 tokens long. The system message is the natural place for the stable prefix.

The systemPrompt string should contain the full stable prefix. As long as it exceeds 1,024 tokens and remains identical across calls, OpenAI will cache it automatically within its TTL window.

Anthropic requires explicit opt-in via a cache_control breakpoint. The breakpoint is placed on the last content block of the stable prefix. Everything before (and including) that block is cached; everything after is processed fresh.

The cache_control: { type: "ephemeral" } annotation marks the boundary. On the first call, Anthropic reports cache_creation_input_tokens. On subsequent calls within the 5-minute TTL, it reports cache_read_input_tokens instead.

Gemini uses a two-step process. First, create a cachedContent resource via a separate API call, specifying the content and an explicit TTL. Then reference that resource in subsequent generateContent calls. The minimum prefix is 32,768 tokens.

Note: The GoogleAICacheManager class may be exported from @google/generative-ai/server rather than the top-level @google/generative-ai depending on your installed SDK version. If the import below fails, try import { GoogleAICacheManager } from "@google/generative-ai/server". Verify with: node -e "const m = require('@google/generative-ai'); console.log(typeof m.GoogleAICacheManager)" — if it prints undefined, use the /server subpath.

The cached content object must be stored and reused. If the TTL expires, the resource must be recreated. In production, persist the cached content name to a database or cache store to survive process restarts; the in-memory Map used in the factory below is lost on every cold start, causing redundant cachedContent creation and additional billing.

A single factory function dispatches to the correct provider-specific builder and returns a normalized shape alongside the raw response for usage inspection.

A single factory function dispatches to the correct provider-specific builder and returns a normalized shape alongside the raw response for usage inspection.

The Gemini path stores cached content objects in a Map keyed by a SHA-256 hash of the full system prompt, avoiding both redundant cache creation on repeated calls and cache key collisions that could occur with prefix-based keys. A secondary inflight Map ensures that concurrent requests with the same prompt do not trigger duplicate cache creation calls.

OpenAI exposes cache data at response.usage.prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens. When this value exceeds zero, the request hit the cache.

Anthropic splits caching into two fields: response.usage.cache_creation_input_tokens (populated on the first request when the provider writes the cache) and response.usage.cache_read_input_tokens (populated on subsequent requests that read from it). Check whether cache_read_input_tokens > 0 to confirm a hit.

Check response.usageMetadata.cachedContentTokenCount. If it exceeds zero, the cached content resource was used for that request.

The cachedTokenDiscountPercent reflects the provider's discount rate applied to cached tokens specifically. The estimatedCostSavingsPercent approximates the actual savings relative to total tokens in the request — for example, if only 100 of 10,000 tokens were cached at Anthropic's 90% rate, the estimated savings would be ~0.9%, not 90%. The hash comparison across requests reveals whether the prefix changed. The token estimate uses a 1.3x multiplier on word count as a rough approximation (+-30% error); production systems should use provider-specific tokenizers (e.g., tiktoken for OpenAI) for precision.

Cache misses happen for several concrete reasons. You changed the prefix between requests (even whitespace or trailing newline differences count). Your prefix falls below the provider's minimum token threshold. The TTL expired — Anthropic's 5-minute default is particularly aggressive. You switched the model parameter between requests. Or, in Gemini's case, you did not pass the cachedContent name to the generation call.

Run with npx tsc && node dist/demo.js openai. Swap the argument to anthropic or gemini to test other providers. The first request populates the cache; the second reads from it.

Realistic savings depend on reuse frequency within the TTL window. OpenAI provides a 50% discount on cached input tokens. Anthropic provides 90% on cached read tokens, though Anthropic bills cache creation calls at 25% above standard input pricing. Gemini provides 75% for Gemini 1.5 Pro (the Flash rate is not confirmed at time of writing). Anthropic reports reduced latency on cache hits, but none of the three providers publish fixed latency benchmarks for caching; consult each provider's official prompt caching documentation for current figures.

These savings only materialize when the same prefix is reused frequently within the cache's TTL. A prefix that changes on every request, or one that falls below the provider's minimum token threshold, will never trigger caching, and you will pay standard pricing.

These savings only materialize when the same prefix is reused frequently within the cache's TTL. A prefix that changes on every request, or one that falls below the provider's minimum token threshold, will never trigger caching, and you will pay standard pricing.

Start by adding streaming response support to the request-builder, since cached prefixes work with streaming on all three providers. From there, implement persistent TTL management for Gemini's cachedContent resources (which otherwise require manual recreation after expiry), and wire the cache usage logger into an observability pipeline like OpenTelemetry spans for production cost monitoring.

For full details on each provider's caching behavior, consult the official documentation: OpenAI Prompt Caching, Anthropic Prompt Caching, and Gemini Context Caching.

#10730. CORS Error Fix: Diagnose Console Errors and Configure Headers

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every frontend developer will eventually stare at a CORS error in the browser console, watching an API call fail for reasons that seem opaque. A CORS error fix requires understanding what the browser is actually telling you, matching that specific message to its root cause, and applying a targeted, security-conscious server configuration.

The scheme, host, and port together define an "origin." https://example.com and http://example.com are different origins (different schemes), as are https://example.com and https://api.example.com (different hosts). https://example.com and https://example.com:443 are the same origin because port 443 is the default for HTTPS, and browsers omit it in origin comparisons. The browser enforces the same-origin policy on client-side JavaScript reading responses, not on sending requests. This distinction trips up many developers: the server may still receive and fully process the request; the browser simply refuses to expose the response to the calling code. The request lands, the database row gets written, and the browser throws an error anyway. The policy exists to prevent a malicious page from silently reading sensitive data from another origin using the user's ambient credentials.

Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS) is a controlled opt-in mechanism via HTTP response headers. The server signals to the browser that a specific foreign origin is permitted to read its responses. Four headers control the mechanism: Access-Control-Allow-Origin specifies which origin is allowed, and Access-Control-Allow-Methods declares which HTTP methods are permitted. Access-Control-Allow-Headers lists which custom or non-simple headers the client may send, while Access-Control-Allow-Credentials indicates whether the browser should expose the response to credentialed requests.

The browser does not treat all cross-origin requests equally. "Simple" requests use GET, HEAD, or POST with a Content-Type of application/x-www-form-urlencoded, multipart/form-data, or text/plain, and no custom headers. A POST with Content-Type: application/json is not a simple request and triggers a preflight.

Simple requests go directly to the server, and the browser evaluates the CORS headers on the response. Anything else triggers a preflight request: an OPTIONS call the browser sends automatically before the actual request, asking the server whether the real request would be acceptable. If the OPTIONS response lacks the proper CORS headers, the browser never sends the real request at all.

A typical Chrome DevTools error reads: Access to fetch at 'https://api.example.com/data' from origin 'https://app.example.com' has been blocked by CORS policy: No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header is present on the requested resource.

Two origins appear in this message. The URL after "fetch at" is the target server, the resource the code tried to reach. The value after "from origin" is the page's own origin, the one making the request. Chrome is stating that the target server's response did not include an Access-Control-Allow-Origin header that matches the requesting origin, so the browser blocked the response from reaching JavaScript.

The most common variant is the missing header: No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header is present on the requested resource. The server simply did not include the header at all.

When the console says Response to preflight request doesn't pass access control check, the server either did not respond to the OPTIONS request correctly or returned a non-2xx status code for it.

Redirect variant: Redirect is not allowed for a preflight request. Developers commonly misdiagnose this one. It occurs when the URL triggers a redirect before reaching the application handler. Typical culprits include HTTP-to-HTTPS upgrades, trailing-slash normalization (e.g., /api/data redirecting to /api/data/), or a load balancer rewriting URLs. The browser's preflight OPTIONS request follows the redirect, and the Fetch specification (§4.8) forbids following redirects on preflight requests, so the entire flow fails. Fix the URL so no redirect occurs.

Credential variant: The value of the 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header must not be the wildcard '*' when the request's credentials mode is 'include'. When fetch is called with credentials: 'include' or XMLHttpRequest sets withCredentials = true, the server must respond with the exact requesting origin, not *. The browser will block JavaScript access to the response if a wildcard is used with credentials.

Entry point: What does the console error say?

Each branch leads to a specific fix section in this article. Bookmark this decision tree and start here every time a CORS error surfaces.

Before modifying any server code, verify what the server actually returns by simulating a preflight request from the command line:

The -i flag prints response headers. -X OPTIONS sets the HTTP method to OPTIONS, mimicking the browser's preflight. The Origin header declares the requesting origin. Access-Control-Request-Method tells the server which method the real request would use. Access-Control-Request-Headers lists the custom headers the real request would include.

If these are absent, the server is not configured for CORS. If the response is a 301 or 302 redirect, the redirect variant is the culprit.

During local development, the least-configuration way to eliminate cross-origin issues is to proxy API requests through the dev server so the browser sees all traffic as same-origin.

With this configuration, a fetch('/api/users') call from the frontend at http://localhost:5173 is forwarded to http://localhost:3001/users. The browser never sees a cross-origin request. Without changeOrigin: true, the Host header sent upstream retains the dev server's hostname, which may cause backend rejections if the target validates the Host header. This is strictly a development-only solution. Production builds served from a static host will not have this proxy, so proper server-side CORS headers remain necessary.

Tested with: cors@2.8.5, express@4.18, Node.js 20 LTS. The cors package API surface has been stable since 2.x; check the changelog if you are on an earlier major version.

The simplest fix to unblock development uses the cors npm package with its defaults:

This applies Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * to every response. It unblocks any requesting origin, but it cannot be used with credentialed requests, and it exposes every endpoint to every origin on the internet. Any page an attacker controls can issue authenticated requests to your API and read the responses if the user is logged in. Move past it quickly.

For production, validate the incoming Origin header against an allowlist and reflect only permitted origins:

The origin function receives the Origin header value and calls callback(null, true) to allow the request. When true is passed, the cors package automatically reflects the incoming Origin header in the Access-Control-Allow-Origin response header and sets Vary: Origin. This satisfies the credential requirement and prevents open CORS misconfiguration. Setting credentials: true in the options causes the middleware to include the Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true response header. The cors middleware automatically handles OPTIONS preflight responses when applied at the app level.

Tested with: Next.js 14.x App Router, Node.js 20 LTS. The OPTIONS handler behavior and middleware matcher syntax changed between Next.js 13 and 15; exporting an explicit OPTIONS handler is the safest cross-version approach.

The cors npm package does not plug directly into the Next.js App Router (as of Next.js 13+). Instead, set headers manually in a route handler:

The OPTIONS export explicitly handles preflight requests and returns a 204 No Content with the required CORS headers. Without this, Next.js returns a 405 Method Not Allowed for OPTIONS in some versions (the exact behavior changed between 13 and 14), triggering the preflight failure variant. Exporting an explicit OPTIONS handler is the safest cross-version approach. The getCorsHeaders function returns an empty object for disallowed or absent origins, ensuring that server capabilities (supported methods and headers) are not disclosed to untrusted origins.

For projects with many API routes, centralizing CORS logic in middleware avoids repetition:

The matcher restricts this middleware to API routes only. Access-Control-Max-Age instructs browsers to cache preflight results. Chrome caps this value at 7200 seconds (2 hours); Firefox honors up to 86400. The Vary: Origin header is only set when a cross-origin request is detected (the Origin header is present and allowed), avoiding unnecessary CDN cache fragmentation on same-origin or server-to-server requests. CORS headers including Access-Control-Allow-Methods and Access-Control-Allow-Headers are only sent to allowed origins, preventing server capability disclosure to untrusted origins.

Tested with: Nginx 1.24+. The map directive behavior is stable across modern Nginx versions.

When Nginx sits in front of a backend, it is often the right place to manage CORS centrally. Important: Do not use if with add_header inside a location block -- Nginx's if block causes all parent-scope add_header directives to be silently ignored, breaking CORS headers on actual (non-OPTIONS) API responses.

The map directive builds an allowlist: if $http_origin matches a listed value, $cors_origin is set to that value; otherwise it remains empty. The always parameter ensures headers are added even on error responses (4xx, 5xx), which is critical because Nginx's add_header without always silently drops headers on non-2xx responses.

Nginx's if directive creates a new configuration context. Any add_header inside an if block discards all add_header directives from the enclosing scope.

Why the if block contains all headers and return 204: By placing return 204 inside the if block, the OPTIONS request is fully handled there and never reaches the outer add_header directives or proxy_pass. Non-OPTIONS requests skip the if block entirely and the outer scope's add_header directives and proxy_pass handle them. This separation avoids the inheritance pitfall: each request path (OPTIONS vs. non-OPTIONS) has its own complete, self-contained set of headers.

Note on empty $cors_origin: When the allowlist excludes the origin, $cors_origin resolves to an empty string, and Nginx emits Access-Control-Allow-Origin: (a blank header). Browsers will reject CORS for the request, which is the correct security outcome. However, emitting a blank header is technically non-conformant with RFC 6454. The most robust production solution is to handle CORS in the application layer (Express/Next.js) and have Nginx proxy transparently, or to use OpenResty/Lua for conditional header suppression.

After deploying, verify that both curl -X OPTIONS and curl -X GET against the same URL return the expected CORS headers.

A frequent source of the redirect CORS variant is a proxy_pass trailing-slash mismatch. If the location block is location /api but proxy_pass is http://127.0.0.1:3001 (no trailing slash), Nginx may issue a redirect for /api/ normalization before the request reaches the handler. Similarly, a return 301 https://... rule placed before the CORS location block can redirect the OPTIONS preflight. The fix is straightforward: ensure trailing slashes match between location and proxy_pass, and place any redirect rules after or outside the API location block so they do not intercept OPTIONS requests.

Use an explicit origin allowlist. Avoid * when credentials are involved; the browser will block JavaScript access to the response if a wildcard is used with credentials: 'include'.

Restrict Access-Control-Allow-Methods to only the methods the API actually uses. Listing every HTTP method widens the attack surface unnecessarily.

Set Access-Control-Max-Age to cache preflight responses. Chrome caps this value at 7200 seconds (2 hours); Firefox honors up to 86400 (24 hours). Use 7200 as a safe cross-browser default, or 86400 if your users are primarily on Firefox and you want fewer preflight round-trips.

Include Vary: Origin on every response where Access-Control-Allow-Origin is set conditionally. Without it, CDN or proxy caches may serve a CORS response cached for one origin to a different origin, causing intermittent failures.

Never mirror the request Origin header back without validating it against an allowlist. This is an open CORS misconfiguration that effectively disables the same-origin policy for any attacker-controlled origin.

Do not install browser extensions that disable CORS either -- they mask the problem during development and expose you to real cross-origin attacks on every other site you visit.

Avoid adding CORS headers in both the application framework and Nginx simultaneously. Duplicate Access-Control-Allow-Origin headers cause the browser's CORS origin comparison to fail; the browser receives the response but blocks JavaScript from reading it, producing the same CORS error the fix was meant to resolve.

Read the console error, match it against the decision tree, apply the targeted fix, and verify with the curl preflight command. Use the decision tree above as a reference for every future CORS error.

#10728. Niftski breaks Super Mario Bros. world record in Mario costume as perfect run comes within 2 frames

Super Mario Bros. speedrunner Niftski set a new any% world record on Halloween while dressed as Mario, completing the NES classic in 4:54.299 and moving within two frames of a theoretically perfect run.

The performance lowered Niftski’s previous record of 4:54.332, set in August 2026, by 33 milliseconds. It also pushed the game’s human leaderboard closer than ever to the current benchmark for perfection.

Niftski has spent years pushing Super Mario Bros. toward its limits. In 2021, he became the first player to complete the game in under 4:55, a milestone that was compared to breaking the four-minute mile in speedrunning.

The record chase has since become a battle between Niftski and Brazilian runner LeKukie. LeKukie briefly claimed the world record in June with a 4:54.365 run, cutting the gap to the theoretical limit to six frames before Niftski reclaimed the top spot two months later.

“Only 2 frames (33 milliseconds) stand between this run and absolute perfection / TAS tie,” Niftski wrote after the run.

4:54.299 SMB1 ANY% WORLD RECORD ON HALLOWEEN WHILE LARPING AS MARIO! It doesn't even feel real that I actually achieved this, but here we are. Only 2 frames (33 milliseconds) stand between this run and absolute perfection / TAS tie. 4:54.265 next! pic.twitter.com/S1ZpmEMG4c

That target is based on a tool-assisted speedrun, or TAS, which uses frame-by-frame inputs to determine how quickly the game can theoretically be completed. The relevant benchmark is 4:54.265, created under the same rules used for real-time runs.

There is an even faster TAS time of 4:54.032, but that version uses simultaneous left-and-right inputs that are impossible on a standard NES controller and are banned from human attempts. The 4:54.265 benchmark is therefore treated as the practical theoretical limit for the category.

Super Mario Bros. runs at 60 frames per second, meaning each frame lasts roughly 16.7 milliseconds. The game also uses 21-frame cycles to determine when most levels are completed. That means a runner can lose a few frames in some stages without losing time on the final clock, as long as they still reach the next cycle.

Mario speedrunner claims he’s being derailed on way to ‘perfect’ run

Speedrunner breaks Super Mario Bros world record by the thinnest of margins

Minecraft speedrunner finally sets world record one year after devastating last-second fail

The final level, World 8-4, works differently. It does not offer the same frame-rule cushion, so every successful jump, movement and button press can affect the final result. That is where the remaining gap between Niftski’s record and the TAS benchmark must be erased.

LeKukie remains close behind in the race, and both runners have been streaming attempts at the same impossible-looking goal.

Niftski’s latest record proves that the barrier is no longer theoretical in the way it once seemed. The game has been played for more than 40 years, yet the fastest human performance is now separated from machine-perfect play by less than the blink of an eye.

In 2021, breaking 4:55 felt like the end of what a person could do in Super Mario Bros. Now the question is whether Niftski, LeKukie or another runner can string together the final two frames and complete the first human TAS tie.

#10721. Building Accessible Navigation Menus with HTML, CSS, and JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Navigation is one of the most important components of a website. It helps visitors discover content, move between pages, and understand how a site is organized. However, a navigation menu that works well with a mouse may create problems for keyboard users, people who rely on screen readers, or visitors using small mobile screens.

Building an accessible navigation menu involves more than adding a few ARIA attributes or increasing the size of clickable elements. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, keyboard interaction, visible focus indicators, responsive layouts, and predictable behavior.

In this tutorial, we will build a responsive navigation menu using HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. We will also examine common accessibility mistakes and explain how to test the finished component.

HTML provides several native elements that communicate the purpose and structure of a navigation interface.

A website's primary navigation should generally use a

element containing a list of links. This structure helps browsers and assistive technologies identify the navigation region and understand the available destinations.

The aria-label gives the navigation landmark a descriptive name. This becomes particularly useful when a page contains multiple navigation regions, such as a primary menu and a footer menu.

Using a list also communicates that the links form a related group. Each link has a meaningful destination, making it possible for visitors to navigate the site without relying on visual positioning alone.

A common mistake is using clickable

elements instead of links. Although JavaScript can make a div respond to clicks, it does not automatically provide the same keyboard behavior and navigation semantics as an anchor element.

For navigation to another page, use an anchor. For an action that changes the interface, use a button.

A navigation menu should adapt to the available screen width without hiding important links or creating horizontal scrolling.

The following CSS creates a flexible desktop navigation layout:

Flexbox aligns the logo and navigation horizontally while allowing the link list to wrap when necessary.

The gap property controls the spacing between links without requiring individual margins. Removing the list markers also produces a cleaner navigation layout while preserving the underlying list semantics.

Notice that the hover style includes an underline as well as a color change. This provides an additional visual cue instead of relying exclusively on color.

However, hover effects alone are insufficient because touchscreens do not have conventional hover behavior, and keyboard users do not navigate with a mouse.

Many visitors use the Tab key to move between interactive elements. A navigation menu should make the currently focused link easy to identify.

The :focus-visible pseudo-class allows browsers to display a focus indicator when it is appropriate, particularly during keyboard navigation.

Avoid removing the browser's default outline unless you replace it with a clearly visible alternative.

Keyboard accessibility also depends on the HTML structure. Standard anchor elements already support keyboard focus and activation, so the basic navigation above does not need custom JavaScript keyboard handlers.

That is an important design principle: use native browser behavior wherever possible rather than recreating it unnecessarily.

On smaller screens, a horizontal navigation menu may occupy too much space. A toggle button can provide a compact alternative while preserving access to the links.

The menu button is hidden on larger screens and displayed on smaller ones. On mobile, the navigation links are initially hidden until JavaScript adds the is-open class.

The aria-expanded attribute communicates whether the menu is currently expanded, while aria-controls identifies the element controlled by the button.

These attributes must reflect the actual interface state. Adding them without updating their values when the menu opens and closes can confuse assistive technology users.

The JavaScript should update both the visual state and the accessibility information when the user activates the button.

When the button is clicked, the code reads the current state, reverses it, updates aria-expanded, and toggles the CSS class.

Because the button is a native HTML button, it already supports keyboard activation. There is no need to add separate Enter or Space key handlers.

For a production implementation, you should also consider what happens when the viewport changes from mobile to desktop. For example, if the menu is open on mobile and the visitor resizes the browser, the desktop layout should not remain in an inconsistent state.

A more complete implementation can synchronize the menu state with the responsive breakpoint, close the menu when a navigation link is selected, and ensure that hidden links cannot be reached through keyboard navigation.

A navigation menu may look correct and still be difficult to use. Testing should cover both automated checks and real interactions.

Start with keyboard testing. Use Tab and Shift+Tab to move through the logo, menu button, and navigation links. Check that focus indicators are visible and that the order follows the visual and logical structure.

The menu button appears at the intended breakpoint.

Activating the button opens and closes the navigation.

The aria-expanded value matches the visible state.

Hidden links are not available for keyboard interaction while the menu is closed.

Selecting a link takes the visitor to the correct destination.

You can also use accessibility inspection tools to identify missing accessible names, invalid ARIA attributes, and other common problems.

Automated tools are useful, but they cannot establish that every interaction is understandable. Manual testing with a keyboard and, where possible, a screen reader remains important.

For larger websites, reusable navigation components and automated browser tests can help prevent accessibility regressions when styles or interaction logic change.

Accessible navigation starts with semantic HTML and builds on that foundation through responsive CSS, visible focus indicators, and carefully implemented JavaScript.

Native links and buttons provide much of the expected keyboard behavior automatically. Responsive styles make the menu usable across screen sizes, while accurate ARIA state helps communicate whether a collapsible menu is open or closed.

The most reliable approach is to keep the implementation as simple as possible, use browser features instead of recreating them, and test the actual experience rather than relying only on how the interface looks.

When accessibility is considered from the beginning, navigation becomes easier to maintain and more usable for a wider range of visitors.

#10713. What “Clear Form” Should Really Do: Managing Draft Data in JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A user fills out a form, requests a preview, and then changes their mind. They click “Clear,” the inputs become empty, and the interface appears ready for someone else.

A moment later, the previous preview returns.

The application cleared the fields, but an asynchronous request still held the old values and updated the screen when it finished. Similar problems occur when drafts remain in browser storage, validation messages retain personal details, or a component’s internal state survives a visual reset.

For developers building onboarding, expense, application, and document workflows, clearing a form is a state-management operation. It deserves the same attention as submission.

This article explains how to define that operation, prevent stale results, and inspect the outcome using practical browser and testing tools.

Start by listing the places where a draft exists. The visible inputs are only one location.

When reviewing a financial-data interface, a useful design exercise is to map which information belongs to the current draft and what should happen when the user starts again. The same exercise applies to expense submissions and customer onboarding; the implementation must be assessed separately from the visible interface.

Name actions according to their actual scope. “Clear this form” should not imply that previously submitted records have been deleted. If a draft has been saved remotely, distinguish clearing the current screen from deleting the saved draft.

For a broader introduction to form structure and validation, SitePoint’s guide to modern HTML forms provides useful background.

Autosave is convenient, but persisting the entire form after every keystroke is an architectural decision, not a harmless UI enhancement.

For a short workflow, keeping the draft in memory may be sufficient. For a longer workflow, decide which fields may be retained, how the user understands that behavior, and when the retained copy is removed.

Browser storage options have different lifetimes. According to MDN’s sessionStorage documentation, session storage is associated with a page session and survives reloads. It should not be treated as equivalent to an ordinary variable that disappears when the page reloads.

Nor does shorter retention make storage a security boundary. The OWASP HTML5 security guidance cautions against storing sensitive information in local storage because JavaScript running in the origin can access it.

This reduces unnecessary copies. It does not provide secure memory erasure or protect a page from every script that can access its data.

The native form.reset() method restores controls to their default values. It does not necessarily make every field empty.

For example, an input rendered with a default value can return to that value after a reset. The method also does not clear a separate preview element, invalidate a network response, or update every framework-managed state variable.

Use a small HTML form with empty defaults for the following example:

The clear control uses type="button" so the application can coordinate the complete operation.

In a React or Vue implementation, apply the same policy to the state that controls the inputs. Clearing the DOM while leaving the component state intact can cause the old values to reappear.

Cancellation helps reduce unnecessary work, but request identity is what protects the interface from a stale result.

Assign each request a generation number. Whenever the draft changes or is cleared, advance that number. A response may update the UI only if its number still matches the current generation.

The following JavaScript works with the HTML above. It assumes a same-origin POST /api/preview endpoint that accepts JSON and returns {"summary":"plain text"}. That endpoint is an integration requirement; it is not implemented here.

Editing a field also invalidates the preview. Otherwise, users could mistake a result based on earlier values for a result based on their current input.

The code writes server-provided text with textContent rather than interpreting it as HTML. It also displays a fixed failure message instead of echoing request values into an error.

AbortController allows the client to abort fetch-related work. Aborting a browser request does not guarantee that server processing stopped or that data already received by the server was deleted.

The generation check enforces the narrower promise: obsolete work must not update this interface. SitePoint’s introduction to the Fetch API explains the request and response mechanics behind the example.

The example intentionally does not save drafts. If your application does, add cleanup to the clear operation rather than assuming that resetting the form handles it.

Remove the application’s specific draft entry. Avoid localStorage.clear() as a general cleanup shortcut, since it also removes unrelated keys for that origin.

Keep the UI honest if storage cleanup fails. Do not announce that every saved copy is gone when the application has only emptied the visible controls.

An object URL should be revoked when it is no longer needed, but revoking it does not delete a file the user already downloaded.

For programmatic changes, call the same invalidation function used by input events. Assigning an input’s value in JavaScript does not automatically trigger the user-input event handler.

You do not need a large dependency stack to verify a clear operation. Each of these tools answers a different question.

Chrome’s local-storage inspection guide explains how to inspect stored entries. Check session storage and IndexedDB too if the application uses them; an empty local-storage table is not proof that no browser persistence exists.

Mock Service Worker is useful for creating reproducible response conditions during development. Use fictional fixtures so debugging sessions do not depend on real personal records.

With Playwright network interception, hold a preview response until the test explicitly releases it. Fill the form, request a preview, clear the draft, and only then release the response. Assert that the input remains empty and the preview never returns.

A controlled release is more reliable than hoping a fixed delay creates the right race. Also test two requests completing in reverse order.

A useful review checks the operation at several points:

Supplement automated accessibility checks with keyboard and screen-reader testing. The Playwright accessibility guide explains that automated checks detect only some accessibility problems.

Verify backend behavior separately. Server validation, authorization, retention, and deletion require their own implementation and tests. A clean browser interface cannot demonstrate those guarantees.

A reliable clear operation has a defined scope. It invalidates pending work, resets the application’s draft state, removes derived output, and handles persistence according to the product’s stated behavior.

The implementation can remain small. What matters is that the team knows where draft data lives and tests what happens when events arrive in an inconvenient order.

When developers make those boundaries explicit, “Clear form” becomes a predictable action rather than a visual reset that leaves the previous workflow running.

#10710. British man who spent 28 years walking around the world finally makes it home

A British adventurer is on the verge of completing a 28-year journey around the world, having travelled roughly 31,000 miles to reach his hometown.

Karl Bushby, a 57-year-old former paratrooper from Hull, set off from Chile on November 1, 1998, determined to make his way home without using transport to advance his journey.

What was originally intended to take 12 years turned into almost three decades of walking and swimming that saw him overcome everything from dangerous wildlife to international border disputes.

Now, on November 2, 2026, Bushby is due to complete his expedition by arriving at his mother’s house in Hull, 28 years and one day after setting out.

Known as the Goliath Expedition, Bushby’s journey took him across four continents and around 30 countries, encountering extreme temperatures, dangerous terrain, and numerous setbacks.

Speaking to The Guardian, Bushby explained that he had dreamed of going on a major adventure since childhood. His eventual plan was inspired by a birthday card from his father, which described SAS soldiers discussing the possibility of walking from London to New York.

Among his biggest challenges was crossing the frozen Bering Strait between Alaska and Russia in 2006, where he faced freezing conditions and even encountered a polar bear.

He was subsequently detained in Russia, while visa restrictions, financial problems, and the COVID-19 pandemic caused further delays.

“You can’t account for financial crises, pandemics, visa bans, wars and the bureaucracy you have to deal with,” he told The Guardian, explaining why it took so long to complete.

Couple spent $1 million on “endless” cruise before being told to leave in Cambodia

“Endless” cruise ship journey suddenly ends despite passengers selling homes to live on it

British man is dribbling a football from Africa to the UK in time for World Cup final

More recently, Bushby spent 31 days crossing the Caspian Sea before swimming the English Channel, finally reaching British soil on October 5.

But the journey also came at a significant personal cost. According to Associated Press, Bushby hasn’t seen his mother in around 10 years, while his son largely grew up without him. He also has a 12-year-old granddaughter he’s never met.

Despite waiting decades for this moment, the prospect of returning to normal life has also left him apprehensive.

“My purpose for living is about to abruptly end,” he told The Guardian, explaining that he would need to find a new direction after spending so much of his life travelling.

According to Hull City Council, Bushby is expected to receive a public welcome on November 2, before continuing to his family home to officially complete the journey.
