#10704. Best AI Brand Mention Tracking Tools for Enterprises in 2026: 6 Tools Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

At a large company, the hard part of buying an AI brand mention tracking tool is procurement. IT asks about single sign-on, legal asks where the data sits, and the marketing team that wanted the tool waits weeks to log in. A pilot on one brand in one market looks cheap, then the other markets, the other teams and SSO arrive and the quote looks nothing like the trial.

That is why a generic "best tools" list helps enterprise buyers less than it should. All the tools below track how often ChatGPT, Gemini and Google's AI answers name your brand. What separates them at enterprise size is everything around the tracking: how many people can log in, how logins are controlled, whether the data can leave the tool and whether it copes with ten markets instead of one.

This guide compares six tools on those points. Peec AI ranks first because it combines unlimited users, enforceable SSO, source-level analysis and a published price ladder. The other five suit different buying situations.

AI brand mention tracking measures how often, where and how favourably AI engines name your brand when buyers ask category questions. A tool runs a fixed prompt set daily across engines such as ChatGPT, Gemini, Google AI Overviews, Google AI Mode and Microsoft Copilot, then reports mention rate (visibility), position inside the answer, sentiment, share of voice against competitors and the sources each answer cited.

For an enterprise, the answers differ by engine, country and product line, and a single visibility number hides most of that. Similarweb's May 2026 figures put ChatGPT at 53.9% of AI chatbot web visits, Gemini at 27.9% and Claude at 9.2%, against roughly 76% for ChatGPT in June 2025. The question "what does AI say about us?" is now spread over at least three engines.

Access and identity. Ask how many users each plan allows, whether SSO is available and on which plan, and whether you can enforce it. Most tools put SSO on their top tier or a custom plan, so budget for an enterprise contract early.

Markets and brands. Check whether the price grows per country, language or brand. Ten markets multiply a prompt set quickly.

Data access. The data has to reach the reporting leadership already reads: CSV export, an API, a BI connector or an MCP server.

Source analysis. A mention rate shows where you stand. Source data shows why, by listing the pages each engine read. Without it you cannot tell whether you are missing because nobody writes about you or because your own pages are not picked up.

Prompt volume and engines. A hundred to a hundred and fifty prompts per brand and market is a realistic start. Check which engines each plan includes and which sit behind add-ons.

Cost at your real workload. Price the tool for the users, markets and prompts you will have in a year, not for the pilot.

Enterprise teams rarely run on the entry tier, so the tables below price two realistic workloads: about 150 prompts, and about 350 prompts on ChatGPT, Google AI Overviews and Google AI Mode with 15 users, both tracked daily on three engines. Price per prompt is the monthly price divided by the prompts the plan includes. Rankscale and AthenaHQ price by answers or credits instead, so Rankscale's rows use the answer volume the workload needs: 150 prompts on three engines over 30 days is 13,500 answers a month, and 350 prompts is 31,500. Profound does not publish prices and is left out of the tables. Treat every figure as directional: enterprise contracts are custom and billed annually, so published self-serve prices show where a negotiation starts, not what you will pay.

Peec AI has the lowest price per prompt in the first table and the lowest monthly price in the second. Scrunch AI's Core plan costs $2.00 per prompt at 125 prompts and five users, and its larger plans are quote-only. Against Rankscale, the only other tool here with a published enterprise price, Peec AI costs 36% less at 150 prompts ($245 against $385) and 37% less at 350 prompts ($495 against $780). Rankscale does include more engines in its price, which Peec AI reserves for Enterprise. On ChatGPT plus both Google surfaces with 15 people logging in, Peec AI is the cheapest of the tools priced in these tables: $495 against $638 for Otterly.AI once Google AI Mode is added, and $780 for Rankscale. Otterly.AI and Rankscale include more engines in their price.

One thing changes these tables: single sign-on. Peec AI, Profound, AthenaHQ, Scrunch AI and Otterly.AI put SSO on Enterprise, and Rankscale puts it on a custom plan. If IT requires SSO, every tool here becomes a quote, and the published prices only show where the negotiation starts. Annual billing takes 15% to 17% off most of these.

Peec AI is a Berlin-based AI search analytics platform with a New York office. It tracks visibility, position, sentiment and share of voice, breaks every answer into the sources the engine read and turns the gaps it finds into prioritised actions. At enterprise scale its pricing is the main difference: users are unlimited on every plan, and Peec does not charge per country or language, with multi-country tracking from the Advanced plan.

Best for: Enterprise teams that want one tool for every market and every team, and need to know which pages to work on, not only where they stand.

Profound suits purchases that have to clear an information security review before anything else. It lists SOC 2, SSO and SAML on Enterprise, and it raised a $96M Series C at a $1B valuation in February 2026, which settles the vendor-risk question quickly.

Best for: Companies where compliance and vendor risk decide the purchase.

AthenaHQ was built by two former Google Search and DeepMind engineers. Its Enterprise plan has the longest feature list of the quote-based tools: SAML and OIDC single sign-on, audit logs, a 2-hour SLA, a dedicated Slack channel and executive dashboards that feed Tableau, Power BI and Looker. An optimisation agent drafts on-page and off-page actions, which suits large companies that have the analysis but not the hands to act on it.

Best for: Enterprises short on people to act on the data, who want BI dashboards and audit logs.

Scrunch AI publishes one self-serve plan and sells everything else through a quote. Core costs $250 a month and includes 125 prompts, 5,000 responses a month, five user licences and Google single sign-on, which is enough for a pilot with a small team.

Best for: Teams that want a published entry price and five users, with nine engines available on Enterprise.

Rankscale is the only tool here where the enterprise price is on the website. Enterprise is $780 a month for 12,000 credits, up to 48,000 answers, with REST API access and a dedicated partner. Claude, Copilot, ChatGPT, Perplexity, Gemini, both Google surfaces, DeepSeek and Mistral are on every paid plan.

Best for: Teams that need a published enterprise price and the widest engine list.

Otterly.AI has the lowest entry price here, with unlimited team members on every plan and unlimited workspaces from the Standard plan, which helps when several business units want their own view. It is a lighter product than the five above, and SSO sits on its Enterprise plan, which starts from 1,000 prompts.

Best for: Business units that want their own workspace at a low price.

The most common mistake is buying for the pilot. A team runs a trial on one brand and one market, picks the tool with the friendliest entry price and finds out in month four that adding other markets, other teams and SSO moves them onto a quote that looks nothing like the trial.

Before you start a trial, write down three numbers: how many people will need to log in within a year, how many markets you sell in, and whether IT will require SSO. Then ask every vendor to price that.

Enterprise AI brand mention tracking is mostly a question of fit with how a large company buys. Peec AI is the strongest overall choice because users and markets do not drive its price, SSO can be enforced, and its source analysis shows what to fix instead of only how you rank.

Profound is the better route when a compliance review decides everything, AthenaHQ when you want BI dashboards and drafted work, Scrunch AI when you want a published entry price with five users, and Rankscale when you need a published enterprise price.

Whichever you pick, price your real workload, ask about SSO first and remember that the tool measures: what moves the number is being on the pages the engines read. The best AI brand mention tracking tools for enterprises are the ones that show you which pages those are.

What does an enterprise AI brand mention tracking tool cost?

Directionally, between $245 and $780 a month on published plans for 150 to 350 daily prompts: Peec AI Pro at $245 and Advanced at $495, Scrunch AI Core at $250 for 125 prompts, Otterly.AI Premium at $489 and Rankscale at $385 to $780. On Peec AI that is $1.41 to $1.63 per prompt with unlimited users. Once SSO is required, every tool here is a custom quote. The spread is driven by users and markets more than by features.

If more than a handful of people will log in, IT will usually ask for it. Every tool here puts SSO on Enterprise or a custom plan, so plan the budget for that contract instead of the self-serve tier.

How many prompts does an enterprise need?

A hundred to a hundred and fifty per brand and market. Start with the questions your sales team hears, then the comparisons buyers make between you and named competitors. More markets multiply the set. More product pages usually should not.

SEO usually owns the measurement. What breaks is the fix, because much of it means getting mentioned on sites you do not control, which sits with PR or communications. Agree that split before you buy.

#10703. Scientists think they’ve discovered a new dwarf planet in our solar system

Scientists may have discovered a new dwarf planet hiding on the edge of our Solar System, and it could be one of the largest objects of its kind ever found.

Our Solar System is home to eight planets, but there are also smaller worlds orbiting the Sun, including Pluto, which was famously stripped of its planet status in 2006.

Now, scientists believe another could be joining the list after discovering a mysterious object far beyond Neptune, the most distant of our eight planets.

Named 2026 ED138, the object could be between 370 and 930 miles wide, although more research is needed before it can officially be classified as a dwarf planet.

The discovery was made using the Vera C. Rubin Observatory in Chile, which houses the largest digital camera ever built.

According to the observatory’s official announcement, astronomer Matthew Holman spotted the object on October 13 while analyzing images captured earlier in 2026.

“My first reaction was one of excited disbelief,” Holman said. “Objects like this are very rare, so I immediately started checking whether I or the code had done something wrong.”

ED138 is currently around 46 times farther from the Sun than Earth, placing it beyond Pluto’s average orbital distance.

Despite its estimated size, it had remained undiscovered because it appears against a particularly crowded area of the night sky, filled with millions of stars.

Dwarf planets are objects that orbit the Sun and are large enough for gravity to pull them into a roughly round shape, but haven’t cleared other objects from their orbital paths. There are currently five officially recognized dwarf planets in our Solar System: Pluto, Ceres, Eris, Haumea, and Makemake.

Scientists believe ED138 could meet the requirements, but they need further observations to confirm its size and shape.

There might even be more to come, as the team suggested that several more similarly large objects could still be waiting to be found in the outer Solar System.

This comes months after scientists found a planet like Earth with everything needed to support life.

#10700. Supermarket fridges hacked again as cyberattack turns them up to 122 degrees

A number of stores across Europe have been hit with cyberattacks, with fridges having their temperatures increased to 122 degrees, forcing them to throw away food.

Back in early October, hackers took aim at a number of supermarkets and stores in France. Over the course of two days, they ended up hacking hundreds of fridges, increasing their internal temperatures to 122 degrees, thereby spoiling food and forcing stores to throw it away.

Now, as the calendar has turned over to November, similar attacks have taken place across Europe.

According to the BBC, numerous chains in Belgium, Ireland, Luxembourg, and Sweden have been affected by the new round of attacks, with their fridges are rising to boiling temperatures. Additionally, reports from HLN the Netherlands say that stores in the Netherlands, Finland, and Estonia are also affected.

As per the BBC’s report, 40 Delhaize supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg have been attacked, 13 Supervalu stores in Ireland have been affected, while ICA and Coop shops in Sweden have also been hit by the attack.

“Our refrigerators and freezers have been hacked overnight, and they have set the temperature to 50 degrees,” one ICA spokesperson said.

HLN also reported that IKEA in Belgium has been hit, but noted that the impact is “limited” for most customers.

Some of the other stores have blamed a “malfunction,” while others have cited an “IT incident” as the reason why they’ve had to dispose of food.

#10695. Spotify to charge users to remix songs with AI, but it’ll pay the real artists more

Spotify is reportedly set to launch a new paid add-on that will allow users to make remixes of songs with AI, and pay artists on top of it.

Like most platforms these days, Spotify has had to deal with AI-generated content being uploaded. A number of ‘new’ songs have been uploaded to the profiles of deceased artists, while AI artists have blurred the lines between what’s real and what’s not.

Now, according to The Financial Times, Spotify is considering the implementation of a paid add-on that will let users remix songs and make covers of popular tunes using AI.

As per the report, the new feature would come with an additional charge – around $4-$5 on top of your usual premium subscription – and will allow users to “create their own versions of songs on the platform.” Additionally, the report states that this feature could roll out in early 2027.

The FT noted that users could easily turn a country track into a more upbeat, dance tune with the new tool.

According to the report, these remixed songs could be uploaded to Spotify and added to different playlists, but all plays and royalties would be credited to the artist whose track has been used.

However, artists who don’t use AI near their songs would have the ability to opt out. Multiple bands have come out against the use of AI recently, including Fontaines DC, who were surprised when they noticed AI had been used in the music video for the song ‘Marianne.’

This tool has been in the works for quite some time, with the streaming service striking a deal with Universal Music back in May so that their tracks can be used.

Spotify has given users the ability to generate playlists with AI prompts in the past, but this new feature would be a big step.

It may also bring into question how the tracks would be labeled, seeing as Spotify now has verified tags for real artists versus AI-generated ones.

#10693. NASA’s Perseverance rover completes the first AI-planned drive on Mars

The team behind NASA's six-wheeled Mars explorer tested a vision-enabled artificial intelligence system to map a safe route across the Martian surface without relying on human route planners.

NASA's Perseverance rover has now completed the first drives on another planet that were planned by artificial intelligence. The milestone demonstration took place on Dec. 8 and 10 and was led by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California. During the test, generative AI was used to select waypoints for the rover, a complex planning task that is normally handled by human experts on Earth.

"This demonstration shows how far our capabilities have advanced and broadens how we will explore other worlds," said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman. "Autonomous technologies like this can help missions to operate more efficiently, respond to challenging terrain, and increase science return as distance from Earth grows. It's a strong example of teams applying new technology carefully and responsibly in real operations."

How Vision AI Helped Navigate the Martian Surface

For the demonstration, engineers used a form of generative AI known as vision-language models to examine existing data from JPL's surface mission dataset. The system analyzed the same images and information that human planners typically use, then identified waypoint locations so Perseverance could travel safely across difficult Martian terrain.

The work was coordinated from JPL's Rover Operations Center (ROC) and carried out in collaboration with Anthropic, using the company's Claude AI models.

Mars sits an average of about 140 million miles (225 million kilometers) from Earth. That distance creates long communication delays, making real-time control of a rover impossible. For nearly three decades, rover navigation has depended on human drivers who carefully study terrain data and plan routes in advance.

These planners design paths made up of waypoints, usually spaced no more than 330 feet (100 meters) apart, to reduce the risk of encountering hazards. The completed plans are sent through NASA's Deep Space Network, and the rover carries out the instructions on its own.

AI Takes Over Route Planning for Perseverance

During Perseverance's drives on the 1,707 and 1,709 Martian days, known as sols, the mission team shifted that responsibility to generative AI. The system examined high-resolution orbital images captured by the HiRISE (High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment) camera aboard NASA's Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter, along with terrain slope data from digital elevation models.

Using this information, the AI identified important surface features such as bedrock, outcrops, boulder fields, and sand ripples. It then produced a continuous driving path that included all necessary waypoints.

Before sending the commands to Mars, engineers ran the AI-generated instructions through JPL's digital twin (virtual replica of the rover). This step checked more than 500,000 telemetry variables to ensure the plan would work safely with Perseverance's flight software.

On Dec. 8, Perseverance traveled 689 feet (210 meters) using the AI-generated plan. Two days later, it drove another 807 feet (246 meters).

What This Means for Future Space Exploration

"The fundamental elements of generative AI are showing a lot of promise in streamlining the pillars of autonomous navigation for off-planet driving: perception (seeing the rocks and ripples), localization (knowing where we are), and planning and control (deciding and executing the safest path)," said Vandi Verma, a space roboticist at JPL and a member of the Perseverance engineering team. "We are moving towards a day where generative AI and other smart tools will help our surface rovers handle kilometer-scale drives while minimizing operator workload, and flag interesting surface features for our science team by scouring huge volumes of rover images."

"Imagine intelligent systems not only on the ground at Earth, but also in edge applications in our rovers, helicopters, drones, and other surface elements trained with the collective wisdom of our NASA engineers, scientists, and astronauts," said Matt Wallace, manager of JPL's Exploration Systems Office. "That is the game-changing technology we need to establish the infrastructure and systems required for a permanent human presence on the Moon and take the U.S. to Mars and beyond."

Managed for NASA by Caltech, JPL is home to the Rover Operations Center (ROC). The laboratory also oversees daily operations of the Perseverance rover for NASA's Science Mission Directorate as part of the agency's Mars Exploration Program portfolio.

Materials provided by Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10689. “Existential risk” – Why scientists are racing to define consciousness

As artificial intelligence continues to advance and ethical concerns grow alongside it, scientists say the need to understand consciousness has reached a critical point.

In a new review published in Frontiers in Science, researchers warn that progress in AI and neurotechnology is moving faster than scientific understanding of consciousness. This gap, they argue, could lead to serious ethical problems if it is not addressed.

The authors say explaining how consciousness emerges is now an urgent scientific and moral priority. A clearer understanding could eventually make it possible to develop scientific methods for detecting consciousness. That breakthrough would have far-reaching consequences for AI development, prenatal policy, animal welfare, medicine, mental health care, law, and emerging technologies such as brain-computer interfaces.

"Consciousness science is no longer a purely philosophical pursuit. It has real implications for every facet of society -- and for understanding what it means to be human," said lead author Prof Axel Cleeremans from Université Libre de Bruxelles. "Understanding consciousness is one of the most substantial challenges of 21st-century science -- and it's now urgent due to advances in AI and other technologies.

"If we become able to create consciousness -- even accidentally -- it would raise immense ethical challenges and even existential risk," added Cleeremans, a European Research Council (ERC) grantee.

Consciousness, commonly described as awareness of both the world around us and ourselves, remains one of science's most difficult puzzles. Despite decades of research, scientists still lack agreement on how subjective experience emerges from biological processes.

Researchers have identified brain regions and neural activity linked to conscious experience, but major disagreements remain. Scientists continue to debate which brain systems are truly necessary for consciousness and how they interact to produce awareness. Some researchers even question whether this approach captures the problem correctly.

The new review examines the current state of consciousness science, future directions for the field, and the possible consequences if humans succeed in fully explaining or even creating consciousness. This includes the possibility of consciousness emerging in machines or in lab-grown brain-like systems known as "brain organoids."

The authors argue that developing evidence-based tests for consciousness could transform how awareness is identified across many contexts. Such tools could help detect consciousness in patients with brain injuries or dementia and determine when awareness arises in fetuses, animals, brain organoids, or even AI systems.

While this would represent a major scientific advance, the researchers caution that it would also create difficult ethical and legal questions. Determining that a system is conscious would force society to reconsider how that system should be treated.

"Progress in consciousness science will reshape how we see ourselves and our relationship to both artificial intelligence and the natural world," said co-author Prof Anil Seth from the University of Sussex and ERC grantee. "The question of consciousness is ancient -- but it's never been more urgent than now."

A deeper understanding of consciousness could have wide-ranging effects across society.

In medicine, it could improve care for patients who are unresponsive and assumed to be unconscious. Measurements inspired by integrated information theory and global workspace theory[1] have already detected signs of awareness in some individuals diagnosed with unresponsive wakefulness syndrome. Further progress could refine these tools to better assess consciousness in coma, advanced dementia, and anesthesia, and influence treatment decisions and end-of-life care.

Mental health treatment could also benefit. Understanding the biological basis of subjective experience may help researchers develop better therapies for conditions such as depression, anxiety, and schizophrenia by narrowing the gap between animal studies and human emotional experience.

Greater insight into consciousness could reshape how humans think about their moral responsibilities toward animals. Identifying which animals and systems are sentient could influence animal research practices, farming, food consumption, and conservation strategies. "Understanding the nature of consciousness in particular animals would transform how we treat them and emerging biological systems that are being synthetically generated by scientists," said co-author Prof Liad Mudrik from Tel Aviv University and ERC grantee.

Consciousness research could also affect how the legal system understands responsibility. New findings may challenge traditional legal concepts such as mens rea, the "guilty mind" required to establish intent. As neuroscience reveals how much behavior arises from unconscious processes, courts may need to reconsider where responsibility begins and ends.

At the same time, advances in AI, brain organoids, and brain-computer interfaces raise the possibility of creating or altering awareness beyond natural biological limits. While some researchers believe consciousness might arise through computation alone, others argue that biological factors play an essential role. "Even if 'conscious AI' is impossible using standard digital computers, AI that gives the impression of being conscious raises many societal and ethical challenges," said Seth.

The authors emphasize the need for a coordinated, evidence-based approach to studying consciousness. One proposed strategy involves adversarial collaborations, in which competing theories are tested against each other through experiments designed jointly by their supporters. "We need more team science to break theoretical silos and overcome existing biases and assumptions," said Mudrik. "This step has the potential to move the field forward."

The researchers also argue that scientific work should place greater emphasis on phenomenology (what consciousness feels like) alongside studies of function (what consciousness does).

"Cooperative efforts are essential to make progress -- and to ensure society is prepared for the ethical, medical, and technological consequences of understanding, and perhaps creating, consciousness," said Cleeremans.

Materials provided by Frontiers. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10686. AI Agents Need Idempotency: The Distributed Systems Problem Hiding Inside Agentic AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A customer wants a $480 refund for a faulty part of a shipment he received. After waiting for 30 seconds for the payment API to process the refund, the customer support AI sends another request. It ultimately is fulfilled. However, as it turns out, the first request had also been fulfilled. The only thing missing was the notification of the refund for the customer. So the customer received two refunds of $480 each instead of the one he was asking for.

The AI was acting appropriately within its given constraints; the surrounding system couldn’t tell if the initial action had already been taken and failed to deliver the result. A well-known problem in distributed systems is gaining more notoriety as AI-powered agents increasingly perform actions beyond simply generating text. Idempotence is the solution to this problem.

Idempotence means an action can be performed multiple times with the same result as performing it once. This concept is particularly important for an AI agent that issues refunds, creates tickets, sends emails, or updates business applications.

AI agents as distributed software components have simple processes.

Think → choose a tool → call an API → receive a result → decide what to do next.

As we have outlined, many of the agent actions will utilize services and networks that can have issues from time to time. Consider the simple action of asking a payment provider to issue a refund. There are three possible outcomes:

The payment service never gets the request.

The refund went through, and the agent got the answer.

The refund went through, but the agent never received it back.

The most severe case, however, is when the time to complete an operation exceeds the timeout, but the operation does complete successfully. Since there is no answer, the software agent never learns of the operation's successful completion and must attempt to complete the operation again.

Distributed systems have dealt with the issues of unreliable networks for a long time already (retries, duplicate requests, etc.). By introducing agents, we give the software more freedom to choose when and how to proceed.

Consider an agent with these tools: search_customer(), check_inventory(), refund_payment(), send_email(), create_order(), delete_account()

Calling search_customer() twice will usually not cause much trouble.

In other words, an agent runtime should be able to tell if a tool only gets data or makes changes to the outside world. In the second group are things like sending money, getting money back, placing orders, writing messages, making accounts, and changing infrastructure.

There are side effects to these treatments. It's risky to think that agents can just retry every failed tool call as they get access to more business systems.

One common way to handle the duplicate requests issue is to implement idempotency keys.

Idempotency-Key: refund-order-123-run-842

The service stores the idempotency key and the result of processing the associated request.

It allows the refund to be processed the first time the idempotency key is sent to the service by the agent. Thereafter, if the response to the refund is lost and the agent attempts to resubmit the refund with the same idempotency key, the service will recognize that the refund has already been processed and return the previous result to the agent instead of processing another refund.

Many of the world’s leading payment companies, including Stripe, PayPal, and Square, use idempotency keys to allow their customers to safely retry requests.

However, one important rule for agents is that the agent software or workflow system—not the AI model—creates the idempotency key, and it is up to date.

By the time the model retries the same step, the Operation ID will have remained the same. This means the system can recognize the request as another attempt to execute the same action as before and not a new action.

Things could be easy if we could tell the model to ‘remember if it has already issued one of these before issuing another.' But even in the production environment, this approach is not sufficient. The agent could crash. The context could go away. The workflow could be taken off to be executed on another machine. The problem is that the model lacks sufficient information about what has or has not happened in external systems. Instead, the system should keep a record of the execution state outside of the model. The model determines what should happen. The runtime determines safe execution.

Modern agent tools include a name, description, and input parameters for the production system. More importantly, the agent runtime also needs to know about the behavior of the individual tool. For example, ‘refund_order’ modifies the application outside this tool, so the operation must be re-executed with the same actionID used for the initial failure. Also, as an example, refunds larger than $5,000 require manual approval. On the other hand, a ‘search_customer’ action is only a read, so re-execution can occur without human intervention. Based on this information, the OS makes decisions about the danger of the individual tool call. Automatic re-execution is still possible, even if the search fails. However, refunds require a bit more caution to avoid double posting of money. Thus, in addition to the input models for the AI tools used in real life, so-called “execution rules” are also required. These rules state when re-execution, increased security, or human intervention is required.

Suppose an AI agent were to perform the following three actions: 1) Make whole the customer, 2) Send a confirmation email with the order update and a proof, and 3) Check the status of the order in process. We had described how stopping the AI from paying out twice was only half the problem; the system also needed to know where the work had stopped. To that end, we’d like to introduce the concept of durable execution. For durable execution, the agent must do more than simply remember the actions it has taken. We need to keep track of the progress of the actions of the AI. The refund action was marked complete, but the email action was not sent yet. Thus, when the agent is restarted, it continues from where it left off rather than starting over. To create reliable AI agents, we need a few things: stable operation IDs, irrevocable actions, durable state, controlled retries, and logging. There are also cases where one wants to perform a recovery or compensating action (e.g., the agent makes a reservation but then fails to perform the subsequent action). The recovery action would be to cancel the reservation that had been made.

While controls with a person in the loop are mostly suitable for fine-grained actions that cannot be undone, idempotence ensures that an action is not accidentally executed more than once, and human approval is required to decide whether the action should be executed in the first place. This point is illustrated by an example: An employee correctly approves a $10,000 refund. However, the payment request somehow times out and is automatically re-executed several times. Without adequate idempotency security, the customer might end up with even more refunds than intended. Note, however, that a person’s approval cannot prevent double killing in such high-risk agent processes. Therefore, additional decision controls and corresponding execution controls are usually required to ensure that all actions are executed safely and correctly.

There are 5 simple questions that need to be answered before allowing an AI agent to take real-world actions.

Improving the model alone will not make the agent reliable unless we can answer all of these questions.

Much work in AI today focuses on making agents smarter, better at planning, and better to use as tools. But running agents in production has its own problems. API calls time out, processes crash, networks fail, and responses get lost, and the smartest agent can try again and again until it works. These are not new problems. Engineers working on distributed systems have been struggling with these same issues for decades. So long as the AI agent is confined to a simple test environment, of course it can query payment systems, customer information, and corporate tools. But when it is turned loose on the production corporation’s infrastructure (with all the payment systems, customer information, and corporate tools that entails), it must also be reliable. A smart model can figure out that a customer is due a refund. But a strong system can only give that customer a refund once. So the important question to ask of an AI agent before turning loose on it is, "Some powerful new tool: what happens if the action worked?" The answer will tell you whether or not the AI agent is currently able to operate reliably in production.

#10685. A tiny light trap could unlock million qubit quantum computers

After years of slow progress, researchers may finally be seeing a clear path forward in the quest to build powerful quantum computers. These machines are expected to dramatically shorten the time required for certain calculations, turning problems that would take classical computers thousands of years into tasks that could be completed in hours.

A team led by physicists at Stanford University has developed a new kind of optical cavity that can efficiently capture single photons, the basic particles of light, emitted by individual atoms. Those atoms serve as the core components of a quantum computer because they store qubits, which are the quantum equivalent of the zeros and ones used in traditional computing. For the first time, this approach allows information to be collected from all qubits at once.

Optical Cavities Enable Faster Qubit Readout

In research published in Nature, the team describes a system made up of 40 optical cavities, each holding a single atom qubit, along with a larger prototype that contains more than 500 cavities. The results point to a realistic route toward building quantum computing networks that could one day include as many as a million qubits.

"If we want to make a quantum computer, we need to be able to read information out of the quantum bits very quickly," said Jon Simon, the study's senior author and associate professor of physics and of applied physics in Stanford's School of Humanities and Sciences. "Until now, there hasn't been a practical way to do that at scale because atoms just don't emit light fast enough, and on top of that, they spew it out in all directions. An optical cavity can efficiently guide emitted light toward a particular direction, and now we've found a way to equip each atom in a quantum computer within its own individual cavity."

An optical cavity works by trapping light between two or more reflective surfaces, causing it to bounce back and forth. The effect can be compared to standing between mirrors in a fun house, where reflections seem to stretch endlessly into the distance. In scientific settings, these cavities are far smaller and use repeated passes of a laser beam to extract information from atoms.

Although optical cavities have been studied for decades, they have been difficult to use with atoms because atoms are extremely small and nearly transparent. Getting light to interact with them strongly enough has been a persistent challenge.

Rather than relying on many repeated reflections, the Stanford team introduced microlenses inside each cavity to tightly focus light onto a single atom. Even with fewer light bounces, this method proved more effective at pulling quantum information from the atom.

"We have developed a new type of cavity architecture; it's not just two mirrors anymore," said Adam Shaw, a Stanford Science Fellow and first author on the study. "We hope this will enable us to build dramatically faster, distributed quantum computers that can talk to each other with much faster data rates."

Beyond the Binary Limits of Classical Computing

Conventional computers process information using bits that represent either zero or one. Quantum computers operate using qubits, which are based on the quantum states of tiny particles. A qubit can represent zero, one, or both states at the same time, allowing quantum systems to handle certain calculations far more efficiently than classical machines.

"A classical computer has to churn through possibilities one by one, looking for the correct answer," said Simon. "But a quantum computer acts like noise-canceling headphones that compare combinations of answers, amplifying the right ones while muffling the wrong ones."

Scientists estimate that quantum computers will need millions of qubits to outperform today's most powerful supercomputers. According to Simon, reaching that level will likely require connecting many quantum computers into large networks. The parallel light-based interface demonstrated in this study provides an efficient foundation for scaling up to those sizes.

The researchers showed a working 40-cavity array in the current study, along with a proof-of-concept system containing more than 500 cavities. Their next goal is to expand to tens of thousands. Looking further ahead, the team envisions quantum data centers in which individual quantum computers are linked through cavity-based network interfaces to form full-scale quantum supercomputers.

Broader Scientific and Technological Impact

Significant engineering hurdles remain, but the researchers believe the potential benefits are substantial. Large-scale quantum computers could lead to breakthroughs in materials design and chemical synthesis, including applications related to drug discovery, as well as advances in code breaking.

The ability to efficiently collect light also has implications beyond computing. Cavity arrays could improve biosensing and microscopy, supporting progress in medical and biological research. Quantum networks may even contribute to astronomy by enabling optical telescopes with enhanced resolution, potentially allowing scientists to directly observe planets orbiting stars beyond our solar system.

"As we understand more about how to manipulate light at a single particle level, I think it will transform our ability to see the world," Shaw said.

​​Simon is also the Joan Reinhart Professor of Physics & Applied Physics. Shaw is also a Felix Bloch Fellow and an Urbanek-Chodorow Fellow.

Additional Stanford co-authors include David Schuster, the Joan Reinhart Professor of Applied Physics, and doctoral students Anna Soper, Danial Shadmany, and Da-Yeon Koh.

Other co-authors include researchers from Stony Brook University, the University of Chicago, Harvard University, and Montana State University.

This research received support from the National Science Foundation, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, Army Research Office, Hertz Foundation, and the U.S. Department of Defense.

Matt Jaffe of Montana State University and Simon act as consultants to and hold stock options in Atom Computing. Shadmany, Jaffe, Schuster, and Simon, as well as Aishwarya Kumar of Stony Brook, hold a patent on the resonator geometry demonstrated in this work.

#10682. Scientists create smart synthetic skin that can hide images and change shape

Synthetic materials are widely used across science, engineering, and industry, but most are designed to perform only a narrow range of tasks. A research team at Penn State set out to change that. Led by Hongtao Sun, assistant professor of industrial and manufacturing engineering (IME), the group developed a new fabrication technique that can produce multifunctional "smart synthetic skin." These adaptable materials can be programmed to perform a wide variety of tasks, including hiding or revealing information, enabling adaptive camouflage, and supporting soft robotic systems.

Using this new approach, the researchers created a programmable smart skin made from hydrogel, a soft, water-rich material. Unlike conventional synthetic materials with fixed behaviors, this smart skin can be tuned to respond in multiple ways. Its appearance, mechanical behavior, surface texture, and ability to change shape can all be adjusted when the material is exposed to external triggers such as heat, solvents, or physical stress.

The findings were published in Nature Communications, where the study was also selected for Editors' Highlights.

Inspired by Octopus Skin and Living Systems

Sun, the project's principal investigator, said the concept was inspired by cephalopods such as octopuses, which can rapidly alter the look and texture of their skin. These animals use such changes to blend into their surroundings or communicate with one another.

"Cephalopods use a complex system of muscles and nerves to exhibit dynamic control over the appearance and texture of their skin," Sun said. "Inspired by these soft organisms, we developed a 4D-printing system to capture that idea in a synthetic, soft material."

Sun also holds affiliations in biomedical engineering, material science and engineering, and the Materials Research Institute at Penn State. He described the process as 4D printing because the printed objects are not static. Instead, they can actively change in response to environmental conditions.

Printing Digital Instructions Into Material

To achieve this adaptability, the team used a method called halftone-encoded printing. This technique converts image or texture data into binary ones and zeros and embeds that information directly into the material. The approach is similar to how dot patterns are used in newspapers or photographs to create images.

By encoding these digital patterns within the hydrogel, the researchers can program how the smart skin reacts to different stimuli. The printed patterns determine how various regions of the material respond. Some areas may swell, shrink, or soften more than others when exposed to temperature changes, liquids, or mechanical forces. By carefully designing these patterns, the team can control the material's overall behavior.

"In simple terms, we're printing instructions into the material," Sun explained. "Those instructions tell the skin how to react when something changes around it."

One of the most eye-catching demonstrations involved the material's ability to conceal and reveal visual information. Haoqing Yang, a doctoral candidate in IME and the paper's first author, said this capability highlights the potential of the smart skin.

To demonstrate the effect, the team encoded an image of the Mona Lisa into the hydrogel film. When the material was washed with ethanol, it appeared transparent and showed no visible image. The hidden image became clear only after the film was placed in ice water or gradually heated.

Yang noted that the Mona Lisa was used only as an example. The printing technique allows virtually any image to be encoded into the hydrogel.

"This behavior could be used for camouflage, where a surface blends into its environment, or for information encryption, where messages are hidden and only revealed under specific conditions," Yang said.

The researchers also showed that concealed patterns could be detected by gently stretching the material and analyzing how it deforms using digital image correlation analysis. This means information can be revealed not only visually, but also through mechanical interaction, adding an extra level of security.

The smart skin also demonstrated remarkable flexibility. According to Sun, the material can easily shift from a flat sheet into complex, bio-inspired shapes with detailed surface textures. Unlike many other shape-changing materials, this transformation does not require multiple layers or different substances.

Instead, the changes in shape and texture are controlled entirely by the digitally printed halftone patterns within a single sheet. This allows the material to replicate effects similar to those seen in cephalopod skin.

Building on this capability, the team showed that multiple functions can be programmed to work together. By carefully designing the halftone patterns, they encoded the Mona Lisa image into flat films that later transformed into three-dimensional forms. As the sheets curved into dome-like shapes, the hidden image slowly appeared, showing that changes in shape and visual appearance can be coordinated within one material.

"Similar to how cephalopods coordinate body shape and skin patterning, the synthetic smart skin can simultaneously control what it looks like and how it deforms, all within a single, soft material," Sun said.

Expanding the Potential of 4D-Printed Hydrogels

Sun said the new work builds on earlier research by the team on 4D-printed smart hydrogels, which was also published in Nature Communications. That earlier study focused on combining mechanical properties with programmable transitions from flat to three-dimensional forms. In the current research, the team expanded the approach by using halftone-encoded 4D printing to integrate even more functions into a single hydrogel film.

Looking ahead, the researchers aim to create a scalable and versatile platform that allows precise digital encoding of multiple functions within one adaptive material.

"This interdisciplinary research at the intersection of advanced manufacturing, intelligent materials and mechanics opens new opportunities with broad implications for stimulus-responsive systems, biomimetic engineering, advanced encryption technologies, biomedical devices and more," Sun said.

The study also included Penn State co-authors Haotian Li and Juchen Zhang, both doctoral candidates in IME, and Tengxiao Liu, a lecturer in biomedical engineering. H. Jerry Qi, professor of mechanical engineering at Georgia Institute of Technology, also collaborated on the project.

That doesn’t mean Blizzard has completely abandoned the idea, though.

#10676. AI reads brain MRIs in seconds and flags emergencies

A newly developed artificial intelligence system from the University of Michigan can analyze brain MRI scans and deliver a diagnosis in a matter of seconds, according to a new study. The model identified neurological conditions with accuracy reaching 97.5% and was also able to assess how urgently patients needed medical care.

Researchers say this first-of-its-kind technology has the potential to reshape how brain imaging is handled across health systems in the United States. The findings were published in Nature Biomedical Engineering.

"As the global demand for MRI rises and places significant strain our physicians and health systems, our AI model has potential to reduce burden by improving diagnosis and treatment with fast, accurate information," said senior author Todd Hollon, M.D., a neurosurgeon at University of Michigan Health and assistant professor of neurosurgery at U-M Medical School.

Hollon named the new technology Prima. Over a one-year period, his research team evaluated the system using more than 30,000 MRI studies.

Across more than 50 different radiologic diagnoses involving major neurological disorders, Prima delivered stronger diagnostic performance than other advanced AI models. In addition to identifying disease, the system also proved capable of determining which cases required higher priority.

Certain neurological conditions, including strokes and brain hemorrhages, demand immediate medical attention. Hollon said that in these situations, Prima can automatically alert health care providers so action can be taken quickly.

The system was designed to notify the most appropriate subspecialist, such as a stroke neurologist or neurosurgeon. Feedback becomes available immediately after a patient completes imaging.

"Accuracy is paramount when reading a brain MRI, but quick turnaround times are critical for timely diagnosis and improved outcomes," said Yiwei Lyu, M.S., co-first author and postdoctoral fellow of Computer Science and Engineering at U-M.

"At key steps in the process, our results show how Prima can improve workflows and streamline clinical care without abandoning accuracy."

Prima is classified as a vision language model (VLM), a type of artificial intelligence that can process images, video, and text together in real time. While artificial intelligence has been applied to MRI analysis before, researchers say Prima takes a different approach.

Earlier models were typically trained on carefully selected subsets of MRI data and designed to perform narrow tasks, such as identifying lesions or estimating dementia risk. Prima was trained on a much broader dataset.

Hollon's team used every available MRI collected since radiology records were digitized at University of Michigan Health. This included more than 200,000 MRI studies and 5.6 million imaging sequences. The model also incorporated patients' clinical histories and the reasons physicians ordered each imaging study.

"Prima works like a radiologist by integrating information regarding the patient's medical history and imaging data to produce a comprehensive understanding of their health," said co-first author Samir Harake, a data scientist in Hollon's Machine Learning in Neurosurgery Lab.

"This enables better performance across a broad range of prediction tasks."

Addressing MRI Delays and Radiology Shortages

Each year, millions of MRI scans are performed worldwide, many of them focused on neurological disease. Researchers say the demand for these scans is growing faster than the availability of neuroradiology services.

This imbalance has contributed to staffing shortages, diagnostic delays, and errors. Depending on where a patient receives a scan, results may take days or even longer to return.

"Whether you are receiving a scan at a larger health system that is facing increasing volume or a rural hospital with limited resources, innovative technologies are needed to improve access to radiology services," said Vikas Gulani, M.D. Ph.D., co-author and chair of the Department of Radiology at U-M Health.

"Our teams at University of Michigan have collaborated to develop a cutting-edge solution to this problem with tremendous, scalable potential."

Although Prima performed strongly, researchers emphasize that the work is still in an early evaluation phase. Future research will focus on incorporating more detailed patient information and electronic medical record data to further improve diagnostic accuracy.

This approach mirrors how radiologists and physicians interpret MRIs and other imaging studies in real clinical settings. While artificial intelligence is already used in health care, most existing systems are limited to narrowly defined tasks.

Hollon describes Prima as "ChatGPT for medical imaging," noting that similar technology could eventually be adapted for other imaging types, including mammograms, chest X-rays and ultrasounds.

"Like the way AI tools can help draft an email or provide recommendations, Prima aims to be a co-pilot for interpreting medical imaging studies," Hollon said.

"We believe that Prima exemplifies the transformative potential of integrating health systems and AI-driven models to improve health care through innovation."

Additional authors: Asadur Chowdury, M.S., Soumyanil Banerjee, M.S., Rachel Gologorsky, Shixuan Liu, Anna-Katharina Meissner, M.D., Akshay Rao, Chenhui Zhao, Akhil Kondepudi, Cheng Jiang, Xinhai Hou, Rushikesh S. Joshi, M.D., Volker Neuschmelting, M.D., Ashok Srinivasan, M.D., Dawn Kleindorfer, M.D., Brian Athey, Ph.D., Aditya Pandey, M.D., and Honglak Lee, Ph.D., all of University of Michigan.

Funding/disclosures: This work was supported in part by the National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke (K12NS080223) of the National Institutes of Health.

The content is solely the responsibility of the authors and does not necessarily represent the official views of the NIH.

This work was also supported by the Chan Zuckerberg Initiative (CZI), Frankel Institute for Heart and Brain Health, the Mark Trauner Brain Research Fund, the Zenkel Family Foundation, Ian's Friends Foundation and the UM Precision Health Investigators Awards grant program.

#10675. Web-Ready AI Images: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI image generation has become a normal part of many web projects. However, the image a model produces is rarely the image a browser should receive. A single large PNG straight from a generator can weigh several megabytes, ignore the visitor's screen size, and slow down your Largest Contentful Paint (LCP) before the page has a chance to impress anyone.

The good news is that the fix is not complicated. If you treat the generated image as a high-quality master file and run it through a small, repeatable pipeline, you can ship images that look sharp on every device and load quickly on slow connections.

This tutorial walks through that pipeline from start to finish: generating at the right size and aspect ratio, choosing modern formats, creating responsive variants with Node.js, writing srcset and sizes correctly, and avoiding the performance mistakes that show up most often in audits.

Image generators are built to produce visually impressive results, not optimized web assets. Their output is usually a single file at one resolution, often in PNG format, and sometimes with an aspect ratio that does not match your layout.

Sending that file directly to the browser creates three problems. First, mobile visitors download far more pixels than their screens can display. Second, PNG is a poor choice for photographic images because its lossless compression produces large files. Third, without width and height information, the browser cannot reserve space for the image, which causes layout shifts as the page loads.

A web pipeline solves all three. You generate one master image, export multiple sizes in modern formats, and let the browser choose the best version for each visitor.

Optimization starts before you export anything. The most efficient pipeline begins with an image that already fits your layout.

Look at where the image will appear and note its aspect ratio at each breakpoint. A full-width hero might use 16:9 on desktop, while a blog thumbnail might use 4:3 or 1:1. Then identify the largest size the image will ever display. For a full-width hero, that could be 1920 pixels wide on large screens, or more if you support high-density displays at wide viewports.

Your master image should be at least as large as that maximum rendered width. Upscaling a small image later almost always introduces blurriness or artifacts.

Modern image-generation models can follow detailed instructions, so describe the technical requirements in your prompt, not just the subject. Specify the aspect ratio, intended display size, visual style, and any areas that need to remain clear for HTML text. The exact controls available will depend on the tool you use, so check its official documentation before building a workflow around a particular feature.

A practical web-focused prompt might read: "hero image of a modern home office at sunrise, soft natural light, calm blue and warm wood tones, clear empty space on the left third for headline text, no text or logos in the image."

That last instruction matters. Avoid baking headlines or buttons into images. Text inside an image cannot be translated, resized for accessibility, or indexed reliably, and it often becomes unreadable at small sizes. Keep text in HTML and use the image for visuals only.

Once you have a master file, decide which formats to serve. For most photographic and illustrative AI images, a combination of AVIF, WebP, and JPEG covers every modern browser while keeping file sizes small.

AVIF typically offers the strongest compression for photographic content and is supported by all major modern browsers, including Safari 16 and later. WebP is slightly less efficient but encodes faster and has near-universal support. JPEG remains a reliable fallback for older browsers and email clients.

Use PNG only when you specifically need lossless quality, such as for sharp-edged graphics or screenshots. Both AVIF and WebP support transparency, so you rarely need PNG for images with transparent backgrounds anymore.

Creating a dozen versions of every image by hand is tedious and error-prone. Instead, script the process. The Sharp documentation explains how to resize images and export them in different formats.

Then create a script that exports several widths in each format:

const widths = [480, 768, 1080, 1440, 1920];

{ ext: 'avif', format: 'avif', options: { quality: 50 } },

{ ext: 'webp', format: 'webp', options: { quality: 75 } },

{ ext: 'jpg', format: 'jpeg', options: { quality: 80, mozjpeg: true } },

for (const { ext, format, options } of formats) {

.resize({ width, withoutEnlargement: true })

Save the file as an ES module, such as build-images.mjs, and run it with Node. The withoutEnlargement option prevents sharp from upscaling the master when a target width is larger than the original.

The quality values above are reasonable starting points, not universal rules. AVIF and WebP quality scales do not map directly to JPEG quality, so compare outputs visually at the sizes your users will actually see. Smooth gradients, which are common in AI-generated skies and backgrounds, are especially prone to banding at aggressive settings.

The MDN responsive images guide explains how srcset and sizes work. The srcset attribute lists available image files and their intrinsic widths. The sizes attribute tells the browser how wide the image will be displayed in the layout. With both pieces of information, the browser can choose the smallest file that still looks sharp on the visitor's screen, including high-density displays.

Here is an example for an image inside an article column that is 720 pixels wide on larger screens and nearly full width on smaller ones:

alt="A modern home office at sunrise with a wooden desk and soft natural light"

The most common mistake is omitting sizes or leaving it at 100vw for images that are not full width. When that happens, the browser assumes the image fills the entire viewport and downloads a much larger file than necessary. Always match sizes to your actual CSS layout.

To offer AVIF and WebP with a JPEG fallback, wrap your image in a picture element. The browser checks each source in order and uses the first format it supports. See the MDN documentation for the picture element for the full syntax.

srcset="dist/hero-768.avif 768w, dist/hero-1440.avif 1440w, dist/hero-1920.avif 1920w"

srcset="dist/hero-768.webp 768w, dist/hero-1440.webp 1440w, dist/hero-1920.webp 1920w"

srcset="dist/hero-768.jpg 768w, dist/hero-1440.jpg 1440w, dist/hero-1920.jpg 1920w"

alt="A modern home office at sunrise with a wooden desk and soft natural light"

The img element is still required. It holds the fallback file, the dimensions, the alt text, and the loading attributes, while the source elements simply provide alternative formats.

If you need a different crop on mobile, such as a square version of a wide hero, add media attributes to your source elements. This technique is called art direction, and it is a good reason to generate or crop a separate master image for small screens rather than relying on CSS to hide important parts of a wide composition.

Always include width and height attributes on your img element. Modern browsers use them to calculate the image's aspect ratio and reserve the correct space before the file downloads, which helps prevent Cumulative Layout Shift. In CSS, set the image to width: 100% and height: auto so it scales responsively while keeping its proportions.

Use loading="lazy" for images below the fold, but never on your hero or other LCP image. Lazy loading your most important image delays it unnecessarily. Instead, add fetchpriority="high" to the LCP image so the browser downloads it early.

Write alt text that describes the image's purpose in context. If an image is purely decorative, use an empty alt attribute so screen readers skip it. Generated images do not come with meaningful descriptions, so this step always requires human judgment.

Use descriptive file names, such as home-office-sunrise-1440.avif, instead of generic names like image-final-v3.png. Clear names make assets easier to manage and give search engines a small amount of extra context.

Be aware of metadata. Some AI image providers, including OpenAI, embed C2PA provenance data that identifies an image as AI-generated. sharp strips most metadata by default, which reduces file size but also removes that provenance information. If transparency matters to your project or your client, recent versions of sharp offer a keepMetadata() method, and you can also disclose AI-generated imagery in captions or credits.

In Chrome DevTools, open the Network panel, filter by images, and resize the viewport or switch to device emulation. Reload the page and confirm that smaller files load on smaller screens. You can also select the image in the Elements panel and check its currentSrc property in the console to see which file the browser actually chose.

Then run Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. Look for warnings about properly sized images, modern image formats, and LCP.

Not always. AVIF often produces smaller files for photographic content, but it encodes more slowly. Serving both with a JPEG fallback is a safe default.

Four to six widths usually cover most layouts. Choose them based on your breakpoints and the largest rendered size of each image.

Review the current terms of service for the model and the platform you use. Licensing terms can differ between providers and plans.

AI image generation removes much of the cost and time of creating custom visuals, but it does not remove the need for good web engineering. Generate a high-quality master at the right aspect ratio, export responsive variants in modern formats, write accurate srcset and sizes values, protect your LCP and layout stability, and test everything before you ship.

Once the pipeline is scripted, every new image takes seconds to optimize. That combination gives you the creative speed of AI and the performance your visitors expect.

#10673. Brain inspired machines are better at math than expected

Computers designed to mimic the structure of the human brain are showing an unexpected strength. They can solve some of the demanding mathematical equations that lie at the heart of major scientific and engineering problems.

In a study published in Nature Machine Intelligence, Sandia National Laboratories computational neuroscientists Brad Theilman and Brad Aimone introduced a new algorithm that allows neuromorphic hardware to solve partial differential equations, or PDEs -- the mathematical foundation for modeling phenomena such as fluid dynamics, electromagnetic fields and structural mechanics.

The results demonstrate that neuromorphic systems can handle these equations efficiently. The advance could help open the door to the first neuromorphic supercomputer, offering a new path toward energy efficient computing for national security and other critical applications.

The research was funded by the Department of Energy's Office of Science through the Advanced Scientific Computing Research and Basic Energy Sciences programs, as well as the National Nuclear Security Administration's Advanced Simulation and Computing program.

Solving Partial Differential Equations With Brain Like Hardware

Partial differential equations are essential for simulating real world systems. They are used to forecast weather, analyze how materials respond to stress, and model complex physical processes. Traditionally, solving PDEs requires enormous computing power. Neuromorphic computers approach the problem differently by processing information in ways that resemble how the brain operates.

"We're just starting to have computational systems that can exhibit intelligent-like behavior. But they look nothing like the brain, and the amount of resources that they require is ridiculous, frankly," Theilman said.

For years, neuromorphic systems were mainly viewed as tools for pattern recognition or for speeding up artificial neural networks. Few expected them to manage mathematically rigorous problems such as PDEs, which are typically handled by large scale supercomputers.

Aimone and Theilman were not surprised by the outcome. They argue that the human brain routinely carries out highly complex calculations, even if people are unaware of it.

"Pick any sort of motor control task -- like hitting a tennis ball or swinging a bat at a baseball," Aimone said. "These are very sophisticated computations. They are exascale-level problems that our brains are capable of doing very cheaply."

Energy Efficient Computing for National Security

The findings could have major implications for the National Nuclear Security Administration, which is responsible for maintaining the nation's nuclear deterrent. Supercomputers used across the nuclear weapons complex consume vast amounts of electricity to simulate the physics of nuclear systems and other high stakes scenarios.

Neuromorphic computing may provide a way to significantly cut energy use while still delivering strong computational performance. By solving PDEs in a brain inspired manner, these systems suggest that large simulations could be run using far less power than conventional supercomputers require.

"You can solve real physics problems with brain-like computation," Aimone said. "That's something you wouldn't expect because people's intuition goes the opposite way. And in fact, that intuition is often wrong."

The team envisions neuromorphic supercomputers eventually becoming central to Sandia's mission of protecting national security.

What Neuromorphic Computing Reveals About the Brain

Beyond engineering advances, the research also touches on deeper questions about intelligence and how the brain performs calculations. The algorithm developed by Theilman and Aimone closely mirrors the structure and behavior of cortical networks.

"We based our circuit on a relatively well-known model in the computational neuroscience world," Theilman said. "We've shown the model has a natural but non-obvious link to PDEs, and that link hasn't been made until now -- 12 years after the model was introduced."

The researchers believe this work could help connect neuroscience with applied mathematics, offering new understanding of how the brain processes information.

"Diseases of the brain could be diseases of computation," Aimone said. "But we don't have a solid grasp on how the brain performs computations yet."

If that idea proves correct, neuromorphic computing might one day contribute to better understanding and treatment of neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's.

Building the Next Generation of Supercomputers

Neuromorphic computing remains an emerging field, but this work represents an important step forward. The Sandia team hopes their results will encourage collaboration among mathematicians, neuroscientists and engineers to expand what this technology can achieve.

"If we've already shown that we can import this relatively basic but fundamental applied math algorithm into neuromorphic -- is there a corresponding neuromorphic formulation for even more advanced applied math techniques?" Theilman said.

As development continues, the researchers are optimistic. "We have a foot in the door for understanding the scientific questions, but also we have something that solves a real problem," Theilman said.

#10671. Almost half of all new Steam games use AI now

Almost half of all games released on Steam in October 2026 disclosed the use of AI-generated content, according to a new analysis of more than 63,000 games released on the platform since January 2024.

Valve introduced mandatory AI disclosures in January 2024, requiring developers to declare certain uses of generative AI in their games.

At the time, just 1.7% of new releases carried an AI disclosure. That figure has since climbed to 49.9% in October 2026, almost double the 26.4% recorded during the same month last year.

The findings come from Reddit user u/ploz, who analyzed Steam’s publicly available data to track how frequently developers disclose AI-generated content.

According to the analysis, which covered approximately 63,600 games, the proportion of new Steam releases disclosing AI use has increased steadily over the past three years.

Across 2024, 13.2% of games disclosed AI-generated content, rising to 24% in 2025. So far in 2026, that figure has reached 40.1%, with October recording the highest monthly percentage in the dataset at 49.9%.

The analysis also found that AI disclosure is much more common for smaller games.

Of the titles released in 2026 with fewer than 10 Steam reviews, 47% disclosed AI use. Among games with at least 1,000 reviews, that figure dropped to just 14%.

Developers releasing lots of games were the most likely to disclose AI content. Those responsible for 10 or more releases in 2026 reported AI use in 68% of their games, compared to 37% among developers with just one release.

AI-generated artwork was the most common application, appearing in 63% of disclosures. Text and translations accounted for 30%, followed by store page and marketing materials at 26%, music at 21%, and code at 16%.

Steam predicted to make over $20 billion this year for first time ever

Steam makes major change to AI rules for games

Indie dev accused of using AI voice acting in Steam game reveals it was actually his wife

AI-generated voices appeared in just 7% of disclosures, while only 3% of games reported using AI to generate content during gameplay.

In October, one popular game had to take out a loan to keep the free demo running, because its AI bill was climbing so high.

It’s also not possible to say that this is all the games using AI, as Steam’s disclosures rely on developers reporting it themselves, so undisclosed AI content is not included.

Valve also changed its disclosure requirements in January 2026 to focus on AI-generated content players can see or hear, rather than tools used behind the scenes during development.

#10670. Quantum computer breakthrough tracks qubit fluctuations in real time

Researchers at the Niels Bohr Institute have significantly increased how quickly changes in delicate quantum states can be detected inside a qubit. By combining commercially available hardware with new adaptive measurement techniques, the team can now observe rapid shifts in qubit behavior that were previously impossible to see.

Qubits are the fundamental units of quantum computers, which scientists hope will one day outperform today's most powerful machines. But qubits are extremely sensitive. The materials used to build them often contain tiny defects that scientists still do not fully understand. These microscopic imperfections can shift position hundreds of times per second. As they move, they alter how quickly a qubit loses energy and with it valuable quantum information.

Until recently, standard testing methods took up to a minute to measure qubit performance. That was far too slow to capture these rapid fluctuations. Instead, researchers could only determine an average energy loss rate, masking the true and often unstable behavior of the qubit.

It is somewhat like asking a strong workhorse to pull a plow while obstacles constantly appear in its path faster than anyone can react. The animal may be capable, but unpredictable disruptions make the job much harder.

A research team from the Niels Bohr Institute's Center for Quantum Devices and the Novo Nordisk Foundation Quantum Computing Programme, led by postdoctoral researcher Dr. Fabrizio Berritta, developed a real time adaptive measurement system that tracks changes in the qubit energy loss (relaxation) rate as they occur. The project involved collaboration with scientists from the Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leiden University, and Chalmers University.

The new approach relies on a fast classical controller that updates its estimate of a qubit's relaxation rate within milliseconds. This matches the natural speed of the fluctuations themselves, rather than lagging seconds or minutes behind as older methods did.

To achieve this, the team used a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a type of classical processor designed for extremely rapid operations. By running the experiment directly on the FPGA, they could quickly generate a "best guess" of how fast the qubit was losing energy using only a few measurements. This eliminated the need for slower data transfers to a conventional computer.

Programming FPGAs for such specialized tasks can be challenging. Even so, the researchers succeeded in updating the controller's internal Bayesian model after every single qubit measurement. That allowed the system to continually refine its understanding of the qubit's condition in real time.

As a result, the controller now keeps pace with the qubit's changing environment. Measurements and adjustments happen on nearly the same timescale as the fluctuations themselves, making the system roughly one hundred times faster than previously demonstrated.

The work also revealed something new. Scientists did not previously know just how quickly fluctuations occur in superconducting qubits. These experiments have now provided that insight.

Commercial Quantum Hardware Meets Advanced Control

FPGAs have long been used in other scientific and engineering fields. In this case, the researchers used a commercially available FPGA based controller from Quantum Machines called the OPX1000. The system can be programmed in a language similar to Python, which many physicists already use, making it more accessible to research groups worldwide.

The integration of this controller with advanced quantum hardware was made possible through close collaboration between the Niels Bohr Institute research group led by Associate Professor Morten Kjaergaard and Chalmers University, where the quantum processing unit was designed and fabricated. "The controller enables very tight integration between logic, measurements and feedforward: these components made our experiment possible," says Morten Kjærgaard.

Why Real Time Calibration Matters for Quantum Computers

Quantum technologies promise powerful new capabilities, though practical large scale quantum computers are still under development. Progress often comes incrementally, but occasionally major steps forward occur.

By uncovering these previously hidden dynamics, the findings reshape how scientists think about testing and calibrating superconducting quantum processors. With current materials and manufacturing methods, moving toward real time monitoring and adjustment appears essential for improving reliability. The results also highlight the importance of partnerships between academic research and industry, along with creative uses of available technology.

"Nowadays, in quantum processing units in general, the overall performance is not determined by the best qubits, but by the worst ones: those are the ones we need to focus on. The surprise from our work is that a 'good' qubit can turn into a 'bad' one in fractions of a second, rather than minutes or hours.

"With our algorithm, the fast control hardware can pinpoint which qubit is 'good' or 'bad' basically in real time. We can also gather useful statistics on the 'bad` qubits in seconds instead of hours or days.

"We still cannot explain a large fraction of the fluctuations we observe. Understanding and controlling the physics behind such fluctuations in qubit properties will be necessary for scaling quantum processors to a useful size," Fabrizio says.

Materials provided by University of Copenhagen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
