#10669. Butterball unveils free Thanksgiving gadget that makes guests do chores

Butterball has unveiled a new Thanksgiving gadget designed to get dinner guests involved in holiday preparations by assigning them chores throughout the day.

The turkey company is giving away the Butterball Buddy, a turkey-shaped device that helps hosts delegate tasks instead of handling everything themselves.

The limited-edition gadget arrives ahead of Thanksgiving 2026, with Butterball planning three separate giveaways throughout November.

The Butterball Buddy is designed to make assigning Thanksgiving chores easier by giving guests tasks to complete during holiday preparations.

Rather than leaving all the cooking, cleaning, and other responsibilities to the host, the device encourages everyone to contribute.

The gadget features a turkey-inspired design and takes a playful approach to managing the work involved in preparing Thanksgiving dinner.

Guests pass the Butterball Buddy around during the gathering. When it chimes and vibrates, the person holding it receives a preprogrammed task, such as setting the table, helping prepare food, or washing dishes.

Once the task is complete, the guest passes the device to someone else, allowing chores to be distributed before, during, and after the meal.

The concept was inspired by Butterball’s 2026 Togetherness Report, which found that 85% of Thanksgiving hosts would prefer guests to offer help, but only 38% actually ask them to pitch in.

According to the company, one-third of hosts hesitate to ask for assistance because they don’t want to inconvenience their guests.

The Butterball Buddy also has a second use. After Thanksgiving, it can function as a kitchen timer, while the back of the device includes contact information for Butterball’s Turkey Talk-Line.

Most teams set up AI visibility tracking for ChatGPT, add Google, and stop there. Claude tends to come up later, usually when someone notices that half the engineers, analysts and founders they know use it every day. That instinct is sound. Claude's audience leans towards the people who research software and services before they buy.

The numbers back it up. Similarweb put Claude at 3.3% of AI chatbot web visits in February 2026 and 9.2% in May 2026, close to three times the share in three months, while ChatGPT's share kept falling. Those are web visits only. Usage in the Claude apps, in Claude Code and through the API does not show up in that figure, so 9.2% is a floor.

This guide compares six tools that track Claude. Peec AI comes first because it connects Claude answers to the pages behind them. The other five cover different budgets and needs.

A Claude tracking tool sends a fixed set of prompts to Claude on a schedule and records what comes back: whether your brand is named, where in the answer, how it is described, and which sources the answer relied on. Run daily, those records become trends you can compare against competitors.

It is not a rank tracker. Claude gives no list of ten blue links, so the useful numbers are mention rate, position inside the answer, sentiment and cited sources.

How the tool queries Claude. Some tools send your prompts to Anthropic's API. Others drive the Claude app. The two can give different answers, because the app has its own web search behaviour and the API, queried cold, often does not. Neither is wrong. Your buyers use the app, and the API is cheaper to run at scale. What matters is that you know which one you are looking at and that it stays the same over time.

Which plan includes Claude. Claude is a premium engine for several vendors. It can sit on the entry plan, on a higher tier or on Enterprise only, and a free tier often leaves it out.

What it costs per run. Credit-based tools can charge more for Claude than for other engines. Check the credit formula before you size a plan.

Source data. A mention rate says whether Claude names you. Source data says why it does not. Pick a tool that shows the pages Claude read.

Prompt volume. 50 to 150 prompts per brand is a realistic start. Fewer than 15 is mostly noise. Check how many prompts the cheaper tiers cover with Claude specifically: on credit-based tools Claude costs more per run, so the entry plan can cover far fewer Claude prompts than the headline number suggests.

Peec AI is a Berlin-based AI search analytics platform that tracks visibility, position, sentiment and share of voice across AI engines, then shows which sources drive those answers. Claude is part of the Enterprise plan and the agency Scale plan, tracked next to ChatGPT, Gemini, Perplexity, Microsoft Copilot and both Google surfaces. That makes it the pick for teams that want Claude as one engine in a wider view rather than as a stand-alone report.

Best for: Teams that track Claude together with ChatGPT and Google and want to know which pages to work on, not only where they stand.

Scrunch AI tracks Claude on its Enterprise plan, alongside nine engines in total. The published Core plan at $250 a month covers four engines and leaves Claude out, so it suits teams that start with ChatGPT, Perplexity, Google and Copilot and add Claude when they move to a quote.

Best for: Teams that want a published entry price now and add Claude through an Enterprise contract later.

Rankscale puts Claude on every paid plan from $99 a month and covers the widest engine list at that price: Claude, Copilot, ChatGPT, Perplexity, Gemini, both Google surfaces, DeepSeek and Mistral. It is credit-based, and the formula is published, which matters because Claude costs 2 credits per run, more than most engines. On Pro, 1,200 credits cover about 20 prompts run daily on Claude alone.

Best for: Small teams and agencies that want Claude and a long engine list for the least money.

AthenaHQ was built by two engineers who worked on Google Search and DeepMind. Claude is included from the Starter plan, among eleven engines. The free Essential tier does not include Claude, so it shows you the other engines first. Its optimisation agent drafts on-page and off-page recommendations, which suits teams that have the analysis but not the hands to act on it.

Best for: Teams short on people to write the follow-up work.

Profound is the shortest path for companies where an information security review decides the purchase. It lists SOC 2, SSO and SAML on Enterprise and raised a $96M Series C at a $1B valuation in February 2026. Claude is part of the Enterprise engine list, which runs to up to nine engines.

Best for: Enterprises that need a compliance posture on paper before they can buy.

Nightwatch is worth a look if you want Claude mentions next to the keyword rankings you already track and you are not paying for one of the large SEO suites. AI tracking is included in the plan price.

Best for: SEO teams that want one dashboard for classic rankings and Claude.

If Claude is one engine among several and you need to know what to fix, choose the tool with source analysis. If Claude is the only new engine and budget decides, Rankscale and Nightwatch cost the least. If you have a security review to pass, start with Profound or Peec AI's Enterprise plan. If you have no Claude coverage at all and cannot add a vendor, a few tools let you connect your own Anthropic API key, and Anthropic then bills you directly for the runs.

Claude is now a real share of the AI answers your buyers read, and its answers often differ from ChatGPT's. A single-engine number gives you false confidence in either direction. Peec AI is the strongest choice for teams that want Claude as part of a full view of sources, gaps and actions, with the caveat that Claude requires the Enterprise plan or, for agencies, the Scale plan. For the cheapest route to Claude coverage, Rankscale and Nightwatch are the practical options, though Rankscale's entry plan covers only about 20 daily Claude prompts, against 50 AI prompts on Nightwatch's entry plan. Whichever you choose, ask how the tool queries Claude and keep that method fixed. A tool measures. What moves the number is being on the pages Claude reads, and the best Claude tracking tools show you which ones those are.

No. If you can add one engine after ChatGPT, add Gemini, because its share is about three times larger. Add Claude third, and earlier if your buyers are technical.

Does Claude cite the same sources as ChatGPT?

Often not, and that is the practical reason to track it. Being named in one engine tells you little about the other.

With some tools, yes. Gauge lets you connect your own key on any plan, and Anthropic bills you directly for the runs. It is a cheap way to add Claude if your main tool does not include it.

How do I turn Claude tracking results into something I can act on?

Look for source data, not only a mention rate. A mention rate says whether Claude names you, while source data shows which pages Claude relied on and where competitors are cited and you are not. Peec AI classifies every cited page, reports the gaps and ranks recommended actions from them, and AthenaHQ goes a step further by drafting follow-up content. Tools that stop at mention counts tell you where you stand but not what to change.

#10664. ChatGPT as a therapist? New study reveals serious ethical risks

As more people seek mental health advice from ChatGPT and other large language models (LLMs), new research suggests these AI chatbots may not be ready for that role. The study found that even when instructed to use established psychotherapy approaches, the systems consistently fail to meet professional ethics standards set by organizations such as the American Psychological Association.

Researchers from Brown University, working closely with mental health professionals, identified repeated patterns of problematic behavior. In testing, chatbots mishandled crisis situations, gave responses that reinforced harmful beliefs about users or others, and used language that created the appearance of empathy without genuine understanding.

"In this work, we present a practitioner-informed framework of 15 ethical risks to demonstrate how LLM counselors violate ethical standards in mental health practice by mapping the model's behavior to specific ethical violations," the researchers wrote in their study. "We call on future work to create ethical, educational and legal standards for LLM counselors -- standards that are reflective of the quality and rigor of care required for human-facilitated psychotherapy."

The findings were presented at the AAAI/ACM Conference on Artificial Intelligence, Ethics and Society. The research team is affiliated with Brown's Center for Technological Responsibility, Reimagination and Redesign.

Zainab Iftikhar, a Ph.D. candidate in computer science at Brown who led the study, set out to examine whether carefully worded prompts could guide AI systems to behave more ethically in mental health settings. Prompts are written instructions designed to steer a model's output without retraining it or adding new data.

"Prompts are instructions that are given to the model to guide its behavior for achieving a specific task," Iftikhar said. "You don't change the underlying model or provide new data, but the prompt helps guide the model's output based on its pre-existing knowledge and learned patterns.

"For example, a user might prompt the model with: 'Act as a cognitive behavioral therapist to help me reframe my thoughts,' or 'Use principles of dialectical behavior therapy to assist me in understanding and managing my emotions.' While these models do not actually perform these therapeutic techniques like a human would, they rather use their learned patterns to generate responses that align with the concepts of CBT or DBT based on the input prompt provided."

People regularly share these prompt strategies on platforms like TikTok, Instagram, and Reddit. Beyond individual experimentation, many consumer facing mental health chatbots are built by applying therapy related prompts to general purpose LLMs. That makes it especially important to understand whether prompting alone can make AI counseling safer.

Testing AI Chatbots in Simulated Counseling

To evaluate the systems, the researchers observed seven trained peer counselors who had experience with cognitive behavioral therapy. These counselors conducted self counseling sessions with AI models prompted to act as CBT therapists. The models tested included versions of OpenAI's GPT Series, Anthropic's Claude, and Meta's Llama.

The team then selected simulated chats based on real human counseling conversations. Three licensed clinical psychologists reviewed those transcripts to flag possible ethical violations.

The analysis uncovered 15 distinct risks grouped into five broad categories:

The Accountability Gap in AI Mental Health

Iftikhar noted that human therapists can also make mistakes. The key difference is oversight.

"For human therapists, there are governing boards and mechanisms for providers to be held professionally liable for mistreatment and malpractice," Iftikhar said. "But when LLM counselors make these violations, there are no established regulatory frameworks."

The researchers emphasize that their findings do not suggest AI has no place in mental health care. Tools powered by artificial intelligence could help expand access, particularly for people who face high costs or limited availability of licensed professionals. However, the study highlights the need for clear safeguards, responsible deployment, and stronger regulatory structures before relying on these systems in high stakes situations.

For now, Iftikhar hopes the work encourages caution.

"If you're talking to a chatbot about mental health, these are some things that people should be looking out for," she said.

Ellie Pavlick, a Brown computer science professor who was not involved in the research, said the study underscores the importance of carefully examining AI systems used in sensitive areas like mental health. Pavlick leads ARIA, a National Science Foundation AI research institute at Brown focused on building trustworthy AI assistants.

"The reality of AI today is that it's far easier to build and deploy systems than to evaluate and understand them," Pavlick said. "This paper required a team of clinical experts and a study that lasted for more than a year in order to demonstrate these risks. Most work in AI today is evaluated using automatic metrics which, by design, are static and lack a human in the loop."

She added that the study could serve as a model for future research aimed at improving safety in AI mental health tools.

"There is a real opportunity for AI to play a role in combating the mental health crisis that our society is facing, but it's of the utmost importance that we take the time to really critique and evaluate our systems every step of the way to avoid doing more harm than good," Pavlick said. "This work offers a good example of what that can look like."

Materials provided by Brown University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10662. Valve’s canceled Portal prequel made playable by modders nearly 20 years later

A Portal prequel has been brought back to life nearly 20 years after Valve canceled it, thanks to fans who pieced together a playable version using leaked files.

Known as F-Stop, the game was originally developed in 2007 and set before the first Portal. Although Valve ultimately moved forward with Portal 2 instead, a series of leaks has now given players a chance to see what could have been.

According to Kotaku, members of the Valve Cut Content Discord community (which is currently invite-only) have managed to reconstruct F-Stop using a collection of leaked assets, source code, and map data.

The first pieces surfaced in August 2026, when a massive leak of approximately 12TB of Steam data exposed material from several Valve games, including early versions of Portal 2 and Left 4 Dead.

Following that, additional source code and map data reportedly appeared through the Valve Cut Content Discord server more recently, allowing fans to assemble a playable build.

Unlike Portal, F-Stop doesn’t involve creating portals to navigate environments. Instead, players use a camera to photograph objects before placing them elsewhere in the world to solve puzzles.

As reported by Engadget, the reconstructed version is still unfinished, with missing textures and dialogue. However, much of the campaign is playable, and footage showing the game in action has begun appearing on YouTube.

F-Stop dates back to Valve’s Directed Design Experiments in 2007, an initiative that saw developers split into teams to experiment with different gameplay concepts.

Valve ultimately opted to make Portal 2 instead, which was released in 2011.

#10661. What we've been playing - "Shoving Moon Flesh into an alien grandma's mouth"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Hello, it is me, your regular weekend host Mertie and I am here once again to guide you gently into Saturday with our usual feature What We've Been Playing, where we write a little about the games we've been, yes, playing. This week, Bertie - before fleeing for a well-deserved break and leaving me with the keys to the place - runs a one-on-one D&D game; Connor plays with his Moon Flesh in Warframe, and I've been serenely gallivanting over yonder hills.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago.

I'm a little busy outside of work at the moment with rehearsals for a play I'm in (The Importance of Being Ernest), but I did find time for another Dungeons & Dragons first: a one-on-one game. I've been wanting to do one of these for a while, and, as I discovered recently, one-on-one games are a whole thing. Duets, they're called, rather poetically. You need to modify campaigns a bit to be playable this way, because most are designed for groups of four or five people, and you probably need to give a player a companion character as backup. But otherwise, it's totally doable. Some people make adventures exclusively for one-on-one games.

I ran a mini-adventure called D8 Night, created by the now-closed dice shop Dakota Irish. It's a nice, warm two-hour-or-so affair, where the player is caught in a time-stilled village and tasked by some magical mischief maker to retrieve the components of a romantic meal before being allowed to leave. It's a simple enough set-up, and as with a lot of things D&D, the set-up doesn't really matter so much as the playing of it and the unexpected things that happen. And unexpected things did happen. I played with my partner so I know her likes pretty well, I like to think - that's the attraction of a one-on-one set-up like this - but I didn't expect her to connect so strongly with some of the incidental characters. This resulted in entire areas being imagined-to-life that weren't written on paper. The imagination never ceases to amaze me.

Anyway, I'm off to Comic Con this weekend to celebrate my birthday - it'll be my birthday when you're reading this. I'm off to geek-out about Critical Role and watch the new Laika animated film, and to stuff my face with food.

I had a lovely time playing developer Funselektor's Art of Rally follow-up Over the Hill last week, roaming its beautifully stylised wilderness expanses in my cheerful 4x4 so I could write some words about it all. But it clearly got its hooks into me, and I went on to spend pretty much my entire Saturday just pootling around the Canadian Valhallas, the Algerian desert, thoroughly transfixed by the tranquil rhythms of it all.

It really is a game for the curious - and the infinitely patient. As the old meme goes: See that mountain?, you can climb it. Just be prepared for a lot of slow-going, a lot of trial-and-error, and a lot of determination. But while it should be frustrating, the opposite somehow ends up being true, and it's easy to feel at peace in this striking wilderness, guitars strumming gentle encouragement as you become one with the world.

You haven't experienced FOMO until you've experienced MMO do-this-or-you'll-never-be-able-to-do-it-again FOMO: The once-in-a-lifetime grinds, the cosmetics that may be vaguely impressive to onlookers in a decade's time, and the first-thing-in-the-morning, last-thing-at-night routines of logging on and doing busywork.

For those unaware, Warframe is currently bracing for a journey into a whole new galaxy. The trip requires fuel, but given we're talking about the big-red-number-loving, fashion-forward, weirdly wonderful Warframe here, this fuel isn't diesel. No, Warframe players in their thousands are currently shoving Moon Flesh into an alien grandma's mouth.

We're taking parts of Eris - a grim petri dish home to countless infested ghouls - and, fouler still, the Mesa warframe. Like a ripe cheese, its stink is what meemaw craves, and providing it brings great rewards. These include weapons and dead-handy consumables like Legendary Cores and Archon Shards, but perhaps more delectable are the cosmetics. Some must be unlocked through a community-wide effort, while others are available to buy with Moon Flesh from the jump. And, of course, once the event ends, these will never return. Never ever.

I've been waking up at 6am everyday and doing missions for this Moon Flesh, and I've been doing it before bed as well. I, like many hopeless MMO/-live service players, am petrified that something in the game may never be mine to hoard. It has cut into my gym time, my morning coffee time, and indeed my sleep time. I owe my recent spree of unsubtle yawns in morning team meetings to Warframe. That's either a glowing endorsement, or a sign I need to ease up on this stuff. Who knows?

Am I allowed two entries? I'm not actually sure, but Bertie's not here to stop me so... ALSO, I have found myself consumed by Switch Sports Resort this week in a way I absolutely wasn't expecting to be when I fired it up for review. In my head, Nintendo's long-running series has always very much been a party game first, and an engaging solo endeavour a distant second.

But Switch Sports Resort is a bit of a treasure, and I've been having a great time with it on my own: taking it online for a bit of ranked or casual play, working through some achievements, or just firing up one of its fantastic open-world sightseeing modes for a leisurely explore. I hope Nintendo's got some post-launch plans in the works, because I'd love for it to go a little bit harder with the open-world stuff, particularly where flying is concerned. But until then there's skateboarding, which is bloody brilliant, and I'm already craving more.

#10660. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Kraft Mac & Cheese has introduced a new Cinnamon Roll flavor, combining its signature cheesy pasta with the warm spices of the popular pastry.

Kraft announced the limited-time release on November 2 alongside another new flavor, Chili Crisp, as it expands its lineup with combinations inspired by changing consumer preferences.

According to the company, more than 60% of its fans are interested in trying new and unexpected flavors beyond its traditional mac and cheese offerings.

The new Cinnamon Roll Mac & Cheese combines cinnamon with Kraft’s familiar cheese sauce to create what the company describes as a creamy, slightly tangy flavor.

Unlike Cinnamon Roll, the Chili Crisp variety will be sold at multiple retailers, including Meijer, Walmart, and Amazon, for a limited time.

#10656. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

Materials provided by Swansea University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10655. Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2 is more than a retread of Netflix's beloved first season - it's a story that truly understands what it means to exist in Night City

Last night, I sat down with a greasy pizza and a few bottles of beer and braced myself for 10 episodes of heartbreak. Cyberpunk: Edgerunners' second season is out, and it follows in the footsteps of Studio Trigger's beloved, and tragic, debut. My expectations were way off.

Not because it wasn't good: Edgerunners Season 2 is fantastic. Packed with a wonderful cast of troubled characters, their story brought to life with gorgeous and often unorthodox animation. The soundtrack, the voice acting... Quality-wise the bar is as high as the first season. What surprised me was the decision to bring something many may find peculiar given Cyberpunk's dire setting and desperate energy. The show is hopeful. Defiant, even.

In the show's opening episode, we are introduced to characters at rock bottom. King, having suffered from cyberpsychosis, has had his augmentations stripped out of his body, and lost his home. D's in the midst of a lonely journey of revenge, while Talia is rubbing elbows with the Maelstrom gang. Roman, a 12-year-old, is broke and struggling to pursue filmmaking in a world that's left the medium behind. In the pursuit of money, he is nearly diced up.

These characters have all the reason in the world to lose themselves to despair, to burn out. Some almost do. However, as the season progresses, they each find a reason to push on, to live in spite of the odds. By the end of the season, while their problems may not have vanished, those who can choose to push on regardless of what life may throw at them. There are more smiles and laughs in the final episode than there are in the first. In the world of Cyberpunk, that's surprising. Defiant.

Violence in Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2 is not treated as some awesome, cathartic spectacle. Through the literal lens of Roman, it's seen as the monstrous act it is. Characters are urged to not go out and kill people - even those who arguably deserve it - consistently throughout the show. In the first season, we saw the brutal reality of killing in David's descent into cyberpsychosis. In this season, characters without that particular affliction are confronted with the futility of the act.

Around halfway through the season, a cowering lady is half-heartedly executed by a side character, and the show lingers on the scene as her head slumps down onto the ground. It's a grisly moment, a siren blaring starkly, broadcasting a very simple message: violence is not the answer.

There is a sentiment that there are no good endings in Night City, and it's a fair belief to have. Magda Zych, senior writer at CD Projekt Red, described the game's story as a 'noir tragedy', and the dystopian world of the tabletop RPG presents a similarly sad motif. Crime is rampant, people are victimised for no reason, people are chewed up and spat out. This was true in Mike Pondsmith's original vision for Night City, it was true in Cyberpunk 2077, and it's true in Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2.

So there's courage in living as best you can in spite of the injustice of the world. If the first season of Cyberpunk: Edgerunners could be aligned, roughly, to 2077's 'The Sun' ending (where V charges into Arasaka in a blaze of glory), Season 2 can be seen as a parallel to 'The Star' ending, where V takes what time they have left and spends it with their friends and loved ones, divorced from the grinding reality of Night City's grinding concrete. Some NPCs react to this decision during the credits with scorn; it is the antithesis of the 'burn bright, die young' Edgerunner philosophy.

I imagine this season may not have met the expectations of many viewers, who are left cold by how different it is from the first season. And yeah, it would have been entirely acceptable to rehash the same sort of story we saw in 2022 again here, and many people - including myself - would have loved it. But to choose not to do that, to steer the narrative into a totally different direction, is smart. You might even say... defiant.

#10653. Warzone Season 1 removes Verdansk and Rebirth Island but they return with updates

Warzone is removing two of its most iconic maps, Verdansk and Rebirth Island, when Season 1 begins on November 6, but they won’t be gone for too long.

Both maps have been staples of the battle royale in recent years, with Verdansk offering large-scale matches and Rebirth Island providing a smaller, faster-paced alternative through Resurgence.

However, Activision has confirmed that neither will be available when the new season launches, as two brand-new locations take their place.

As confirmed in Activision’s official Season 1 announcement, Hajin from DMZ will replace Verdansk as Warzone’s main Battle Royale map, while Zodiac takes over from Rebirth Island for Resurgence.

Hajin is a massive map set across North and South Korea, featuring 13 major points of interest. Players will be able to explore crowded cities, farmland, and military facilities, with changing weather conditions adding another challenge.

It will also feature a brand-new Gulag, where eliminated players can fight for another chance to return to the match.

Meanwhile, Zodiac is a much smaller location built around a South Korean drone manufacturing facility, featuring 10 named locations. Its industrial setting includes underground tunnels, moving trains, and interactive hazards.

The good news is that the old maps aren’t disappearing permanently. Activision has confirmed that Verdansk and Rebirth Island will both return during Season 1, although an exact date hasn’t been announced.

When Verdansk comes back, it will feature new locations including the Signal Station, Grazna-7 Launch Complex, Overlook skyscraper, and Drone Labs. These POIs are said to bring everything from “frozen forests and industrial sprawl to dense city blocks” to the map.

#10652. Phantom Blade Zero review

I wasn't around to witness the golden age of Martial Arts cinema, but there are moments in Phantom Blade Zero that make me feel as though I've tumbled through a crack in time onto the set of a kung fu film. There's one particular encounter that sticks in my mind. Approaching a group of enemies in a shaded valley, I slowly raise my sword, and the first enemy rushes at me. Fool. I perfectly parry the blow, performing a slow-motion somersault to land behind him and plant my sword firmly in his back. The next guy comes running in, and I kick him in the face while simultaneously stabbing the previous enemy in the throat. A third joins the fray - this one on horseback - so I boot him off his horse, and use his steed to trample his helpless friend into the dust. Then, at last, there is silence.

This is Phantom Blade Zero at its absolute peak: cinematic, free-flowing, and full of surprises. Its combat channels the seamless motion and creative choreography of the very best kung fu films, with some frankly astonishing combat animations that seem almost limitless in their ingenuity and variety. It's a game of extraordinary beauty, with jaw-dropping landscapes and hugely ambitious combat encounters. If only - if only! - it could maintain this incredible level of fluidity in all areas of its epic tale.

Our adventure takes place in a fantastical, wuxia-flavoured world called the Wulin: a martial underworld where vicious ne'er-do-wells seem to be running riot, and generally making life miserable for regular people who are just trying to live their lives. Truly, a world miles apart from our own. You play as Soul, a warrior and outcast member of an organisation called The Order. Having played through the entirety of the game, I'm still not entirely sure what The Order stands for in terms of values, but I'm sure they originally meant well. I'm also not really sure what I can tell you about the story - we've been asked not to discuss anything that happens in the prologue - so you're all going to have to use your imaginations here. I can tell you it involves betrayal, a protagonist with limited days to live, a revenge mission, and various malevolent entities from another world. All the standard ingredients you need for a fantasy epic, really.

To warn you in advance, it doesn't take long for Phantom Blade Zero to start dumping an absolutely dizzying number of character names, bloodlines, cults and various feuding sects on you. The way the world lore is delivered - often through reams of text in collectible scrolls dotted around the Wulin - is definitely not one of Phantom Blade Zero's strengths. Most of the game's characters have much to say about nothing, offering various platitudes, truisms and declarations of values that are hard to connect with the underlying story. My advice is to just let everything wash over you, and you'll eventually pick up on the main plot points. While the narrative content leaves much to be desired, the storytelling does include some absolute visual treats - even utilising a traditional Chinese art form to do so. Unfortunately I can't actually praise this in full, because we were also asked not to discuss these elements in our reviews.

Anyway, I think we all know why we're here, and that's because the main draw of Phantom Blade Zero is its combat. The FromSoftware influence is evident in so many aspects of this game, from the series of 'bell' checkpoints scattered throughout the world, to the multi-stage bosses that, yes, can deliver one-shot attacks. Regardless of your weapon loadout, the most important thing you can do in a boss battle is correctly dodge red attacks, and parry blue ones. It's the single most effective way of breaking a boss's guard so that you can enter a punishment phase, and finally get your heavy-hitting moves in. It's kinda like high-octane Sekiro. So if you're not a fan of this dodge/parry combat style, this might not be the game for you.

Perhaps Soulslite would be the better term for Phantom Blade Zero, as there are a lot of clever adjustments here that allow for much speedier combat than other Soulslikes would permit. There isn't a traditional stamina bar, but instead a resource called 'sha-chi', which depletes when you block or parry enemy attacks and when you use heavy attacks. It's a nifty way to allow players to use quick attacks, sprints and dodges without costing stamina. Tied to this is Phantom Blade Zero's two-weapon system, which promotes regular switching between main weapons. If you're running low on sha-chi using one weapon, simply swap to the other and - ta dah! - you have a fresh sha-chi bar to work with (although there is a slight cooldown to prevent total abuse of this mechanic).

This is particularly useful when you want to get some heavy hits in, or if you need to prevent your guard from being broken, which happens when your sha-chi runs low. Another welcome adjustment is that the parry and dodge windows feel really quite generous, which is essential when enemies attacks are coming at you with such speed. If you need to do a last-minute parry, you can cancel nearly any of your moves to do so, which prevents that infuriating problem where you end up locked into an attack juuust as the boss raises their big meat cleaver right above your noggin. The greatest mercy of all is that if you're killed during the second phase of a boss fight, you can just re-enter the boss fight at the start of that second phase. It makes your failures slightly less heartbreaking, and gives you a chance to hop right back into the action with a full set of resources to play with.

Then there are the weapons themselves, which are incredibly flashy and give you access to some genuinely exciting abilities. One sword requires you to tank up on booze, then allows you to send the weapon flying around through the air to attack enemies at range - an enjoyably silly moveset which may not be the most effective build, but which is still fun to play nonetheless. Later you get pole-like swords that can charge through enemies, twin swords that rotate in orbit around you, and a weapon that can summon flowers out of the ground to drain an opponent's sha-chi. There's definitely a process of mastering these weapons and understanding how best to use their abilities in combat - particularly because each has its own unique combinations that are gradually unlocked as you find upgrade materials around the world. Eventually, you find quest items which allow you to 'soul temper' weapons to unlock their most powerful latent abilities, which can suddenly transform an underpowered weapon into something really quite viable (hello again, drunken sword). There's also a class of secondary weapons, known as phantom edges, which are intended for use as additional tools you can draw on in battle to solve problems like high boss guards or unblockable attacks. I constantly experimented with weapons to get a feel for them, and was able to fine-tune my build so that I had a loadout for dealing with large numbers of enemies, and another loadout for breaking boss guards. I got into a real rhythm with the combat, and knew exactly which order in which to pull my moves to maximise my damage.

Against regular enemies found around the Wulin, these weapons feel extraordinary. The combat achieves that perfect kung-fu flow state, and you can bounce around between enemies parrying their blows, lowering their guards, and opening them up for executions. I adore how generous Phantom Blade Zero is with these executions - hit a QTE at the right time, and you can take out two enemies at once. There also seems to be a never-ending supply of these animations: Soul will often use the enemy's own weapons against them, meaning you'll still be finding brand new execution moves 30 hours into the game. It makes the combat constantly surprising, giving variety to the encounters and encouraging you to be aggressive to unlock these deadly finishers. On the regular pathfinder difficulty, the normal enemies feel really well-balanced - you get that sense of flow, but it's absolutely still possible to mess things up if you go charging in without the right loadout, or without priorisiting ranged enemies first.

Against boss enemies, however, these once-powerful weapons are suddenly defanged. During my playthrough, a very high proportion of the bosses felt spongy - especially on pathfinder mode, but even if you lowered the difficulty to wayfarer, which is supposed to be the fun 'rampage through the game' difficulty setting. The weapons which felt so effective against other enemies suddenly became toothpicks against these bosses, even during their punishment phases. Successfully parrying or dodging was rewarded with a very minor dink to a boss's sha-chi, instantly giving you that sinking feeling that you're in for a very long, repetitive fight.

In a Soulslike game, low damage is normally a hint to start experimenting with your build or to try different weapons to see if you can find the 'key' to a boss fight, but Phantom Blade Zero is not very good at communicating exactly what these might be. As an example, one early obligatory boss fight sees you fight an enemy with a spiked shield, and attacking said enemy causes you to start bleeding. It's obvious that you need to break the shield, and that you need to use one of the game's secondary weapons to do so - but when you use the axe phantom edge, very little happens, and that lack of reactivity caused me to go back walking through the world thinking I'd missed something. Turns out I was on the right track, and just needed to keep spamming the move or do a 'charged' version of it (which the game doesn't exactly encourage, as the wind-ups for these charged phantom edge moves are so long that they're normally not viable in the middle of a fast-paced battle). In another case, a phantom edge which shoots water - and is specifically described as being effective against fire types - did absolutely nothing against a fire type enemy. I often didn't feel rewarded for testing these different phantom edges, and the sponginess of the bosses, particularly in the early to mid game when your build options are limited, made a lot of these battles feel artificially lengthy.

This is one of my main gripes with the game, but this is also where things get… interesting. Two days before the review embargo was due to lift, developer S-Game released a 67GB update targeting performance and bugs, along with boss, enemy, ability and weapon stats. "These changes aim to make the difficulty curve feel smoother, both in boss fights and as you progress through each area," we were told. Obviously, this did not give me time to replay the entire game to see if the changes made a difference to the overall experience. I did return to some old save files and found that the sponginess remained present in several boss fights - especially those early obligatory ones - so it appears to still be an issue. It's possible that the launch build will allow player parries, dodges and weapons to feel a little more effective against bosses - but alas, I can only write about the version of the game I've played.

Despite the variety of animations and move sets used by the bosses, a lot of these battles also seem to fall into a fairly strict defend/attack pattern where you don't have much opportunity to disrupt enemy movesets. But that's not to say all boss battles feel exactly the same. Those with multiple smaller enemies allow you to feel like you're doing more damage, while some of the most memorable battles break out of the standard pattern to bring in something totally different. One boss fires missiles horizontally like a bullet hell, while another sees you dance on top of poles while exchanging cannon fire with your enemy. The latter was absolutely fantastic: visually stunning, unexpected, and a moment of pure unadulterated fun.

Of course, an essential part of any Soulslike/lite is noodling around the world to find upgrades and blow off steam between boss fights, and praise should go to the exploration aspect of Phantom Blade Zero. The world map is formed of traditional hand-painted ink wash paintings, with mists that part to reveal new areas with each bell you discover. These beautiful maps only hint at what you'll encounter in each area: you have to find your way through by observing the landscape and spotting hidden pathways on foot. There are plenty of 'aha' moments where you finally find the way up to a distant chest on a cliff that you'd spotted a while back. The Metroidvania elements of the game similarly build anticipation for being able to return to an area, with the phantom edge weapons you unlock through the main story later giving you access to previously-locked areas. S-Game does occasionally overcook this by placing too many closed doors next to each other: on entering the game's main city, I encountered a cluster of seven locked areas within the first few streets, which was a bit dispiriting. No room at the inn for poor old Soul, it seems. My advice is to really take your time in your initial exploration of an area, and not rush to the next region until you're fairly certain you've explored as much as you can at that stage. It's hard to see from the world map what you might have missed - and because enemies don't respawn, retraversing through these levels later on can feel weirdly empty.

Phantom Blade Zero has some extraordinary highs, but a gradual build-up of niggles eventually starts to weigh on you during a playthrough. It's a death by a thousand cuts, of things that really should have been caught during play testing. There's an essential key that's easy to miss because it glows orange and is placed next to a fire of the same colour. It's easy to get caught on geometry, and you end up doing much of your fighting against walls when you push your enemies there with long-combination attack patterns. On several occasions bosses hopped outside their arenas to attack me from behind invisible walls - a sort of reverse cheese that I really didn't appreciate. Certain sidequest missions misdirected me on the map, leaving me going around in circles when it turns out an entirely unrelated quest needed to be completed first. I experienced some severe frame rate dips in certain areas of the game, too - not ideal for a fast-paced action game. These nuisances do stack up, and although S-Game said it has addressed the escaping bosses, performance issues and the quest guidance fails in its latest patch, it's hard to say conclusively whether all of these issues have been fully resolved. The frame rates do seem to be better since the update, but that annoying side quest loop is still in place.

On/off setting for motion blur and camera shake. Adjustable brightness options, two font size options. Independent sliders for music, effects and dialogue. Four difficulty settings. Control scheme screen easily available, full key and button remapping. Can invert Y/X axis. Adjustable controller vibration and sensitivity settings.

All of which leaves us with quite a tricky picture. This game is exceptional in so many areas - the animations, the beautiful environments - and when the combat works, the sense of flow is incredible. Street fights with multiple opponents feel so cinematic and so satisfying, allowing you to be truly creative and expressive with your fighting style. Then you walk into a boss battle and feel all that power fade, like you're waiting your turn to get hits in with a wooden sword. This slows the pace of the early to mid game in particular, and I wish S-Game had been prepared to speed us through to the real meat of its gameplay a little faster. Combined with the other smaller issues and niggles - plus the pretty unremarkable narrative and lore dumps - it just takes the shine off what is otherwise a very impressive action title. Who knows, maybe another patch or two will even things out. Because there are some incredibly fun weapons to play with here… if only they had been sharpened.

A copy of Phantom Blade Zero was provided for this review by S-Game.

#10650. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

Materials provided by Osaka Metropolitan University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10649. 8 Checks Developers Should Add Before Importing PDF Tables into an Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A PDF table can look correct and still produce unreliable application data. A wrapped description becomes two rows. An account code loses its leading zeros. A repeated page header is imported as a transaction. A missing decimal separator changes an amount without triggering an obvious error.

These problems are easy to overlook when an exported spreadsheet opens successfully. But opening a workbook proves very little about whether its contents belong in a database.

For developers building reporting dashboards, inventory systems, or document-import features, PDF extraction should feed a validation workflow. The following eight checks help separate readable output from data your application can safely process.

The examples use fictional invoice-style records and Python. They demonstrate a narrow validation contract rather than a complete PDF conversion service.

Not all PDFs contain the same kind of information.

A digitally generated document may include selectable text and vector lines. A scanned document may contain only page images. A mixed document can contain both, sometimes with an existing OCR layer that is incomplete or inaccurate.

Inspect representative pages before committing to an extraction strategy. Check whether text is selectable, whether copied Check parsing and validation behavior against fixtures

The pdfplumber documentation explains its text and table capabilities, including visual debugging. Its strengths are primarily associated with machine-generated PDFs; it is not an OCR engine.

OCRmyPDF adds a searchable text layer to scans. That does not, by itself, reconstruct a correct table schema. You still need to determine which text belongs in each row and column.

Practical check: Build a sample set containing a clean digital PDF, a scan, and a multi-page table before evaluating the extraction path.

A conversion step should create an inspectable intermediate result. It should not automatically authorize database writes.

For workflows that need a spreadsheet as the intermediate format, Lido is one option for converting PDF content to Excel. Evaluate the output against representative documents, then apply the same schema and reconciliation checks you would use with a custom extraction script.

Commit accepted records to the application.

This structure lets you replace an extraction tool without changing every downstream rule. It also provides a place to inspect failures before they become production records.

SitePoint’s guide to parsing spreadsheet data with Python provides background on working with spreadsheet formats in an application workflow.

Practical check: The importer should be able to reject a workbook even when conversion completed successfully.

Column names alone are not a sufficient contract. A field called “Date” could represent an invoice date, payment date, or service date.

Decide how repeated headers, subtotal rows, footnotes, and blank lines will be identified. Do not let them disappear through undocumented cleanup rules.

Load a workbook with deliberate type handling

The pandas read_excel reference documents these loading options. Using strings limits unwanted interpretation at this stage, while keep_default_na=False preserves values that would otherwise match default missing-value markers.

However, reading a cell as text cannot recover information already lost upstream. If a converter stored 00127 as the number 127, this setting will not reconstruct the original ID.

Practical check: Compare identifiers against the original document, including examples with leading zeros.

Removing every comma and currency symbol may look convenient, but it hides ambiguity. The strings follow different conventions.

Choose an explicit format per document family. The following parser accepts a deliberately narrow format: ASCII digits, optional correctly grouped thousands commas, a decimal point, and up to two decimal places. It permits a leading minus sign for adjustments.

This intentionally rejects parentheses, currency symbols, exponent notation, and comma-decimal formatting. Those formats may be valid in other documents, but supporting them requires an explicit rule.

Python’s Decimal documentation explains decimal arithmetic. Constructing a Decimal directly from the validated string avoids first introducing a binary floating-point representation.

Practical check: Keep the original extracted value alongside the normalized value, and reconcile the result against other evidence.

A hundred extracted rows are not necessarily the correct hundred rows. One line may be missing while another is duplicated.

Required fields are present and nonempty.

Dates are valid under the declared format.

Line identifiers are unique within the correct scope.

Recognized line-item totals reconcile with the appropriate subtotal.

Each accepted row has a traceable source location.

For a document whose stated subtotal is exactly the sum of its line amounts:

Do not compare that subtotal directly with a grand total containing tax, shipping, or other adjustments. First model those components.

Avoid a generic tolerance such as “accept differences below one dollar.” If the document requires rounding, define where rounding occurs, the currency precision, and the permitted discrepancy.

SitePoint’s overview of data preprocessing provides additional context for separating cleaning, transformation, and integration.

Practical check: Inject one missing row and one duplicate into your fixtures. Confirm that the workflow detects both.

When a record fails validation, a reviewer should not have to search a long PDF without context.

Keep a reference to the source document, page number, extraction run, and original field value. Where the extractor exposes cell coordinates, retain them so a review interface can highlight the relevant region.

Distinguish “not extracted,” “blank in source,” and “not applicable.” Turning all three into an empty string makes it harder to understand what happened.

Confidence scores can help prioritize work, but they are not interchangeable across tools and do not replace business validation. A highly confident extraction can still be placed in the wrong column.

Practical check: Ask someone unfamiliar with the document to resolve a flagged record using only the review interface and its source reference.

An import may stop after extraction, during validation, or while writing records. Retrying should not create another copy of every accepted line.

Assign each import a stable identity. A document hash can help identify byte-identical uploads, while an explicit tenant or account scope prevents unrelated users’ imports from being treated as one.

A hash alone is insufficient for every duplicate policy. Two differently encoded PDFs may represent the same invoice, and one file may legitimately need reprocessing after a parser correction.

Track both the document identity and processing version. Separate extraction attempts from committed business records.

At the database boundary, define the appropriate uniqueness rule and use a transaction for the unit you promise to import atomically. For example, invoice lines might be unique by account, invoice identity, and line number—not by invoice ID alone across all customers.

When reprocessing changes existing values, make that an explicit replacement or correction workflow rather than an unnoticed side effect of retrying.

Practical check: Run the same accepted import twice and verify that the application record count remains unchanged.

The most valuable fixtures are not always the most visually complicated. A tiny change can alter business meaning while producing valid-looking data.

Use pytest parameterization to exercise numeric-format boundarie. SitePoint’s Python unit-testing guide explains the broader testing workflow.

Parser tests do not verify the extraction engine. Add end-to-end fixtures with manually verified expected records for repeated headers, wrapped descriptions, scans, and multi-page tables.

Measure field accuracy and the number of documents that pass all required checks. A strong average can conceal a weak result on the one field your application depends on most.

Practical check: Retain a regression fixture for every meaningful production failure, using synthetic or appropriately sanitized documents.

PDF conversion produces candidate data. Your application decides whether that data is complete, consistent, and suitable for use.

A dependable workflow identifies the source format, preserves extraction evidence, enforces explicit column rules, reconciles values, and makes retries predictable.

Start with one document family and a small verified fixture set. Expand only when you can explain both why a record was accepted and how a reviewer can investigate one that was rejected.

#10648. 86-year-old man and his dog found safe two months after disappearing at sea

An 86-year-old sailor and his dog have been found alive more than two months after disappearing during a voyage across the Pacific Ocean.

The pair were rescued roughly 460 miles off the coast of San Diego, California, on October 28, after spending more than two months without contact with the outside world.

According to the US Coast Guard, the man had been sailing aboard his vessel, Willow Wind, from Astoria, Oregon, to Pago Pago, American Samoa, accompanied by his dog.

However, nobody had heard from him since August 20, and authorities were eventually notified that he was overdue on October 24.

Three days later, on October 27, rescuers finally caught a break when an emergency beacon registered to Willow Wind transmitted a distress signal.

A Coast Guard aircraft located the damaged sailboat later that evening, setting the stage for a dramatic rescue.

As reported by PEOPLE, the commercial vessel Grand Orion arrived at the scene around 3:20 AM on October 28, but rough sea conditions initially prevented its crew from reaching the stranded pair.

After more than 14 hours, rescuers finally managed to bring both the man and his dog aboard at approximately 5:36 PM.

The following morning, a Coast Guard helicopter hoisted the pair from the Grand Orion, which was then around 170 miles west of Ventura, California, and transported them to San Diego.

Despite the ordeal, the 86-year-old reportedly suffered no major injuries. However, he was described as weak and having difficulty walking before being taken to Scripps Mercy Hospital for further evaluation.

Fishermen survive 5 days stranded in Pacific Ocean using cooler as makeshift boat

“Goodest boy” Bruce the dog rescued after drifting miles out to sea in kayak

Hero dog leads police to owner after they were stuck on floor for hours

His dog’s condition was not detailed, but both survived the ordeal. Their damaged sailboat was left drifting at sea.

“We are grateful to the merchant mariners and Coast Guard crews whose coordinated efforts brought this search to a successful conclusion,” Coast Guard Commander Jason Nguyen said.

The remarkable survival story follows another dramatic Pacific Ocean rescue in August, when two fishermen survived five days stranded at sea using a large fishing cooler as a makeshift boat.

Meanwhile, the lucky canine joins Bruce the German Shepherd, who was rescued earlier this year after drifting nearly 2.5 miles out to sea in an inflatable kayak.

As for the 86-year-old sailor and his companion, authorities have not revealed what caused their voyage to go wrong or how they managed to survive for so long without contact.
