#10647. The 13 scariest, spookiest video games you should play this Halloween

The nights are drawing in, which can only mean one thing: more bed. But also the impending frights and sights of All Hallow's Eve. Yes, the season of good fear is upon us again, summoning visions of cardboard box robot costumes and other, more devilish things.

And in the spirit of it all, we've crowded around the ouija board to spirit up a list of our horror game favourites - playable on PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 1 & 2, and VR - that might just sate your macabre appetites this Halloween. Some are scary, some are grim, others offer milder - but no less spooky - chills. We'll be doing a smattering of other spooky stuff as the week goes on, but if you're looking for more recommendations, we did a different horror list last year. And if you've your own favourites, please do share them below!

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

Where better to start then with Resident Evil, one of the granddaddies of survival horror gaming? And Resident Evil Requiem is, as much as anything, a celebration of the series as it turns 30. If Resident Evil is a series of two halves - one part campy action, one part genuine scares - Requiem is the perfect tribute, and when it leans heavily into horror in its sublime opening hours, it's genuinely terrifying. New protagonist Grace Ashcroft is the perfect accompaniment as she leads these first-person segments, roaming a wonderfully atmospheric Rhodes Hill Chronic Care Centre and dealing with some very human zombies who, disturbingly, don't yet seem to realise they're dead. Then comes series stalwart Leon S. Kennedy, roundhouse kicking and quipping to ease the tension in intermittent third-person interludes before it's back into the scares. Eventually, the horror gives way to heavier action, but then go on the party hats for a final stretch of glorious fan-service that feels entirely deserved. It's not only a great Resi game, it's one of the best games this year.

Silent Hill, another grandaddy of the survival horror genre, has always been the moodier of the two big series, trading Resident Evil's B-movie thrills for quieter, more ambient psychological scares. And it's the same story with this year's Silent Hill Townfall, which - continuing the trend established by last year's marvellous Japan-set Silent Hill F - shifts the fog-shrouded action again, this time to a small Scottish island blighted by tragedy. Developer Screen Burn, known for the acclaimed likes of Stories Untold and Observation, takes some big swings here, trading the series' classic third-person perspective for a more intimate first-person one, and shifting the focus from erratic gunplay to stealth. It also tells an unexpectedly gritty and very British tale as it jumps between multiple protagonists. The result is an effective, affecting, and relentlessly suffocating piece of horror. It's not perfect, proving perhaps a bit too relentless, but few series remain this exciting, and so willing to reinvent themselves, after nearly three decades.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

What Little Nightmares originator Tarsier Studios did next. Having stepped away from the horror series that made its name a few years back, Tarsier returned earlier this year with a fresh macabre offering that, on paper at least, doesn't sound like much of a departure at all. Much like Little Nightmares, Reanimal follows the adventure of two lovable, spindly-limbed moppets - here, Boy and Girl - lost in a hellish, hostile world players traverse by scrambling over platforms, solving light puzzles, and fleeing horrifying entities during the big crescendo moments. What sets it apart, however, is the sumptuous cinematic flair of presentation and a much darker mood: in Reanimal, Little Nightmares' Burton-esque whimsy gives way to a bleaker, more vicious - and undoubtedly more violent - vision that's often genuinely disturbing. That darkness is somewhat alleviated by the comfort of two-player co-op, but however you choose to play, Tarsier's latest is a treat. A game of phenomenal mood and artistry, as striking as it is enigmatic, and a wonderfully grim accompaniment for Halloween.

If the name sounds familiar but the game does not, that might be because developer David Szymanski's Iron Lung was turned into a movie earlier this year by none other than YouTuber Markiplier. And even if you've seen the film adaptation, the original game is worth checking out; it's a masterclass in lo-fi horror that's quite unlike anything else. Early on, Iron Lung seals you into a squalid submersible and plunges you into a blood ocean on a distant alien world. Your goal is to chart its labyrinthine depths - an unknowable expanse of suffocating darkness and silence - searching for several points of interest. The problem? No windows. Instead, you're forced to rely on the tiny, murky image of the world outside beamed onto the submersible's TV screen; the darkness so overbearing, all you can see are the glimpses of strangeness caught in your floodlights. As such, movement becomes a game of blind micro-bursts and cautious rotations, as you sporadically consult your map. That's stressful enough, but Iron Lung plays some clever tricks; its stellar sound design increasingly suggesting something terrible lurks in the darkness. Slowly, the tension reaches suffocating, unbearable heights, and you'll frankly be glad it only takes 45-minutes or so to complete.

Ghost Town's biggest roadblock is the fact it's a VR-exclusive game. But if you've got the means to check it out, it's a gem. Ghost Town is the work of Fireproof Games, and it shares some similarities with the studio's earlier, acclaimed The Room series - particularly in its love of tactile puzzle boxes and contraptions. But Ghost Town is far more ambitious, building a propulsive urban fantasy adventure around its core puzzling, and plunging players into a brilliantly evocative recreation of 1983 London as protagonist and ghost hunter Edith Penrose hunts for her missing brother. As the grimy streets of the capital make way for the craggy seclusion of the North Sea, and, eventually, strange realms beyond, it's wonderfully spooky stuff - full of great writing, captivating world-building, endearing characters, fantastic puzzles, and some magnificently imaginative set-pieces - there's a bit with a lighthouse trapped in time thanks to a troubled spirit that's a real highlight. This one's for you if you've ever suspected dark arts were occurring on the London Underground.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

It's 1879 and you, private investigator Declan Ward, have been summoned to a remote, rain-lashed corner of western Ireland in search of a young woman named Evelyn Deane. It's a journey that brings you to the titular Blake Manor, now a grand hotel, where the turnips - historically accurate jack-o'-lanterns, if you were wondering - are out in force. We are, you see, three days from All Hallow's Eve, and the hotel is bustling with guests: spiritualists, mentalists, naysayers, and more; here to witness the Grand Séance and a never-before-seen attempt to commune with the dead. All, perhaps, also know something about Evelyn Dean's whereabouts. Soon enough, the detective work begins, and you 'll start sniffing around empty rooms and tantalising corners, searching for discarded notes, newspaper clippings, and other useful clues. You'll also want to interrogate the guests as they move around the house according to their hourly schedule. But every action nudges the clock on further, meaning you'll need to spend your time wisely as awful calamity approaches. Soon new secrets, new mysteries, begin to emerge, and as the clockwork rhythms of day make way for the nightmarish hallucinations of night, is anyone safe?

A tribute to a Doom modder's childhood friend, replicating the family home they used to play together in. As Myhouse.wad creator Veddge explained when he first shared the acclaimed level on the DoomWorld forums back in 2023, an unfinished, work-in-progress version of the house was found on a floppy disc gifted to him inside a box of his dead friend's belongings. A journal included in the download file explains more about the mod's somewhat difficult creation - an endeavour Veddge chose to undertake to honour his friend's memory. But the effort was undoubtedly worth it - as is the slight faff of getting Myhouse.wad to run if you're new to Doom things - and it serves as a simple but touching tribute to friendship across its ten minutes of nostalgic 90s FPS playtime. Only... what's that looking through the window on the ground floor? And was that door in the hallway always there?

Really, you could pluck any Rusty Lake game out of the 18 or so released across the series' ten years of existence, and you'd be in for a grimly macabre treat. But Servant of the Lake is developer Rusty Lake's latest offering, taking players back to 1839, where they've been hired to serve the troubled Vanderboom family across an increasingly strange three-day reunion. Servant of the Lake isn't quite as ambitious as some of the series' other games, such as 2022's excellent co-op mysteryThe Past Within. But what it lacks in structural and narrative experimentation, it makes up for in puzzles, serving up an enjoyably refined take on the series' deceptively clever mix of casual point-and-click adventuring, room-escape-style puzzling, and fascinatingly fragmented, centuries-spanning narrative. And oh yes, it's weird, as its bucolic vistas and gentle bird song gradually give way to blood and butchery, and the comforting logic of reality dims. Sure, it's more darkly amusing than outright disturbing, but Rusty Lake still calls for fresh meat this Halloween.

Probably not one for the faint of heart, Horses gained infamy after being banned by Steam and then Epic Games last year. It all unfolds across two weeks on a farm where humans are kept, masked and naked, in filthy captivity - a grim sight that goes completely unacknowledged for a surprisingly long time as you perform your daily tasks of gardening, woodchopping, and so on to satisfy the farmer's demands. Soon enough, though, Horses charges full-tilt into the grotesque. But while it'll probably make you gasp, and definitely make you squirm; its shocks are comparatively tame. Instead, writer and director Andrea Lucco Borlera's gleefully odd first-person narrative horror - developed in conjunction with Saturnalia studio Santa Ragione - gains its power from its wholly unique ambience, where surreal, slapstick comedy collides with arthouse experimentalism to create a thoroughly destabilising mood. All this, and it has something meaningful to say - not bad for £4.

Platforms: PC, PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, Switch 2

As a game, Hellraiser Revival is wobbly at best, particularly in its opening hours where its wonky gunplay and undercooked stealth never quite coalesce. That means its real-world bits - which include a lengthy stay in a dildo-strewn BDSM club - have a tendency to drag. But when the Labyrinth intermittently calls, developer Sabre Interactive pulls out all the stops. As Hellraiser Revival protagonist Aiden enters this confounding realm - an Escher-esque, non-Euclidean nightmare of piercing chains and endless, impossibly configured stone that serves as home to the Cenobites and their demonic deity Leviathan - the dull, uneven combat gives way to puzzles, platforming, and wonderfully orchestrated pursuit sequences. And it makes the whole thing worthwhile. Slowly, as the line between the Labyrinth and the real-world blurs - and as Saber starts to have more fun with its combat tools - it gets considerably more engaging. Revival and its endless tableaus of horrifically rended flesh are definitely, absolutely not for the squeamish, but Hellraiser fans - and anyone looking for a bit of body horror nastiness - should get a kick out of this one.

Platforms: Meta Quest, PC, PS5, PSVR2, Xbox Series X/S, Switch

Back in 2023, Vertigo Games released a genuinely brilliant remake of developer Trilobytes' seminal 90s CD-ROM adventure, The 7th Guest, for VR. I loved it, and spent many happy hours roaming evil toymaker Henry Stauf's wonderfully eerie mansion, reimagined in new-fangled, fully explorable 3D. Its puzzle made some clever use of VR's 1:1 movement capabilities, and its cast - bringing to life the ill-fated guests of Stauf's macabre soiree - were a blast, hitting exactly the right level of camp and presented using some kind of volumetric witchcraft, letting you walk around their apparitions despite being live-action and FMV. It's great, spooky fun for puzzle fans - even if you're not familiar with the original - but its VR requirements meant it couldn't be enjoyed by all. Well, earlier this year, Vertigo released a non-VR version for Xbox, PS5, and PC. And while it loses a little in translation to flatscreens and standard controls, it's still an enjoyable way to enjoy a cult-classic.

Well, it's solitaire, isn't it? Only, that's not entirely true. Forbidden Solitaire is a clever bit of found media horror, inviting players to explore the titular CD-ROM game - an imagined relic of the short-lived 90s multimedia boom in which the games industry convinced itself the future was lurid colour palettes, awkward pre-rendered polygons, and juddery grainy FMV. Developer Night Signal Entertainment and Grey Alien Games capture the era perfectly as you begin to investigate the enigmatic game, which - as your sister quickly informs you over DM during the game's 2019-set narrative framing device - was embroiled in controversy on release, not least because of its disturbing links to gruesome unexplained deaths. Forbidden Solitaire's fictional history is told across police records, press clippings, 90s local news reports, grisly photos, and even a pitch-perfect VHS recording of a cheesy true-crime-style mystery show. But the majority of your time is spent playing Forbidden Solitaire itself, lost in a ghoulish labyrinth said to grant immortality to those that can reach the end. But of course all is not as it seems. Forbidden Solitaire is a wonderfully creepy haunted media yarn, and - wholly unexpectedly - plays a damn good game of solitaire too.

For a while, it looked like Routine might not make it. First announced in 2012, it vanished into the kind of development hell that rarely results in anything good. But Routine isn't just good, it's great; a cracking first-person sci-fi horror that's got a particularly old-school vibe. Routine's tale of ancient influences and rogue AI takes place on a retro-futuristic lunar resort where the lo-fi 80s tech lends proceedings a disorientatingly anachronistic flavour. Here, adverts for VHS recorders flash up on chunky CRT screens as you ride the sleek transportation train, and the mall, with its futuristic pre-fab curves, meets an explosion of neon and plastic palms. Routine is refreshingly unforthcoming about what to do and where to go; giving you a loose objective, your C.A.T. device - a wonderfully tactile gadget that's half maintenance gun, half PDA - then just sending you off to explore. Unfortunately, the resort is kaput; full of flickering lights and ominous shadows. Still worse are the Terminator-like security bots, always primed to give chase when they spy you - their relentless footsteps reaching a horrifying crescendo behind you. It's a game of claustrophobic stealth and panicked pursuits - and that's just for starters. You're about to have a very bad shift.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS5, PlayStation VR2, Xbox Series X/S

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10645. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

#10643. AI fan wikis riddled with errors are muddying the waters of an already contested part of games culture

"These tools have made it very easy for bad actors to crowd out good information..."

Recently, I was writing a piece about Nighthawks and wanted to remind myself of some basic information about the companion characters. As I have a thousand times before, I put the question into Google and clicked the first result: the Nighthawks Wiki.

Of course, wikis aren't necessarily reliable, especially fan-run projects that might not have enough engaged individuals to immediately spot and fix errors. But the "Nighthawks Wiki" that I landed on immediately and obviously had deeper issues. Firstly, it wasn't a wiki at all. Rather than being editable by anybody, this website only invited "corrections" by email. Secondly, it was full of false information that didn't seem likely to have been added by fans getting their facts slightly wrong, like listing companions and skills that don't exist. It also regularly included unrelated information about the 1981 movie and the Edward Hopper painting, both of the same name, and listed them in its "sources" even when they had no relevance to the page in question.

I don't like to accuse anybody of using generative AI, because its hallmarks are difficult to pin down and are often misattributed to the LLM rather than the human writers it rips off – ask any of us how much we enjoy the em-dash, for example. But, interest piqued by the rate and intensity of apparently made up facts, I managed to trace the domain name to a website building service called Spaceship, which also offers an "AI Website builder." (I emailed the address on the "wiki" for comment, but the message bounced.)

A quick search turned up many more similar sites, some of which were actually editable as wikis and others which only called themselves by that name. They typically targeted indie games without gigantic playerbases – the sort of game that people might seek out help with and not find a thriving community ready to provide it.

It's a problem that Tim Bender, CEO for publishing company Hooded Horse, knows well. Unlike most publishers, Hooded Horse creates wikis for their own games, working with fans to make sure they're well-populated with useful information. But lately the company has had to "play whack-a-mole" with AI-generated sites competing for the search results and providing inaccurate information. One of these, for example, appeared for their game Corsair Cove, but was actually part of a more than 20 game scattershot collection operated by a site called GameDB, which the creator admitted to using generative AI to make. "[Generative AI] enables a run of basically commercial enterprises to mass produce and try to flood the search engine results of games to soak up their traffic," says Bender. Attempts to reach GameDB for comment via their public contact email resulted in an error, stating "the remote server is misconfigured".

And the combination of elbowing out legitimate wikis and being full of incorrect information ends up with a lot of frustrated players. "The whole purpose of putting out a wiki is so when [players] search for help, they end up at a site that helps them. And instead we continually hear from players who stumbled up on these things and got confused and are wondering what's going on," says Bender.

That collection of genAI wikis includes one for the 100-player FPS Wardogs; one of at least five that FlamingMojo, a member of the Independent Game Wiki Network, saw appear in the days after the game's release in September. And he says that the frustration the genAI-created sites causes players also impacts those who are trying to do the legitimate work of creating and maintaining these volunteer-led resources: "It's hurt our reputation, as players have been misinformed but we haven't created or updated the relevant page yet so they bounce off [wikis] entirely."

Fanwikis are already a contested part of games culture. Hosting sites like Fandom dominate the space, and have themselves introduced AI features that had to be rolled back due to their inaccuracies and unpopularity. Many groups have decided to leave these hosts, but those who go independent often struggle to show up in Google's search rankings underneath the competition from the umbrella sites.

Whether on a hosting site or independent, fans rarely make any money from the effort they put into their wikis. Either they sacrifice any ad revenue to the bigger platforms, or they pay for their own hosting, which is rarely covered by ads or donations. FlamingMojo, for example, pays for the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki out of his own pocket and doesn't run any ads on it.

But counterintuitively, the low profitability of these kinds of sites only leads to worse outcomes when generative-AI versions are concerned. "There's not that much ad revenue, but there's going to be a little bit and that's all it takes," says Bender. "Because the barrier is so low, why not just spew out a mountain of it and flood the search results?"

It's also possible the generative-AI sites are sometimes aiming for outcomes beyond a little ad revenue. "Some of them, you don't know what data they're collecting," says Bender. In at least one case, a generative-AI site was pointing users to download additional software that offers a paid subscription. The software had been reviewed poorly online and opening the website caused my malware software to kick in and warn me it had been flagged as "potentially unsafe." (None of the sites I attempted to contact for comment responded to my emails.)

Independent wikis also now shoulder a double burden, as generative AI scrapers send huge amounts of traffic their way, potentially affecting site stability. Publicly available internal data from the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki shows 7m requests in a 30 day period from AI crawlers. (5m of these come from Meta alone. While FlamingMojo is able to tame other scrapers using files that direct them to real pages rather than brute-force trying every link, he's found that Meta simply "hammers every page multiple times per second" with no care for "any sensible ratelimits.") These scraping waves hit smaller wikis particularly hard, as their maintainers aren't necessarily expecting huge amounts of traffic and don't have the compute capacity to handle huge spikes in visitors.

"Independent gaming wikis are in a difficult place," says FlamingMojo. As well as competition for SEO, both from corporate sites and now genAI, he says server costs have increased between three- and ten-fold, again due to the impact of genAI. Building a group of people who want to volunteer their time and energy to keep a resource updated isn't easy, although the Dune: Awakening Community Wiki has found success through gamifying contributions, in affiliation with the developer giving out in-game rewards.

And, Bender points out, all of this is happening within a much wider context of genAI's impact on the availability of information. "In a more fundamental way … these tools have made it very easy for bad actors to flood things and crowd out good information which people put dedicated time into. This is just one example of a much broader struggle that, honestly, whatever frustration I feel for this on my end, I'm sure you [journalists] feel at a thousand times that."

But it's possible that, by being one part of the wider issue, fanwiki culture could also hold a sliver of the bigger solution. "The way we survive the AI pollution is with community," says FlamingMojo. "Many people chipping in a small bit, and feeling a sense of shared ownership."

#10641. Revisiting Ecstatica - the pioneering survival horror that used its fancy ellipsoid graphics tech to fill its world with dongs

There's a well-known phenomena in video games development known as time-to-penis. It's a somewhat tongue-in-cheek measurement of the time players, when given an exciting suite of new customisation tools, will take to create the first inevitable in-game dong. Ecstatica sort of implies it works the other way round too. That developers - when giddy with a new tech toy - can't always help themselves either. But maybe I'm getting a little ahead of myself.

Ecstatica, developed by Andrew Spencer Studios and published by the long-defunct Psygnosis, originally released back in 1994. It follows a bumbling traveller who unwittingly becomes embroiled in the fate of a demon-cursed medieval village after making a rest stop in search of water. It was heavily inspired by survival horror game granddaddy Alone in the Dark (and arrived a full year before Resident Evil), but while it adopted a similar fixed-camera approach to its adventure, it had some tricks all of its own.

Most immediately notable was the decision to build its pre-rendered backdrops and characters out of ellipsoids; giving the medieval village of Tirih and its surrounding environs a softer, more organic feel compared to the angular lines afforded by the limited polygons of the time. And even today its bold, vibrant, and decidedly spherical aesthetic is quite unlike anything else.

Ecstatica isn't, I should say, new to me. I first played it back on its original release. And it's a game that never quite left my mind; in large part, I'll admit, because of the relative proliferation of dick, boob, and bulge. Faced with the infinite potential ellipsoids could offer, Andrew Spencer Studios' urge to penis was perhaps inevitable, and, lo, the moment our tights-wearing hero dismounts his horses in the opening cinematic, neither his bulbous front bauble or two–giant-eggs-in-a-hanky posterior leave anything to the imagination. And if you opt for the alternative lady hero, well, she's got ample going on there too.

And so it continues: prancing fawns with mega-breasts, muscle gimps with proud chipolatas. Ecstatica really is a singular sort of horror game. But while the cheerfully perky genitalia is undoubtedly what my teenage brain chose to squirrel away in the memory vaults, I also had distant recollections of a game that was intriguing for other, more interesting reasons. So when SNEG - a publisher founded by GOG veterans and focused on reviving lost classics - announced it was re-releasing Ecstatica and its sequel in time for Halloween, I knew I had to go back.

And you know what? After spending a few happy hours revisiting Ecstatica these past few nights, I kind of feel bad I'd mostly condensed it down to its naughty bits (for starters, the pickles, plums, and melons aren't quite as frequent as I remember them). This really is a fascinating game, with a wonderfully, uniquely odd mood - playing something like a bawdy fairytale as it blends daft slapstick silliness with an effectively sinister undertone. This is a game where our hero will stop for an unprovoked pee in a bush or, after being turned into a frog, must seek a kiss from the busty woman who lives down the road. The surprisingly great voice acting, while scant, is pure British whimsy, but throughout the village: demonic destruction, gory evisceration, and crucifixion. And Ecstatica walks a tonal tightrope that somehow works.

It still looks fantastic in its own strange way too; its broad-daylight horrors are vibrantly hued, everything is exuberantly animated, and there's so much characterful incidental detail. As you roam from one jauntily angled screen to the next, searching for objects you'll need to progress - a bible dropped by a hanged priest you can read aloud at the monastery gates, or a flying broomstick you'll need to navigate a boobytrapped dungeon - you'll encounter ale-swigging bears, perpetually annoyed imps with tiny pitchforks, and peeved minotaurs. You'll trip on rocks and inelegantly face-plant; you'll smack your bonce on low ceilings; you'll excitedly put on a suit of armour only to discover it slows you to a comically useless crawl. And you can even kick a priest off the belltower if you're feeling particularly mean.

It's not your usual sort of survival horror, then, but it's got one hell of a trick up its sleeve: recurring enemies that'll readily give chase, notably including an unkillable werewolf that's essentially a precursor to the stalkers - think Resident Evil's Mr. X - beloved by modern-day horror games. You'll catch him peeking around corners as the camera switches to a new angle, he'll launch a surprise attack from the rooftops, or suddenly give chase if you disturb his murdering. And when that happens, he's relentless - the only way you'll lose him is to inflict enough damage he'll give momentary pause, or hide in a cupboard until he moves on. There's even a little stealth. It's rudimentary stuff by today's standards, not helped by the unwieldy combat and tank controls, but it still manages to muster a whiff of tension. And it's remarkable to rediscover something so pioneering in a title I'd mentally reduced to 'the willy game' - particularly when horror's better-known 3D stalker series, like Clock Tower, were yet to come.

Granted, Ecstatica has enough old-school jank that it's not an easy recommendation for modern players; from its wayward combat to its untelegraphed (though pretty amusing) sudden deaths. Or the inventory system, which demands you drop anything your two hands can't hold right there on the floor - so you better remember where you left it. And you're screwed if you accidentally place something in tall grass or just outside the fixed camera frame. SNEG's remaster, though, is decent, and an enjoyable way to experience this cult-classic curio.

And 'curio' is perhaps the best word to describe Ecstatica, with its pioneering survival horror spirit, its ellipsoids, and its unashamed love of slapstick, boobs, and dong. It's been an unexpected pleasure revisiting it some 30 years later, and I'm excited I'll soon have my very first brush with Ecstatica 2. All I know about the 1997 sequel is its hero isn't blessed with a prominent bauble from the off, so its time-to-penis is already looking like a longer one.

#10640. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10639. Staggering number of Americans would abandon their pets to play GTA 6 early

A shocking number of American gamers say they would give up their pets, sex, and even some of their favorite habits just to play GTA 6 a week before everyone else.

With Grand Theft Auto 6 finally set to launch on November 19, 2026, the wait is almost over. However, after 13 years without a new mainline GTA game, some fans are apparently willing to sacrifice just about anything to get into Vice City sooner.

According to a survey conducted by Unwind Media and reported by the New York Post, nearly 10% of pet-owning gamers said they would be willing to give up their pets in exchange for playing GTA 6 one week early.

And somehow, that’s not even the only wild statistic.

The survey, which involved more than 1,300 gamers, also found that over 20% would give up sex for an entire month to get their hands on Rockstar’s upcoming blockbuster seven days early.

Interestingly, women were more willing to make that sacrifice than men, with 25% saying they would forgo sex compared to 19% of male respondents.

Things got even more extreme among gamers who had already pre-ordered GTA 6. In that group, 32% said they would give up sex for a month, while a staggering 13% would surrender their pets.

Apparently, man’s best friend is no match for Vice City.

Pets and sex weren’t the only things players were willing to sacrifice, either. The survey found that 53% would give up alcohol, 43% would quit smoking, and more than 40% would ditch fast food or social media.

Coffee and streaming services were also on the chopping block, although smartphones proved harder to part with, as just 16% were willing to give theirs up.

Of course, these are hypothetical responses from surveyed gamers, not evidence that millions of Americans are actually preparing to abandon their dogs.

North American GTA 6 fans will be among the last to play thanks to regional launch

Analyst says GTA 6 won’t hurt Fortnite and Roblox as “not everyone” will play it

GTA 6 sick days predicted to cost the US $1 billion

Still, the findings highlight just how ridiculous the anticipation surrounding GTA 6 has become.

The survey also found that more than half of employed adult gamers planned to take time off for the release, rising to over 62% among those who had pre-ordered.

As Dexerto previously reported, an estimated 1.5 million Americans could miss work on launch day, potentially costing the US economy $1 billion in lost productivity.

One California car company has already announced it will pause operations because employees want to play, while a US Army unit even offered soldiers four days off as an incentive to re-enlist.

And for American gamers desperate to play as soon as possible, there’s another catch. North America is among the last regions scheduled to unlock GTA 6, with New Zealand players getting access hours earlier.

Thankfully, that wait is measured in hours rather than weeks. Your dog should be safe.

#10637. Beyond the Password Reset: Building Recovery Experiences That Protect Online Shopping

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An unfamiliar delivery address appears in a shopper’s account. Minutes later, an order confirmation arrives for something they never purchased. They try to sign in, but their password no longer works.

For the consumer, this is an urgent problem: stop the misuse, recover access, and understand what happened. For developers, it exposes a gap between building a login screen and building a service that remains useful when that login is compromised.

This article examines recovery from a business-to-consumer perspective: what an individual shopper needs, and how developers can translate those needs into safer interfaces and backend controls.

Account takeover and identity theft can overlap, but they are different problems.

Account takeover means someone has gained unauthorized control of an existing account. Identity theft involves misuse of someone’s personal information. Recovering access to one shopping account does not necessarily resolve misuse elsewhere.

Design the interface around questions a worried customer can answer:

A recovery page should explain the scope of help. “Recover your shopping account” is a specific promise. “Restore your identity” describes a much broader outcome that an ordinary retailer cannot deliver on its own.

Before building the workflow, list the consumer-visible assets involved: account access, addresses, order history, payment references, contact details, and support messages. Each needs its own review or remediation step.

A shopper who cannot sign in must still be able to locate help. Provide a public “My account may be compromised” entry point alongside ordinary password recovery.

Separate the two journeys. Someone who forgot a password usually needs a credential reset. Someone reporting suspicious activity may also need unauthorized changes reversed, orders investigated, and existing access revoked.

Avoid using a submitted email address as proof of ownership. A public report can initiate triage, but it should not expose order history or authorize sensitive changes.

OWASP’s Forgot Password Cheat Sheet recommends consistent responses for existing and nonexistent accounts, rate limiting, and securely generated reset tokens that expire and work only once.

If the details match an account, we’ll send instructions through an available recovery method.

The frontend message is only one part of that protection. Different response timing or API behavior can still reveal account existence.

After verifying a recovery factor, resist granting immediate access to every account feature.

A useful architecture gives the consumer a short-lived recovery session with narrowly defined permissions. For example, it might permit reviewing security settings while preventing purchases, data exports, or changes to payment information.

This type documents intent; it does not enforce security. The server must validate the session, its expiry, its association with the account, and the required permission on every request.

Check resource ownership separately. Permission to request an order review must never allow a consumer to submit another customer’s order identifier.

Do not treat an attacker’s recently changed email address as trustworthy recovery evidence. Review recovery options against previously established evidence and the account’s required level of assurance.

A new password is insufficient if an attacker still holds a valid session.

OWASP’s compromise-recovery guidance calls for reviewing recovery contacts and authentication methods, removing unauthorized changes, and invalidating existing sessions and outstanding recovery credentials after successful recovery.

For a consumer application, translate that into a visible checklist:

Use a durable workflow so a partial failure cannot silently appear as completion. Credential replacement and session revocation may span different systems; record pending work, retry failed operations safely, and show the consumer an accurate status.

JWT-based applications need particular care. Changing a password does not automatically invalidate an independently verifiable token. Choose an enforceable revocation strategy, such as checking a server-side session record or account security version on protected requests.

The OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet provides background on session lifecycle controls.

Recovery screens are often used on a phone, in a hurry, by someone who is uncertain about what happened.

Use short steps with clear outcomes: “Review recent changes,” “Secure your sign-in,” and “Check your orders.” Explain why information is requested before asking for it.

Use native form controls with visible labels. Associate field instructions and errors with their inputs, keep keyboard navigation predictable, and move focus deliberately when a step changes. SitePoint’s HTML Form Elements guide is useful background for building these controls.

A simple reporting form might begin with:

Provide an alternative when the consumer cannot access their usual inbox. That alternative needs its own verification process; support staff should not bypass security simply because a caller sounds convincing.

Once the immediate account problem is contained, a consumer may need help addressing misuse of personal information elsewhere.

Keep this guidance separate from the application’s own recovery controls. A retailer can investigate its orders and secure its accounts. It cannot directly correct records held by unrelated organizations.

For U.S. consumers, link to the FTC’s IdentityTheft.gov as a government resource. Offer country-appropriate resources when serving other regions.

Consumers considering paid assistance can also review an identity recovery service to understand the support available for broader restoration tasks. They should check eligibility, exclusions, and service terms before relying on it.

External assistance should remain optional. Restoring access to a customer’s account should not depend on purchasing another product.

Consumers need an understandable record of what happened. Offer a case summary with relevant timestamps, reported changes, affected orders, completed actions, and unresolved questions.

Separate customer-facing information from internal investigation details. A consumer may need to know that an address changed; they do not need raw authentication tokens or internal fraud-detection rules.

Treat recovery data as sensitive. Avoid recording passwords, recovery secrets, or full payment details in logs. Restrict staff access to case information and define retention periods appropriate to its purpose.

If evidence uploads are necessary, provide a secured upload route with access controls and a deletion policy. Do not ask customers to send sensitive documents to an ordinary support inbox by default.

A successful redirect is a weak definition of recovery.

After recovery, help consumers strengthen their sign-in. SitePoint’s Developer Guide to Implementing Passkeys offers a starting point for exploring passkey authentication.

A stronger sign-in method still needs a carefully designed fallback. Evaluate the recovery path whenever you change authentication.

For a shopper, recovery succeeds when unauthorized access stops, legitimate access returns, and the next steps are clear.

Developers can support that outcome by treating recovery as a complete product journey: limited permissions, enforceable revocation, accessible interfaces, accurate case status, and clear boundaries around external assistance.

The most useful question in a design review is simple: if this were your account, could you regain control and tell what still needs attention?

#10636. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

Materials provided by University of Southern California. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10635. MrBeast spent $7M and hired 250 artists for animated series featuring Marvel & Star Wars voice actors

MrBeast has revealed that he spent $7 million creating the second season of his animated series, Beast Labs, bringing together hundreds of artists and voice actors from major franchises.

The YouTube star originally released a 30-minute pilot for the show in 2025, but decided to overhaul the project after viewers criticized its storyline, characters, and lack of emotional depth.

In a new behind-the-scenes video, Jimmy Donaldson explained how he assembled an international production team to improve the series, recruiting talent with experience working on Marvel, Star Wars, South Park, and Bob’s Burgers.

According to MrBeast, the new season involved 250 artists from 16 countries, who worked together to produce more than 140,000 hand-drawn frames of animation.

The creator initially attempted to animate the project himself before realizing he needed a much larger team to bring his vision to life.

He also recruited experienced television writers to address criticism of the original pilot, including Jameel Saleem, who previously worked on South Park and Bob’s Burgers.

“With Beast animation, our rule from day one was character first,” Saleem explained. “You can’t just throw them into fights. You have to make the audience care about who wins.”

The production also expanded beyond the laboratory setting featured in the original pilot, introducing new environments including deserts, cities, shopping malls, and car chases.

MrBeast said the third episode is “basically a Mad Max movie,” while confirming that Season 2 runs for more than 100 minutes.

The revamped show also features established voice actors, including Fred Tatasciore, who has voiced the Hulk across Marvel projects, alongside talent associated with Star Wars and anime productions.

MrBeast specifically highlighted Tatasciore’s performance, claiming the actor became emotional while recording dialogue for the series.

MrBeast is making a big-budget 2D animated cartoon series starring himself

MrBeast explains why he doesn’t regret losing millions making Beast Games

“That’s Fred Tatasciore. He voiced the Hulk for 15 years, and as he was voicing our show… he cried,” Donaldson said.

Several familiar internet personalities also appear in the new season, with streamers Sketch and Valkyrae featured in the first episode.

Members of MrBeast’s own team have roles as well, including Nolan and Tareq, while the character Panthar returns with a more developed storyline.

Donaldson also addressed comparisons between the show’s animation and Invincible, noting that viewers had repeatedly brought up the Amazon animated superhero series in comments on a recent teaser.

“Weirdly, quite a bit of them are about how the animation is better than Invincible,” he said. “I don’t know why everyone keeps commenting that, but that aside, everyone seems VERY excited.”

Despite the show’s animated format, MrBeast suggested viewers shouldn’t assume it is intended exclusively for younger audiences.

“People think it’s a kid show? They have NO idea what’s about to hit them,” he said.

#10633. Inside Buckshot Roulette developer Mike Klubnika's terrifically nasty horror universe of post-Soviet malaise

In Mike Klubnika's growing oeuvre of brilliantly deplorable games, the same elements tend to recur. First, smoking: deep, long drags to make the lungs burn and eyes water. Second, club music: hard and fast, accented with just a faint sense of melancholy. Third, guns: big and loud, usually aimed at you from point-blank range, ready to blow your brains out.

Across three excellent games in almost as many years - 2023's Buckshot Roulette, 2025's S.p.l.i.t, and 2026's Machine Party - Klubnika has remixed each of these elements with design verve and stylistic aplomb. Buckshot Roulette is a gruesome game of chance delivered at the business end of a pump-action shotgun. S.p.l.i.t. is a Matrix-esque hacking game with the gnarliest, most harrowing ending I've played in recent years. Machine Party, meanwhile, delivers a seedy, hyper-violent take on Mario Party: funny, yes; brutal, too.

But each of these games also contains further tell-tale signs of the expanding Klubnikaverse: grubby low-poly graphics whose every surface seems to be coated in some kind of unpleasant residue; bleak settings that hint at a shared near-future authoritarian setting. These games are short, too, lasting just an hour apiece. But that means they land even harder - thwack - like a pistol whip to the face.

There is a "shared vibe," Klubnika tells me over a video call. "It's like, if you're someone living in this type of universe then you probably don't have what you want most of the time."

Klubnika, a 24-year old university drop-out from the tucked-away enclave of Tallinn, Estonia, describes more specific emotions that he intends his games to arouse: "isolation", "desperation", "risk", "worry." It's hardly crowd-pleasing stuff, but that hasn't stopped his three most recent titles enjoying massive commercial success. Buckshot Roulette has sold 10 million copies. S.p.l.i.t has shifted 200,000. Machine Party currently sits at 1.5 million.

Like other young creatives working in and around horror - notably Backrooms director Kane Parsons and Obsession director Curry Barker - Klubnika was a child of the computer. He was part of a "tech-savvy" friendship group in Tallinn: some made YouTube videos, others were self-taught programmers. Early on, Klubnika made 3D art in Blender while messing about with sound design and video editing. All of them played video games.

Klubnika's desktop PC, then, was both a creative instrument and portal into exotic worlds and taboo-feeling media: Klubnika remembers pirating the edgy top-down shooter Hotline Miami when he was 10 or 11 years old before discovering the music of Aphex Twin in one particularly strange level of the Half-Life multiplayer mod Sven Co-op.

When Klubnika wasn't glued to his screen, he was hitting house parties in Tallinn. But then it was back to the computer with a view to pushing his 3D art skills towards architectural visualisations. However, Klubnika grew bored because "archviz is very technical," he says. "I started making art instead." Klubnika applied to the world-renowned Estonian Academy of Arts, though his application was unsuccessful (clearly, the admissions staff didn't appreciate the young artist's eye for the macabre). Thereafter, he began studying English Language, though he dropped out after a single term because Buckshot Roulette was blowing up.

Ever since, Klubnika seems to have possessed a knack for unlocking Steam's inscrutable algorithm - of winning the wishlist war and riding high on the recommendations tab. This success did not occur overnight: Klubnika self-published 19 small games prior to this moment, five of which are collected on 2023's Unsorted Horror. "I got into game development with the idea it's probably not going to work out as a career," he says. "I did it for the love of the game."

All games are born from specific circumstances: a forest that naturally invites a game of hide and seek; the military-funded MIT computer labs that birthed nascent video games in the 60s. Klubnika's games are no different: first and foremost, their greyscale atmospheres are inspired by the huge, concrete buildings of Tallinn's Soviet era (which offer a stark counterpoint to the city's more straightforwardly picturesque medieval architecture). More specifically, Klubnika, like all young men in Estonia, was required to complete mandatory military service.

He trained in communications, which meant he spent a lot of time fiddling with radios. "I had a pretty easy time," he says, comparing the more technical requirements of his role to the physicality demanded of a machine gunner. "But it was still very demanding," he stresses. Especially challenging was spending large amounts of time away from home: he was stationed three hours away from Tallinn and needed a permit to return.

"There's something a little bit weird about this type of bureaucracy which doesn't feel human to me," says Klubnika. Rather than freely cavorting about Tallinn, he found himself incorporated into a stringently controlled military system. "My games take those ideas to the extreme," he says. "The people in them are very small small cogs in the machine."

It's easy to imagine the protagonist of S.p.l.i.t. toiling away in front of a large, old-fashioned computer as something of a mirror to the radio-operating Klubnika during his military service. But the events of S.p.l.i.t. are no mere training exercise. One mistake and everyone dies, including the player. The game's ending, which involves a devilish triptych of life-ending options - a noose, power drill, and screwdriver - makes this point in awful, hilarious fashion, like a cross between Pi and Eraserhead. It took my breath away and also left me feeling queasy - a testament to Klubnika's commitment to stacking one grim punchline on top of another.

But the Estonian game maker's military service days also yielded moments of levity. He enjoyed smoking with his fellow trainees; a "great ritual," he says, and a means of "decompression" (it's also a pastime Klubnika loves seeing in other media - video games like Arctic Eggs and TV shows such as True Detective). That explains one of his major motifs, then.

Klubnika's eyes also light up at the mention of firearms in the military. "Yeah, we had firearms and shooting drills and stuff," he says, casually noting the "super cool" feeling of firing an AR-15 assault rifle and the "sheer destruction" of a hand grenade. That, then, seems to explain Klubnika's fondness for including ballistic, brain-splattering weaponry in his games. He recalls one explosive training exercise, in particular: "You start off in a bunker and then you go out and you do the exercise. Then you go into a different bunker, and the entire unit goes through. So you're sitting there with a bunch of people that are, like, 20-years-old, and they're all holding high-explosive grenades. It's like, ‘This is crazy bro. Surely nothing will go wrong.'"

The anecdote seems to speak to a central sensibility of Klubnika's gritty games: a foregrounding of violent power that finds no contradiction in the powerlessness of those who wield it. Discussing Machine Party, Klubnika describes the contrapuntal nature of his work in simpler terms. "The idea was to have this fun party game, but it's industrial horror - and it's also kind of sad," he says. At one memorable moment, players get to enjoy respite from the otherwise murderous experience, running across a field of golden wheat, as if fleetingly inhabiting a scene from Terence Malick's 1978 movie, Days of Heaven. Klubnika's right: it is sad - a better life is dangled in front of these people, and then cruelly snatched away.

Barring any kind of major geopolitical rupture involving Estonia, neighbouring Russia, and Europe, Klubnika plans to keep making precisely the type of games that has brought him such success. He has toyed with the idea of scaling up but decided against it for fear of becoming an "administrator" - a suit who delegates the creative process rather than actually does it. "If I end up doing that then a large part of the reason why I make games is gone," he says.

The next game, reveals Klubnika, should harmonise two aspects of his work: the replayable arcade mechanics of Machine Party and Buckshot Roulette, and the single-player story focus of S.p.l.i.t. "I want to find a balance between the two styles," he says.

Regardless of its ultimate form, Klubnika will make it in a staunchly DIY manner: he will laboriously hand-paint each grotty surface, stretch every texture into a warped shadow of itself, and smash together 3D models of varying fidelity into a single scene. In the Klubnikaverse, a nightmarish fun-house distortion of the real world, these kinds of ostensible visual flaws deepen immersiveness rather than interrupt it. This is a fast, dirty way of making video games - and it suits Klubnika's nasty fiction down to its trash-strewn ground.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox Series X/S
