#10632. Dawn of War 4 may be the most generous RTS I've played

Maybe the best way to understand the kind of game Dawn of War 4 wants to be is through a single button. If you're playing a campaign mission in DoW 4 and you're not really feeling it, or you made a silly mistake right at the end and got summarily wiped by the AI, or maybe you're in a hurry and just really curious about what happens next in the story, you have a quietly revolutionary option to hand: you can just hit the 'skip mission' button, and move on.

Dawn of War 4's game director, Jan Theysen explained the thinking to me like this: "In the end, we feel it's not that we want the player to play, necessarily, the game we envision for them. It's more like: here is the thing you can play with, and have fun."

That line of thought seems to permeate Dawn of War 4 in all kinds of places, and in turn makes it come across as incredibly generous. My most recent time with the game, for instance, focused on the campaign and the skirmish mode. The campaign alone is massive. It has four sub campaigns within it, one for each faction, their stories linking to form one grand narrative surrounding the planet of Kronus - returning here from the original Dawn of War's cult favourite expansion, Dark Crusade. Any one of those four campaigns is, according to developer King Art Games, longer than the entire original Dawn of War campaign. And within each there's at least one fork in the road, where you make a choice leading you down one of two mission paths. In other words, each campaign is really almost two campaigns in one.

The two I've played - right the way to completion, making that about half the total game's campaign already - are those of the time-honoured kunnin' Orks, and the shiny new techno-Catholics of the Adeptus Mechanicus (Admech, to the 40k nerds). To pick one example of how these can split, the Orks' campaign initially focuses on Guzkutta - a local Kronus upstart with suitably big ambitions - and you're given the choice of sticking with him or switching to the glorious, returning warboss Gorgutz, who may or may not need knocking off his perch. The campaign that follows mixes wonderfully chaotic infighting between the two Ork factions, with a fair bit of Admech-stomping and human-squashing on the side.

The Admech campaign is similar. Without spoiling much, you begin playing as a deeply inquisitive Potentia-Delta-9, who goes digging around in Kronus' deserts without a sufficient remit. All that's laced with an array of delightful 40k-isms: Mysterious voices! Betrayal! Computer spirits! Outraged officials citing revered and highly verbose bureaucratic procedure! If you've read any of the 40k novels (or indeed the Horus Heresy; those mysterious voices will jump out to anyone familiar with the first couple of books) then you will be well at home here. Dawn of War 4 has nabbed John French, one of the Black Library's Horus Heresy authors, so perhaps none of this should come as a surprise.

These splits then play out smartly in the structure of missions themselves. Some might be a race, for instance, where you and your rival need to fight your way through to a final point beyond a single, powerful enemy's base. I found one version this format especially enjoyable in the Admech campaign, when those two separate, essentially linear paths converged at one point, creating a supremely 40k type of bottleneck as three warring factions all met at once. I found success by letting my rival battle the stronger Ork faction, then darting in to pick off their main army for a major setback, then brute force my way through the remaining, slightly softened Ork horde. Then I sould send my my newer reinforcements from my base back into that chokepoint for more stalling while what was left of my vanguard made a big push to the finish.

Back to the sense of generosity, meanwhile, and the other part of this comes in the skirmishes, where there's an extraordinary amount of customisability available. You can tinker with the game's underlying numbers to make units squishier or tankier by default, or to make research or point-capturing faster or slower. You can change income rates, reinforcement costs, cooldown times. By my count, there are nine 'general' settings, eight 'economy' ones, and eight 'combat' ones to play around with. You can choose from multiple commander units (or disable them or tinker with how they spawn), as well as multiple game modes. There's the classic Annihilation and Domination (wipe out the enemy or take and hold capture points) alongside new modes called Maelstrom and Defence. These have you racing to claim one-off capturable points that drop in periodically all around the map, and, I'm going to assume… defending something (only the first two modes were playable in this preview). Then there's the Last Stand mode, which returns here revamped from Dawn of War 2 - again unavailable in this build - and the army painter; a classic feature which I can't talk about for now. Plus, reams of post-launch stuff to do, from free maps to new modes such as my personal beloved: the Conquest mode popularised in Dark Crusade.

Basically, by RTS launch standards it's pretty massive. The only limiting factor is that by 40k standards, having only four factions is a little thin, given how many there are in the game's universe. Four is perfectly standard for launch-version RTS games, but a few big 40k ones are missing here, most notably Chaos, the Astra Militarum, and the Eldar - who all featured prominently in the previous games. (The Astra Militarum are present and briefly playable in DoW 4's prologue, and pop up here and there in other missions as blobs of unfortunate cannon fodder, so you could reasonably hope they might arrive after launch. More factions have already been promised by King Art for 2027.)That scope is mirrored, in a way, by King Art's approach to Dawn of War 4's mechanical side. DoW has a little bit of every Dawn of War in it - yes, even Dawn of War 3, with its huge scale, spectacle, many active abilities, and Imperial Knight-class super units, for starters - and it's seemingly all the better for it. The sense when you play is that you're playing Dawn of War in its ultimate form: lavish, spectacular, dark, authentic, deep, and complex - but also a little campy, playful, and inviting to newer RTS players too.

Some more of that generosity, then: there are times, particularly when Orks and Admech are involved, where the screen is quite literally full of units. An ingenious simplifying factor in these cases is the new right-click-and-drag function, whereby you can select a group of enemies to attack in the same way you'd click-and-drag to choose your own units to command. Doing so sees your selected units automatically prioritise targets amongst the opposing group you've chosen.

You might think that sounds like it's oversimplifying the approach, removing the hard-earned skill of RTS veterans to micromanage, a bit like the combo shortcuts of modern fighting games. But that's not quite right. Instead, in practice I found it means you can efficiently move huge numbers of units into battle knowing they won't make truly irrational decisions (for the most part), and then get into micromanaging after the fact - which is where the great players will undoubtedly be separated from the good. The same goes for active abilities, of which there are quite a few in DoW 4: you can right-click on one to have that unit automatically use it when it comes off cooldown, so you know you're not leaving anything forgotten amongst the carnage.

"We basically said: let's give the player the option to automate a lot of things," Theysen explained to me. "It might not be 100 percent perfect - there is still something you can do if you really want to be more optimal - but you know, it's pretty good, right? And it's definitely better than not using something because you're overwhelmed." To Theysen, "the philosophy is that as a normal player, an average player, this really helps you out. If you're into your esports, and you're a really high-end RTS player, then you can probably still optimise things and you [will] want to do it yourself. But you know, not everybody is about 20 clicks-per-second."

I was curious about this - so many games with a PvP element find themselves falling into the trap of appealing to the competitive hardcore, leaving the often larger audience behind. "Basically, what we're saying is: we do not decide if this is, you know, the next big esports title. There are a lot of people with a lot more money who tried that," Theysen explained. The team is instead simply trying to "make sure there is no good reason why it couldn't be … but also, this is not our goal. We are not designing the game for the top one percent. It's more like, okay, we want a lot of people having fun with it, and we want to make sure that if people want to play it competitively, it's possible."The approach is the same when it comes to elements of balance - some of which, such as the reinforce function, have already become discussion points on certain subreddits. "If you balance everything for the top one percent then the game might feel wrong for the 99 percent. Because for them it's like: why are these units so weak? Well, they are so weak because there is some sort of thing that some pro player can maybe pull off."

On the topic of reinforcement, players can hit the reinforce button and have a squad produce a new unit after a short moment (or right-click it to have it automatically done as soon as resources are available). Theysen told me that, initially, the game didn't have on-field reinforcements at all. It's a returning feature from the first Dawn of War game, and quite a unique one. Later games from original developer Relic, such as Company of Heroes, saw reinforcement shift to be something only done on recalling your units to their main base. But at first it "felt a little bit weird - it's like lore-wise, it doesn't really make sense. And it's also not a super typical RTS feature."Then the team tested it just in case it was fun, "and it turned out to be really good because it's a way to bring the metagame, or the economy, into the battles, right?" Suddenly the player has a choice between spending lots of money to reinforce mid-battle, prolonging and maybe turning the battle in your favour, or they can micromanage more closely for a more efficient result. In practice I found myself having it set to 'on' almost by default - but in a way that's a conscious decision too, and a kind of crutch that I may have to ween myself off if I want to improve as a player over time. (Or, equally, something I can just leave on, so I can get on with having fun smashing my sea of Orks into Admech turrets.)

Even Dawn of War 4's headline visual innovation is a consideration here. The combat director feature - where hordes of individual units lock onto each other in melee with bespoke fighting animations - works in such a way as to take exactly the same amount of time as if your units were fighting each other the old-fashioned RTS way, Theysen assured me. "The things that are going on below the hood and the visualisation are completely separate from each other," he explained. "You could deactivate the whole thing and the game would still play exactly the same." Including it is essentially a flourish: something done, as I wrote when first testing it out, simply because one of King Art's animation specialists loved how it worked in the original and wanted to try something cool with it here.

This, and so much of Dawn of War 4's apparent munificence, brought another question to mind. How exactly can the team - not a particularly large one, by modern development standards - manage this approach? Amongst all the talk of ballooning budgets and sustainability in games these days, here's a fairly modest studio stacking feature on feature, promising lengthy post-release support - and, crucially, a lot of these little things are what you could fairly describe as luxuries.

Theysen put it down to a few things. The first is time: "We were very lucky," he said, with the team having worked on the game for around five years now. "That's a very long time." But that was made possible by the relatively small team size, "so the burn rate - the overall budget - is not higher than for other RTS games." The other factor is that they've been working with their current tech and fellow teammates for a long time, making that development more efficient. "Since we had the technology, had a lot of experience already, and had the time, we could basically say: okay, let's try to get it right - and everybody was very supportive," he added, citing publisher Plaion and Games Workshop's patience.

"If you look at some RTS games in the past, and you hear they had like an 18-month development time… I would say it's not possible to really make a great RTS game in 18 months," he continued. "Because it's just too complex - even if you have the technology, you discover stuff while you're working on the game, and then you can't follow up because whatever, you have to hit the next deadline. For us it was more like: we like the long development times. We also like long pre-productions where we can really figure out what we [want to] try and do here? And really, really experiment."

There's one more factor Theysen put the most emphasis on, however. "We really try hard to keep the team together. So here there are a lot of people who worked at King Art for like 15 years, or even longer, and everybody knows everybody in the team. It's like you have a sort of shorthand, but also you really understand, well, who is good at what." He gave an example: "Someone might not be the right person for something, and maybe if we put these two together, that's going to be great because you know this is really something they want to work on."

He cites the combat director again. "That was something that came from our head of animation, who was like a huge Dawn of War 1 fan, and he basically said: yeah, we can do the same thing, but we could also make it cooler; give me a couple weeks and I'll try to figure it out. And [our] first impression, everybody was like: I don't know how this is supposed to work. Because we have literally tens of thousands of permutations of who can fight who, right - because not only do we have almost 100 units, we also have all the different weapons and all of that stuff. But he basically said: 'No, no, I have a plan, let me try it out.'"

The result is a feature I've genuinely never seen before in the genre, amongst a sea of other neat little ideas that have no doubt come from someone wanting to try something cool, and insisting they have a plan.

"It basically comes down to having the time first, but also having the confidence," Theysen said. "And knowing the guy [so] that you say: okay, if he believes that works, it probably will, so let him try it. And I think this is really, really valuable. Because you cannot just buy a team. Or cannot just say, you know, we're doing the next great RTS or shooter or whatever it is, and now we buy everybody that we need for that and two years later we have a great game. That's not how it works."

All this has to be hedged by the fact I've only played a chunk of Dawn of War 4 so far, of course. But what a glorious, grim, bewitchingly good chunk it's been. The scale of some of my campaign battles has been stupendous, with huge numbers of units quite literally filling the screen and a level of visual spectacle rarely seen in this genre. The commitment to doing 40k right is eveident throughout, from the obvious narrative inspirations to the relentless, almost comical bleakness (especially in the level of violence imposed on those poor Astra Militarum). And the mix of older elements - requisition points, reinforcement, base-building - with more modern RTS intricacies feels considered and deliberate.

There'll be tinkering to do, no doubt - melee units look to be a bit too strong for now, and some better artillery-style disruptor units or abilities wouldn't go amiss - but there's time for that, and no doubt more to follow it after launch. And even as it is, this is one of the most impressive previews of a strategy game I have ever played.

#10631. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

#10629. Call of Duty player says $280 Asus monitor saved them from stray bullet fired outside house

A Call of Duty player claims their Asus gaming monitor saved their life after a stray bullet flew through their wall and struck their screen during a late-night gaming session.

Reddit user ‘antisocialnae’ shared their terrifying experience on November 2, revealing how a $280 Asus TUF gaming monitor they purchased five years ago may have prevented a tragedy.

According to the player, they were enjoying some late-night Call of Duty when they suddenly heard loud bangs coming from outside. Unlike the gunfire in their game, these shots were very real.

Realizing something was wrong, they immediately dropped to the floor and called 911.

After speaking with the operator, the player checked their wall and discovered a bullet hole directly behind their gaming setup. Even more frightening, the bullet had struck their main monitor, but there was no exit hole.

“I couldn’t believe the monitor held the round in from possibly hitting me head on, hurting me really badly, or absolutely destroying my PC build depending on the trajectory it was going,” they explained.

The monitor in question was an Asus TUF VG258QM 280Hz display, which they purchased for $280 when building their first gaming setup five years earlier.

Understandably, the ordeal left them shaken.

“I cried so much when everything calmed down and I realized how close I was to meeting my maker,” they admitted.

Fortunately, they still have two other Asus monitors, although they hadn’t turned their PC back on to check whether it survived the incident.

PC saves woman’s life by deflecting a bullet while he sleeps

Twitch streamer’s gaming monitor stops bullet and keeps working

Apex Legends player nearly dies IRL after stray bullet hits his monitor

The story is remarkably similar to a 2022 incident involving an Apex Legends YouTuber, whose monitor was struck by a bullet that reportedly missed his head by just three inches.

The incident quickly caught the attention of Asus, with a company representative responding directly to the Reddit thread.

“While we pride ourselves on making durable monitors and making gaming seem lifelike, your monitor definitely went above and beyond in both regards,” they wrote.

“We’ll reach out to you soon to see what we can do to honor its sacrifice.”

Naturally, someone had to ask the important question: “I take it as the warranty doesn’t cover stray 9mm rounds?”

The Asus representative joked that the damage would normally fall under customer-induced damage or an “act of god,” but suggested the unusual circumstances might warrant special consideration.

“Doesn’t mean we can support, but if you do customer service long enough, there are cases that obviously stand out from the rest,” they explained.

A replacement has not been confirmed, but the player already has a pretty good excuse for an upgrade.

And bizarrely, this isn’t the first time gaming hardware has taken a bullet for its owner in 2026. Back in June, a woman credited her gaming PC with saving her life after it deflected a shot fired through her neighbor’s wall.

#10627. With a cracking new tactical feature and dedicated UI "strike team", Football Manager 27 is a huge step back towards FM's best form

Welcome back, Football Manager. I've only played half a day or so of it, whistling through my inductions, board meetings and opening transfer window, but already the positive signs are there. Football Manager 27 is a big, much-appreciated step in the right direction. In a way it's a step back - this is essentially the game I wish FM26 had been - but sometimes that's necessary. One back, two forward. At least it's genuine progress. This is a Football Manager game again.

If that "game I wish FM26 had been" line has raised an Ancelotti-eqsue eyebrow, here's what I mean by it: FM27 has got rid of most - if not quite all - of the cards-and-tiles UI that gave FM26 its notoriety. It's dramatically easier, faster, and more sensible to navigate, though I still think there are places to improve. But it's also maintained the best part of last year's entry, in the majorly overhauled in- and out-of-possession tactics systems. And it's built on that system smartly: elements are tinkered with and made clearer; other parts are expanded, and a new system added entirely, in something called "tactical effectiveness". This sounds almost comically dry but, in true Football Manager fashion, that's the good stuff. This is a really smart system, one that brings with it a huge boost to the role-playing side of the game. Combined with the rolling-back of various UI elements to more traditional FM fare, it makes me feel about as optimistic as I have been for this series for a little while.

It's worth focusing on last year's game for a moment. "We had a lot of technical challenges to solve," explains Rémy Boicherot, FM27's head of design, to me in an interview at Sports Interactive's head office in East London. Part of that was porting the interface to the new engine itself, with last year's game moving to Unity for the first time, and requiring that unprecedented year's delay as a result. The other was with "new graphical data," as Boicherot puts it. "It's a huge undertaking to transfer technology, and this is what we had to focus on last year to release the game." The studio's feedback rounds with players have since brought up two big points of improvement they hoped to make this year. The first, unsurprisingly, was the UI. The second, a little more interestingly, was the question of immersion. "The sense of feeling lost in that world."

With FM27, and those technical challenges seemingly now overcome, "we just focused on gameplay and making sure that the game resonated with our community," Boicherot continues. "We focused all our efforts into this. It was really a consolidation year. We still used a lot of these new philosophies to empower the interface, but with more of a familiar feel, and with just more efficiencies all throughout."

Ant Farley, senior features manager at Sports Interactive, put it similarly. "We were building from scratch," he said, which meant "everything needing to be built from the ground up, and just the volume of work that that entails and the technical complexity of that mammoth task. You can zero in on [the fact] that didn't give us much room to iterate."

He says the dev team, as a result, "realised that FM26 is a game that's good if you're very focused on your own team, and becomes [one with] quite an amount of friction when you're trying to be part of that wider world, starting to browse around." That realisation came "very early on," he says: "We really need to work on this, and the tiles and the remit of the tiles, I think is the really important significant change there… I think on paper the notion of a tile as being both navigation and information is really quite powerful. But what I think we did was, we relied on it for navigation, rather than it being a kind of secondary purpose for it. It became their primary purpose in a lot of places, and what that means then is, you're constructing screens in a way that imbalances the information to some extent."

The improvements are immediately evident. The navigation bar is more sensibly organised, the "portal" now more closely resembles your old inbox, with a bit of colour-coding thrown in. In the simplest terms, I found myself running down far fewer navigational cul-de-sacs than with FM26.

There are still snags, of course. Personally, I think the entire cards-and-tiles system should be left behind now, rather than just most of it. There are just very few circumstances under which a pop-up window is more useful or efficient than navigating to a full page. Boicherot's view here is that certain systems, such as messages from in-game people, suit the pop-up windows better - he may be right, though for me the two are essentially competing UIs overlaid on one another. The classic FM experience - not a spreadsheet, as people like to call it, but really a kind of web browser, where you can seamlessly move from your email inbox-cum-to-do-list to a series of interlinked wiki pages via hyperlinks and a clearly defined navigation bar - remains unbeaten. It's a single, intuitive and widely recognised visual language: underlined text takes you to a page on that topic, while dropdown menus and the nested ones within them take you to the most essential hub pages you'll visit most often. The tiles system only muddies the waters.

Thankfully the team, such as Boicherot, still seems to largely agree. "As a player, you don't want to think about the UI," he says. "Every time you watch a show, you don't want to learn how to use a [remote] control again. So with FM, we just wanted to get that familiar feel again." The studio had a "whole dedicated strike team" to resolving the issue, while the wider team, he says, has coupled that rework with the new drive for immersion, and in turn essentially adapted most of the compositions of the game's various screens to focus on "dynamic elements, on player stats, on the latest matches, who's hot, who's not, who's performing, underperforming. We have quite a lot of news stories as well. We wanted to make sure that we had better journalists in-game, reporting on more interesting stories, and basically giving you a better window to the world."

It's all good progress, ultimately, even if I still have my foibles with the card system's leftovers. And those new additions are really the things I want to talk about - and which I can tell Sports Interactive's developers are keen to talk about as well. Don your anoraks, then. It's time to talk about tactical effectiveness.

The system works like this: replacing the old and somewhat ignorable gauge for your team's tactical familiarity, there's now a gauge called tactical effectiveness. That gauge is influenced by four sub-categories: tactical understanding; player ability; player buy-in; and next match info. Those are then influenced by another layer of influence beneath them. Tactical understanding, for instance, depends on a combination of your player's understanding of their in-possession shape, passing style, build up, chance creation, out-of-possession shape, line of engagement, pressing intensity, and the pressing trap. You'll recognise most of those as tactical settings you can tinker with, which we'll come back to shortly. And even that layer, at times, has things that influence it again. Chance creation, for instance, is dictated by the training contribution, match exposure, players' tactical ability (which I understand from Sports Interactive developers to be influenced by their adaptability, teamwork and other attributes), and the tactical complexity of what you're asking them to do.

Sports Interactive says this is a result of direct conversations they've had with their connections in the Premier League and wider parts of the professional game. Fundamentally, Boicherot says, the previous tactical familiarity system was "quite simplistic" in comparison to what real managers were thinking about. "We also looked at Pep Guardiola's approach at City. [It] was very much, spend quite a lot of money on players, and then you don't see them play for quite a while until he knows that they're ready. And when he puts them on the pitch, they are effective. They just understand how to implement his vision directly on the pitch, and that was something that we felt we couldn't capture with the old system."

All of this is presented in a slightly underwhelming screen of several plain columns with a list of the above factors on them, which is a bit of a shame, but the impact it already had on my thinking in just a few hours with the game was huge. This is, ultimately, a role-playing mechanic, acting as a kind of bridge over the gap between how you think about tactics in Football Manager, and how a real manager might think about them in the real world. The biggest impact happens in how you think about the game - which, in a way, is where a lot of the playing of Football Manager really happens.

Beyond Boicherot's case of Pep's new players, an example given in a brief introduction was a good one: the "new manager bounce" - the notion that a team, after a previous, underperforming manager gets the sack, suddenly has a short-term improvement in results. Technically this is a bit of a myth; the data apparently suggests changing gaffers doesn't actually lead to a noticeable short-term uptick in results on average. But when it does happen, the theory is that these new managers tend to go "back to basics" with their tactics, simplifying instructions for bogged-down, low-morale players and letting them play their game with a bit more freedom.

Take Michael Carrick, for instance, from my trusty old management basket case Manchester United. Previous manager Ruben Amorim's system was complex and demanding, requiring both immense fitness and a large amount of tactical understanding and adherence from his players. Michael Carrick, then an interim, came in and seemingly reverted the team to something far simpler, and the short term impact was an immediate uptick in results. He put Bruno Fernandes back in the number 10 role, reverted to a familiar 4-2-3-1 formation, gave a relatively large amount of creative freedom to more creative players, and set them up in more of a fluid counterattacking mid-block as opposed to Amorim's structured, finely-tuned high press. And initially they did well! And now this season, after opponents had all summer to work through their opposition analysis and realise the tactical plan was actually quite simple, they've been "found out", as the football cliché goes - with results flipping into disaster territory.

FM27's tactical effectiveness system is designed to do something similar (although perhaps not quite as drastically as the Carrick disaster). Simple tactics, such as going long to a target forward, staying compact and hitting teams on the counter, are more easily grasped and implemented by players, leading to better short-term results. Complex pressing, inverting fullbacks, and intricate tiki-taka football meanwhile will likely take far longer to master, potentially leading to early-season struggles as the team learns your fancy, armchair-tactico system. And so in comes the role-playing: do I gamble on tactical purism for a higher long-term ceiling? Or do I secure my short-term future and attempt to gradually transform the team over time? Will fans accept my route one football, even if it gets me immediate results? Will players feel comfortable playing it?

This is where other, interlinking systems come in, and now my anorak is positively bristling. Training has also had quite a major rework, for instance. Training sessions have new categories: tactical understanding, match preparation, footballing development, and physical and recovery. Many of the sessions are familiar, but their recategorisation is important. Previously, we're told, sessions were primarily seen as a means of improving players' attributes - "Defending: Engaged" improved things like tackling and heading, for instance - while a few others helped with tactical familiarity or match fitness, without a clear gauge of their effectiveness.

Now, all of the sessions that come under "tactical understanding" will, you guessed it, help with the "tactical understanding" aspect of overall tactical effectiveness. And knowing that the gauge for, say, your team's understanding of your preferred passing style is in the orange will immediately indicate to you that you need to schedule a few more "Passing Style" training sessions. That then needs balancing with developmental sessions, match preparation sessions such as set pieces - now broken down into individual ones such as corners, free kicks, and so on - and recovery. It's another aspect influenced by actual conversations with those in the real game. Farley highlights the modern emphasis on "tactical periodisation," the approach of making training sessions resemble matches in various ways, down the certain "game state" such as being 1-0 down, consolidating a lead, and so on.

"It's going back a few years now," Farley continues, offering one example of how the team learned from the real game, "but we were very fortunate - I won't name the manager - we had a manager do a 'foot talk' for us, where they were talking about that tactical periodisation, and that kind of, almost playbook, American football-style of: now execute this plan. And that being something they would communicate from the touchline. And it was during covid, behind closed doors games, right? So then when I next watched this team play and you have the benefit of there being no crowd noise and everything, you really actually see it in action with that insight."

Beyond that, there's now an impact on your squad-building, too. Recruiting effectively now means considering your scout's take on a player's tactical suitability - are they likely to fit a high-pressing, high-technicality style such as Guardiola-like tiki-taka? Would James Tarkowski suit a high defensive line? Does Rayan Cherki want to play park-the-bus football? Bruno Fernandes, clearly sensing the importance of the topic with FM27's impending release, made his thoughts quite clear on what he thinks about not pressing opponents after Manchester United's humbling defeat to Como just the other week. This is the tactical effectiveness system personified.

"Take Thierry Henry moving from Arsenal to Barcelona," Boicherot suggests. "There was a big, big adoption time, right? But others are a bit more efficient with different types of player roles. It depends on their attributes, their stats, on their adaptability." It's essentially a melding of soft skill man-management with the modern, analytic side of the game - the type of blend that makes the very best coaches in the world so effective.

If you hadn't guessed, I'm a fan. It's a wonderful way to bring the type of gap-filling I'd already been doing with my imagined narratives into the reality of Football Manager itself. Beyond it, there are more tweaks that get the nod, too. Touchline shouts return, having been patched in apologetically to FM26, but now they're accompanied by a nifty feature that runs in tandem called Tweaks. These are basically one-touch tactical shortcuts you can instigate without leaving the in-match screen, shouts-style, to get your team to push up, or step off the press, or, much to my enjoyment, send the goalkeeper forward for set pieces. Notably Sports Interactive opted to make these tweaks stay active after you make them, until you consciously turn them back off again, as opposed to operating on a timer. The theory there being that the decision to turn them off is often as interesting as the decision to implement them in the first place.

"We wanted to capture a bit more of a 'duel' type of experience against the opposing team manager," Boicherot explains. "How do you want to disrupt your opposition in a quick way, really trying to capture the kind of quick instructions you tell your captain on the sideline and let them go and implement."

International management also gets a welcome bit of attention, with training now properly implemented for that, as does the 2D engine, which has had a lovely visual revamp that still maintains a pleasingly retro tinge. There are reworks to how players' running speed works, which interlinks with fatigue and even the types of runs they might attempt. Tied to that is a new instruction for how a forward should attack the box, all this together theoretically giving slower forwards greater influence on the game. And the onboarding experience has been tinkered with once again, with more manager customisation options and tweaks to how your traits and attributes are set up.

The headline changes, though, are where FM27 is at its best so far. It is easier to play and navigate, "faster to the fun," as well-trained UX developers often like to say, and has genuine potential to be the most tactically interesting and nuanced game in the series to date. Shuffle away the last of those remaining cards, and this could be a seriously good year. Thank goodness for that.

#10626. GTA 6 fan who went viral for hoping to live long enough to play the game has died

A 25-year-old GTA 6 fan who captured the attention of the gaming community after sharing his wish to live long enough to play Rockstar Games’ upcoming title has died.

The fan had been battling interstitial lung disease since he was 17 years old, with his condition eventually deteriorating to the point that he needed a lung transplant.

His family confirmed that he died on October 29, 2026, following a series of infections and worsening health complications, just months after supporters rallied around his story and raised thousands of dollars to help with his medical treatment.

In a TikTok update shared on November 2, a family member revealed that he had developed a severe infection several weeks before his death, causing his lungs to fill with blood.

Doctors reportedly informed the family that he was unlikely to survive and presented hospice care as an option. However, he initially chose to continue treatment, remaining on life support equipment that included ECMO, a machine used to support heart and lung function.

His condition continued to deteriorate, and on October 28, he told his family that he was ready to stop fighting.

The following morning, his loved ones received a call informing them that another infection had developed overnight. His blood work had worsened, and his organs were beginning to shut down.

Family members returned to his bedside, where they found that he was no longer responsive as he had been the previous day.

“He was peaceful. He was at peace. He was peaceful. He was so peaceful,” the family member said. “And he’s no longer fighting. He’s no longer hurting. He’s no longer struggling.”

#10625. Top 5 3D Animation Software in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Choosing the best 3D animation software depends on your experience, budget, and creative goals. Some tools offer complete 3D modeling and animation workflows, while others specialize in professional character animation, visual effects, or AI-powered video creation. This guide compares five options and notes how each fits into web and game projects, so designers and developers can see which one suits their pipeline.

Blender is one of the strongest free options for anyone looking for comprehensive 3D animation software. This open-source application combines modeling, sculpting, rigging, animation, rendering, simulation, and compositing in one package.

Blender provides a broad production workflow without requiring users to pay for a standard commercial license. Its active community, learning resources, and add-on ecosystem make it accessible to independent creators. It is suitable for everything from simple animated scenes to complex short films and game assets.

For web and game developers: Blender ships with a glTF 2.0 importer and exporter, and glTF is the format libraries such as three.js load for 3D on the web. See the Blender manual’s glTF page for export options.

Its extensive interface and feature set can feel overwhelming to new users. Learning advanced rigging, lighting, materials, and rendering takes time, and some established studios may have specialized pipelines built around other applications.

Beginners, freelancers, indie game developers, students, and professionals who need a powerful 3D animation tool without a software subscription.

Autodesk Maya is a professional 3D application widely associated with character animation, rigging, film production, and game development. It offers detailed control over character movement and complex animation workflows, making it a strong choice for artists working in established production environments.

Maya provides sophisticated tools for animating characters and managing complex scenes. Its established role in many professional workflows makes it a valuable skill for artists pursuing careers in film, television, and game development. It also offers detailed control over animation timing, character motion, and rig behavior.

Maya generally requires a paid subscription, which can be expensive for hobbyists and independent creators. Its advanced interface also takes time to learn, particularly for users who have never worked with professional 3D software.

Professional animators, film and television artists, game studios, and users who need advanced character animation and rigging capabilities.

Cinema 4D is a 3D modeling, animation, and rendering application known for its motion graphics workflow. It is widely used for commercial visuals, broadcast graphics, product animations, and creative work that combines 3D elements with graphic design.

Cinema 4D is a strong option for designers who want to create polished motion graphics and branded animation. Its MoGraph workflow can make repetitive or complex movements easier to manage, and its integration with common design workflows is helpful for agencies and creative teams.

For web and product designers: Cinema 4D includes a glTF exporter, which helps when a designer needs to hand a 3D model to a developer for the web. Maxon documents the options on its glTF export settings page.

Cinema 4D is a commercial product, so ongoing licensing costs may be a concern. Although it can handle a wide range of 3D work, users focused on highly specialized character animation or procedural visual effects may prefer tools designed around those workflows.

Motion designers, advertising agencies, broadcast artists, product visualization specialists, and creators who regularly produce animated graphics.

Houdini, developed by SideFX, is a 3D software package known for procedural workflows and advanced visual effects. Rather than relying entirely on manually created steps, artists can build systems that generate and modify complex scenes, simulations, and effects.

Houdini offers exceptional flexibility for technical artists and visual-effects specialists. Procedural workflows make it possible to create reusable systems and adjust complex effects efficiently. Its capabilities are particularly valuable when a project needs detailed simulations or large numbers of interconnected elements.

For game developers: SideFX offers Houdini Engine plugins for Unity and Unreal Engine, which let procedural Houdini assets be used inside those engines. SideFX explains the plugins in its Houdini Engine FAQ.

Houdini has a steep learning curve, especially for beginners who are unfamiliar with node-based workflows. Building effective procedural systems requires patience and technical understanding. While free learning options are available, some commercial uses and advanced workflows require an appropriate paid license.

Visual-effects artists, technical directors, simulation specialists, and experienced 3D creators working on demanding film, television, or game projects.

MagicLight.AI is different from the four tools above. It is an AI-powered video creation platform rather than a traditional 3D application, so it produces finished animated videos, not editable 3D scenes, rigs, or game assets. It is included here for readers who want animated storytelling without building a 3D pipeline. It supports AI-generated characters, scene creation, storyboarding, and voice features.

MagicLight.AI can reduce the time and technical effort needed to create animated videos. It is particularly useful for content creators, educators, marketers, and storytellers who want to focus on the script and final message rather than mastering a complex 3D production pipeline.

MagicLight.AI is not a replacement for dedicated 3D software when you need precise control over mesh topology, character rigs, keyframes, physics, or custom 3D scenes. Creators who need editable 3D assets for games or studio production will need a conventional 3D application such as the ones above.

Content creators, educators, and marketers who want to produce animated videos efficiently without learning a traditional 3D animation workflow.

The right software is the one that supports your actual production needs. Before choosing a tool, consider the following factors.

If you want to model objects, build character rigs, and control animation precisely, start with a dedicated 3D application such as Blender or Maya. If your goal is to create animated storytelling videos from scripts or ideas with less manual production work, an AI video tool such as MagicLight.AI may be a more convenient option.

Blender is an excellent starting point if you need a complete 3D toolset without a standard license fee. Houdini also has a free Apprentice edition with restrictions. Maya and Cinema 4D are commercial applications, so compare their current plans against your expected usage before committing.

Different applications suit different workflows. Blender is versatile, Maya excels at character animation, Cinema 4D is well suited to motion graphics, and Houdini is especially powerful for procedural effects. MagicLight.AI is better suited to AI-assisted video production than to detailed, manually controlled 3D scene creation.

Beginners may find Blender’s extensive learning resources helpful, although its broad feature set takes time to master. Maya and Houdini demand more specialized skills for advanced work, while Cinema 4D is often considered by motion designers seeking a more direct design-oriented workflow. AI video tools can reduce the amount of technical setup required, but still require creative direction and review.

Think about where the finished animation will be used. A web project may need a lightweight glTF model. A game project may require exportable 3D assets, rig compatibility, and a game-engine workflow. A commercial motion graphic may depend on rendering and compositing integration. A social video or animated explainer may prioritize turnaround time, narration, subtitles, and scene continuity.

The best 3D animation software in 2026 depends on your budget, experience, and intended output. Blender is the strongest starting point for many users who want a comprehensive free 3D workflow. Autodesk Maya is a compelling option for professional character animation, Cinema 4D suits motion graphics and commercial visuals, and Houdini stands out for procedural effects and complex simulations. MagicLight.AI offers a different approach: it helps creators generate animated videos with AI rather than manually building a traditional 3D production pipeline.

Blender is one of the best free options because it includes modeling, rigging, animation, rendering, and other production tools in a single open-source application. It is suitable for beginners as well as experienced artists.

Yes. Free software can support professional-quality work when used effectively. Blender, for example, provides a comprehensive toolset for animation and rendering. The final result depends on the artist’s skills, project requirements, hardware, and production workflow.

Blender is a strong choice for beginners who want to learn traditional 3D modeling and animation without paying for a standard software license. Those who mainly want to create animated videos from stories or scripts may prefer exploring an AI video creation platform such as MagicLight.AI.

Blender and Cinema 4D can both export glTF, a format widely used for 3D on the web. Blender is free, so it is the easiest place to start.

#10623. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10622. Mouthwashing is finally on consoles and Xbox Game Pass, and it's a Halloween must for horror fans

A mutilated man stares at you, his single piercing eye following as you move around the room. His filthy bandages hide a ruined face, raw lips circling disturbingly perfect teeth, and his moans echo through the halls. But that eye: it knows something you don't. At least not yet.

Developed by Wrong Organ, Mouthwashing rightly earned a lot of praise when it was released in 2024; it's an extraordinary first-person horror adventure game placing you among the five-strong crew of the Tulpar, a commercial space freighter part-way through its year-long mission to carry its unknown cargo across the stars. Moments into its dramatic opening scene - "0 days before the crash", we're told - things go dramatically awry, establishing a dire tone and a baseline of dread that only builds from there.

Mouthwashing isn't a game laden with jumpscares; instead it is a masterfully crafted discomfort simulator, conjuring unease through its claustrophobic environmental design, strong audio work, disorientating structure and pacing, and a lo-fi visual style that creates spaces for your imagination to fill.

Slowly, for instance, the Tulpar's already gloomy corridors constrict, shrunk by the crew's increasingly desperate use of repair foam; this continuous degradation of the space around you mirroring the minds of its characters, as circumstances grow increasingly dire. For as enjoyable as Mouthwashing's light puzzling is, you're really here for its unexpectedly impactful story.

This is an intelligent game that trusts its audience, and that's most obvious in its breathless, non-chronological narrative. It's a story that doesn't stay still, that offers no moment to breathe. Scenes transition into each other with a digital datamoshing effect, almost as if you're watching system files recovered long after the fact. And each transition often signals a different, disorientating kind of switch, as it's not always clear when - or even who - you are now.

I don't want to spoil much, but there's a surprising amount of subtlety to the way characters are portrayed and gradually evolve. One crew member, for instance, sleeps near a statue of the shipping company's cartoon mascot, which emits voice lines whenever someone draws near. The reason why isn't spelled out to you, but on a second playthrough, this small act of self-preservation becomes clear. And Mouthwashing only grows more rewarding with each subsequent return as you start to notice this attention to detail more and more.

Speaking of characters, one is particularly deplorable; a truly detestable creature up there with Shepherd from the original Modern Warfare trilogy and perhaps even Eric Sparrow if you're a Tony Hawk's Underground fan. Their narcissist journey, culminating in a spiral of delusion that really made my skin crawl, exemplifies what makes Mouthwashing work so well.

Its horror stems from the psychological disintegration of the Tulpar's crew, and the way the resulting violence unfolds. Violence is sparse and unglorified here, but you're often an active participant, much of the nastiest actions occurring when you're in direct control. It absolutely made me wince a few times.

And then comes the closer; one of the most moving monologues I've heard in a video game. It's a final message - not only to a member of the crew, but to you the player - on taking responsibility. By this point, what's done is done, leaving one - perhaps futile - final decision to make. One last chance for a kind of salvation. For me, it's a message that hit home.

Mouthwashing is powerful stuff, then; a different sort of horror this Halloween. It's an easy recommendation, and has been wowing PC players for a while. As of today, though, it's finally available for consoles - PS4, PS5, Switch, and Xbox Series X/S to be exact - and is out on Game Pass too. Additionally, there's currently 40 percent off on Steam.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#10621. Tech multimillionaire and family identified among victims of Florida private jet crash

Tech multimillionaire Adam Robinson, his wife, and their two young children have been identified among the nine people who died in a devastating private jet crash in Florida.

The Learjet 60 was traveling from Austin, Texas, to the Bahamas on Sunday, November 1, when it suddenly plummeted from the sky and crashed into a residential area in Palm Beach County at approximately 12:50 p.m.

The aircraft exploded on impact, leaving a massive crater in the ground and scattering debris across the surrounding neighborhood. All nine people on board died.

According to TMZ, 44-year-old tech entrepreneur Adam Robinson was traveling with his wife, Helen Sharp, their two children, a nanny, and two close friends.

Robinson was the founder of Retention.com and RB2B, two marketing technology companies specializing in customer identification and sales leads. Before entering the tech industry, he worked in finance at Lehman Brothers and Barclays Capital.

The entrepreneur previously built email marketing company Robly, which he reportedly sold in an eight-figure deal.

As People reported, the couple’s children were identified as four-year-old Emma and two-year-old Charlie.

Their nanny, Anita Maria Apellido Cetina, and family friends Rachael Reed and Charmi Fehr were also among those killed.

In a statement, relatives described Emma and Charlie as “the light of their lives.”

The aircraft’s two pilots were identified as 55-year-old Jeff Gregory and 20-year-old Nathalia Gadelha Filgueiras, who reportedly earned her pilot’s license at just 17 years old.

Moments before the crash, air traffic control recordings captured the crew reporting ice accumulation and moderate turbulence while requesting permission to climb to a higher altitude.

The crew’s final radio transmissions included a warning that they were going to crash before the words, “We’re out of time.”

The National Transportation Safety Board has launched an investigation into the crash. Officials say there is evidence the aircraft may have begun breaking apart before impact, although the exact cause remains unknown.

The tragedy comes just months after another prominent Austin entrepreneur, Capital Factory CEO Joshua Baer, died in a separate private jet crash in June 2026.
